# Changelog

All notable changes to the "Isocenter" project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Changed

- **CLAUDE.md now says how to run one mutation by hand, and a test keeps the recipe true (#311).** New subsection at the end of the Commands section, plus `tests/test_mutation_runbook.py`. No library code changes.

  `python -m scripts.mutation_probe` was documented; running a single mutation by hand was not, and the obvious way to do it is wrong in two ways the probe is careful about. Both have produced a wrong verdict in this project before, and a wrong verdict is the expensive kind -- it retires a test that was working, or keeps one that was not.

  **Trap one: stale bytecode.** A `.pyc` from the previous run can be what CPython actually executes, so the verdict describes a mutation that was never in the code under test. That is #174, and the probe's protection against it is real and specific: `scripts/mutation_probe.py:388` puts `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` in the environment of every pytest it launches, and `assert_fresh` re-checks each write against CPython's own cache-validation rule before the tests see it. The variable rather than `-B`, because `run_parallel()` spawns workers and an interpreter flag reaches a spawned child only through `_args_from_interpreter_flags`. A hand-run inherits none of it.

  **Trap two: the wrong copy of the package.** Work here happens in git worktrees and `isocenter` is installed editable from the main checkout, so a plain `python` inside a worktree can import the main checkout: the measurement is real, it is just about a tree you did not edit. Measured with the confound removed -- a bare run resolved `isocenter.__file__` to the main checkout, the same run under `PYTHONPATH=$W` resolved it to the worktree.

  **The mechanism is worth the sentence it costs, because it decides whether the advice works at all.** This venv's editable install is the meta-path-finder flavour, and `install()` **appends** `_EditableFinder` to `sys.meta_path` -- after the stdlib path finder. A `PYTHONPATH` entry is therefore consulted first and wins. Had the finder been prepended, neither `PYTHONPATH` nor `sys.path.insert(0, ...)` would help, and nothing about the outside of the recipe would look different. The runbook line that survives that distinction is the one it now gives: **print `isocenter.__file__` and read it before trusting the measurement.**

  **#311 says "prose only; nothing to test"; this ships three tests, and the disagreement is the point of the milestone.** A runbook is a claim about how to measure, and an unchecked one goes quietly false. The subsection is located by a stable heading and must name `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE`, `PYTHONPATH` and `isocenter.__file__` in backticks and point at #174. The bytecode advice is checked against `inspect.getsource(mutation_probe.run)` rather than against a remembered string, so a switch to `-B` or to a pre-flight cache wipe turns it red -- the same shape as `tests/test_packaging_contract.py`'s pin of its SQLite busy timeout by name.

  **The third test runs the recipe against the live install rather than against a hermetic fixture, and that is a deliberate choice against the cheaper one.** A self-contained version -- two fake packages, one on `PYTHONPATH` -- would pin `sys.path` ordering, which nobody doubts, and would stay green in exactly the situation that makes the advice false: a *prepended* finder. So the test puts a fake `isocenter` on `PYTHONPATH`, runs a script from an unrelated directory with the venv interpreter, and asserts the fake wins. A control run without `PYTHONPATH` comes first: without it, "the fake won" would also be the answer if `isocenter` were not importable at all, and the test would pass while measuring nothing. If the control cannot import, it skips rather than passes, since a pass would be a claim.

  The subsection also records two smaller things that cost time in this milestone: `python3` on `PATH` is a pyenv shim without the project's dependencies, so the venv interpreter has to be named explicitly; and `pytest -q` buffers, so an interrupted run leaves a log indistinguishable from a hang. Its citation of `scripts/mutation_probe.py` is graded by `tests/test_source_citations.py` (#310), in the `:N` spelling rather than the content-pin grammar, because the cited line is a dict literal and a byte-exact quote of it in prose would be brittle for no gain.

- **A line number this tree cites now has to exist, and a quoted line has to still hold the code (#310).** New file `tests/test_source_citations.py`; one comment deleted in `isocenter/configuration.py`; one CLAUDE.md paragraph reshaped. No behaviour change.

  Citations are load-bearing here. Five tests in `tests/test_remediation_invariants.py` are near-identical and each says which of five near-identical `entity.mark_modified()` calls it defends *by line number* -- naming the arm alone would not distinguish them, and #310 records that naming the arm alone is how CLAUDE.md's count of that cluster drifted to three when there were five. Nothing read any citation. **One was already false**: `isocenter/configuration.py` cited line 523 of `config_manager.py`, a file 272 lines long.

  **That one is deleted, not renumbered.** What it originally pointed at is unrecoverable -- `config_manager.py` has never been near 523 lines in its recorded history -- and a renumbered guess would be a fresh false claim wearing the authority of a citation. The comment now states the fact it was evidence for (`config_manager` accepts both the bare-name and the structured `{"name": ..., "action": ...}` tag shapes, and this method always writes the structured one), which the existing structured-tag tests already carry.

  **Three rules, each stopping somewhere deliberate.** *Rule 1*: a citation naming a file in this repository must be in range, in either the `path.py:N` or the `path.py line N` spelling, with an optional `-M` end. A citation naming a file **not** in the repository is skipped -- the tree cites pydicom's `filereader.py`, `filebase.py` and `filewriter.py`, and this guard has no business grading a line number it cannot see and could not fix. *Rule 2*, which is what #310 actually asks for: `` `<code>` at path.py line N `` must find exactly `<code>` on line N, stripped. In-range alone survives an insertion above line 148 -- all five citations start pointing one line high and nothing notices. *Rule 3*: the **set** of cited `entity.mark_modified()` line numbers must equal the set of lines in `remediation.py` whose stripped text is `entity.mark_modified()`. A sixth call inserted below 247 leaves all five existing citations true and only the count wrong, which is the drift that already happened once.

  **Rule 3 is set equality on the numbers, not a count of citations.** There are two citations per line (a test docstring and CLAUDE.md), so a count comparison would be red forever; and nothing hardcodes five, so the requirement moves when the code does.

  **Because skipping is part of the design, the guard asserts it graded something.** A resolver that silently stopped matching would skip every citation and report a clean tree -- the "a silent skip reads as a pass" failure already written down twice here (#162, `tests/test_doc_anchors.py`'s fourth deferral). Rule 1 requires at least ten citations actually resolved and range-checked (23 are today, on a clean checkout), and Rule 2 at least five. Verified in both directions: inserting one blank line high in `remediation.py` turns Rules 2 and 3 red together and leaves Rule 1 green, which is exactly the failure Rule 1 cannot see.

  **Rule 2's grammar is narrow on purpose, and the boundary was checked against prose that already exists.** `tests/test_redaction_attestation.py` writes `` `scan_burned_in_annotations` (`services.py:211`) `` and ``at `services.py:207` `` -- the `:N` spelling, no "line", and the backticked span names a *symbol* rather than a line of code. Those get Rule 1 only. A guard that went red on correct writing would be deleted rather than fixed.

  **`CHANGELOG.md` and `docs/superpowers/` are excluded from the sweep**, and the reason is a rule of this project rather than convenience: both are dated records that are never silently rewritten to match today's code. A stale citation in either is a fact about history, and the only way to make such a failure green would be to falsify the record. `build/` and `dist/` are excluded as build artefacts, or every defect would be reported twice.

  **CLAUDE.md's dirty-tracking paragraph is reshaped, not thinned.** It listed the five lines as a bare comma-separated run of numbers, which no rule can read. It now spells each one in Rule 2's grammar -- laboriously, and the paragraph says why. This is "make the citation checkable", not "stop citing line numbers": the numbers are what let each of five near-identical tests say which call it defends, and removing them would re-open the drift #310 is about.

  **Stated deferral, recorded in the module docstring rather than left to be discovered.** A bare `line <N>` citation naming no file is not checked. `tests/test_remediation_dates.py:86` is one -- "a shape worse than the one at line 392", with no file named. Which file was meant could not be determined from the test, the module under test, or `git log -S`, so the text is left exactly as written rather than repaired by guess.

  No `scripts/mutation_probe.py` `TARGETS` entry, and #310 suggests the opposite, so the reason is written down: a pure text check homed in a `TARGETS`-covered file is re-run against every mutant of that module for zero kill signal.

- **A documented example that shows its output is now executed, and a doctest fence no longer falls out of the name check (#304).** New file `tests/test_documented_output_matches.py`, plus a fix inside `tests/test_documented_api_exists.py`. No library code changes.

  #234 gave the docs a name check: every method a fence *names* must exist. Nothing checked that a fence which prints something prints what the page says it prints, and the two claims that motivated this are exactly the shape a name check cannot see -- README's deleted zone-discovery section stated `to_dataframe()`'s column list and `get_density_matrix()`'s return shape, and both are properties of the values, not of the names.

  **Opt-in, by an HTML comment on the line above the fence** (`<!-- runnable: none -->`, or a fixture name in place of `none`). Most fences in this tree start from a live `Session` over a real dataset; running them would need disk, fixtures and in some cases OCR, and a doc test that is slow or flaky gets deleted. Opting in keeps the check free and keeps its blind spot stated rather than discovered. Comparison is stdlib `doctest` with `NORMALIZE_WHITESPACE | ELLIPSIS` -- no bespoke comparator, for the reason `tests/test_doc_anchors.py` gives for using the real `markdown` slugger.

  **The marker is a comment and not a fence info string, and that is the load-bearing part.** ```` ```python exec ```` would have been the obvious spelling, and it would have stopped `test_documented_api_exists.py`'s `_PYTHON_FENCE` from matching the fence at all -- so opting a fence *in* to output checking would have silently opted it *out* of name checking.

  **The same interaction exists through a second door, and it was already open.** A `>>>` fence has an ordinary ```` ```python ```` info string, so `_PYTHON_FENCE` matches it, but `ast.parse` raises `SyntaxError` on the prompts and the guard's `except SyntaxError: continue` swallowed the whole fence -- every name in it. Any fence rewritten as a doctest would have dropped out of #234's guard with the guard still reporting a clean pass. It is fixed in the same change: `_fence_source()` runs a fence through `doctest.DocTestParser().get_examples()` first and, when it has examples, parses the concatenation of their `.source`. `Example.source` already strips prompts and joins continuation lines, which is why this is two lines rather than a prompt stripper of our own. Pinned by `test_a_doctest_fence_is_still_read_for_method_names`, red before the fix on a `tmp_path` doc whose doctest fence calls `session.no_such_method()`.

  **Four rules, each with a red-today test, because every one of them has a silent-skip answer that would have been cheaper.** A marked fence's output must match (`test_the_check_would_fail_on_a_wrong_expected_output`, a two-fence document where exactly the wrong one is reported, by line -- the shape of `test_doc_anchors.py`'s `test_the_check_would_fail_on_a_broken_anchor`). A marker naming an unknown fixture is an **error**, not a skip: otherwise a fence wears the badge of coverage and has none. A marked fence with **zero** `>>>` examples is an error, which is the most likely way this check quietly stops working -- someone marks an ordinary fence, sees green, and believes its output is pinned. And a marker separated from its fence by a blank line claims nothing, so an HTML comment written as prose cannot adopt whatever fence comes next. The real walk also asserts `marked >= 1` on #299's precedent: a check that grades nothing reports the same "no failures" as one that grades everything.

  **Marked today: README's §5b discovery fence and nothing else**, deliberately. It is the fence whose two claims were wrong before, and its receiver is `result`, which is outside `test_documented_api_exists.py`'s `ROOTS` -- so its calls are checked for output here and were never checked for name resolution there. `docs/ocr.md`'s fences all begin from a live `session` and stay unmarked; that blind spot is written into the new module's docstring rather than left to be found.

  Neither file gets a `scripts/mutation_probe.py` `TARGETS` entry, for the reason `test_documented_api_exists.py`'s docstring already gives: it imports no target module, so a `TARGETS`-covered home would re-run it against every mutant of that module for zero kill signal. `test_documented_api_exists.py`'s stated blind spot also moves from **seven** documented calls on receivers outside `ROOTS` to **twelve**, since §5b added five; understating it would be the same defect that file exists for.

- **README documents zone discovery again, and says what the density matrix is a matrix of (#303).** Documentation plus one guard; no code change. `README.md` jumped from §5a straight to §6, so `session.discover_redaction_zones()` and the whole `DiscoveryResult` surface -- `filter`, `to_dataframe`, `get_density_matrix`, `visualize_heatmap`, `analyze_temporal_stability`, `inspect_clusters`, `to_zones` -- were public API with no entry point in the file most users read first. The new §5b restores it.

  **The reason the section is worth more than a link: `get_density_matrix()` is not an image-space heatmap, and every natural reading of it says otherwise.** It bins each candidate box's *centre*, but normalises by `max(box[0])` and `max(box[1])` across the candidates -- the largest box *origin*, not the image's Rows and Columns. The grid therefore stretches to whatever the scan happened to find: two scans of the same machine are not comparable to each other, none is comparable to the image, and a centre past the largest origin clamps into the last bin. `docs/ocr.md` already feeds it to `plt.imshow` under the title "Burned-in Text Heatmap", which reads as an overlay on the image and is not one. §5b states the limit rather than repeating the implication, and sends readers to `to_zones()` or to each candidate's `box` for coordinates that do mean pixel positions.

  The section also names the `ocr` extra as the dependency of the *scan* (`pixel_analysis.HAS_OCR`) and is explicit that nothing on the returned `DiscoveryResult` needs an extra -- `to_dataframe()` needs only pandas, which is already in `install_requires`. Without `pytesseract` the scan finds no text and returns an empty result rather than raising, which is exactly the failure a reader has to be told about in advance.

  **The section's shape claims are pinned twice.** `tests/test_documented_api_exists.py` gains a guard that the section exists, names the entry point, the result type, `to_zones`, `get_density_matrix` and the `ocr` extra, and states the not-image-space limit; and the section's second fence is a doctest whose output is executed by `tests/test_documented_output_matches.py` (#304). The two claims the deleted §5b got wrong -- `to_dataframe()`'s column list and `get_density_matrix()`'s return shape -- are precisely the two that fence prints.

- **The dirty-tracking paragraph says five, and says when the redundancy holds (#173).** Documentation only: `CLAUDE.md`, plus the `record_phi_status` docstring in `isocenter/entities.py`. No code change anywhere.

  The paragraph said "three `mark_modified()` calls in `remediation.py` are individually redundant and survive deletion untested (#132)". Three of the three claims in that sentence were wrong or incomplete. There are **five** (lines 148, 181, 218, 239, 247, the last two being the `REMOVE_TAG` sequence arm from #167 and the `REMOVE_TAG` Python-attribute arm). They are redundant only on a **first** remediation; after a reload they are load-bearing, and deleting one strips PHI while leaving the entity reporting nothing to save. And they no longer survive deletion untested -- each is now pinned by a test naming its line, so both the "survive deletion untested" clause and the "do not simplify one away" warning retire together.

  **The ruling recorded, so it is not re-opened.** `record_phi_status` short-circuits when the status is unchanged. #173 asks whether that short-circuit should go, since removing it would make all five calls honestly redundant on every path and deletable. It stays, on four measurements. (1) *Provenance*: `git log -S "if self.phi_status is status" -- isocenter/entities.py` returns exactly one commit, `9e8b2d3`, the same one that introduced `record_phi_status` -- it is the original design, not an accident, and the docstring states the reason. (2) *Cost*: over a 206-entity graph, audit → real save → identical audit adds **0** dirty entities as it stands and dirties **all 192 instances** without it. The figure is a delta, not an absolute: 14 entities -- every patient, study and series -- read dirty either way, because `save_all` marks only instances persisted (which is the whole reason #307 has to be instance-scoped). So the graph goes from 14 of 206 dirty to 206 of 206 on a re-scan that found nothing new, on a library whose memory claims are made about 100GB+ datasets and whose `audit()` touches the whole graph by construction. (3) *Interaction*: it would make #297 strictly worse, widening the `save()`-then-`audit()` window from instances whose status changed to every instance scanned. (4) *Coverage*: with the bump made unconditional the suite reports **5 failed, 1255 passed**, and all five are parametrizations of `test_recording_the_status_an_entity_already_carries_changes_nothing` -- the test #132 wrote so that exactly this decision would have to be deliberate. Nothing else in the suite defends the trade, which is an argument for deciding it on measured cost rather than on how the code reads. The rejected alternative is recorded in that test's docstring as well as here: unconditional buys five deletable lines and costs a whole-graph rewrite per re-scan.

  `CLAUDE.md`'s pipeline paragraph also gains a sentence saying that `audit()` and `redact()` both drain the persistence manager on entry, and why.

  **One thing worth knowing before editing that paragraph again: it is not free prose.** `tests/test_claude_md_api_names.py` extracts every backticked identifier in the "Object graph and dirty tracking" section and requires it to resolve on `TrackedEntity` or `PhiStatus`, against an explicit allow-list of the sibling names the section legitimately mentions. A first draft of this rewrite went red on `` `SHIFT_DATE` ``, `` `_scan_study` `` and `` `setattr` `` -- names from `remediation.py` and `privacy.py`, correct in the prose and not part of that surface. The rewrite says them without backticks rather than growing the allow-list, since widening it is how the check stops meaning anything.

- **The five `mark_modified()` calls in `remediation.py` are load-bearing after a reload, and five tests now say which line does what (#173, #132).** No behaviour change; six new tests. #132 recorded that these calls survive deletion untested and deferred the pin, and CLAUDE.md repeated the reasoning as prose. The prose was right about the mechanism and wrong about the scope, and the scope is the part with a PHI consequence.

  **The cluster is five calls, not three or four.** `remediation.py` lines 148 (`REPLACE_TAG`, Python-attribute arm), 181 (`SHIFT_DATE`, `setattr` arm), 218 (`REMOVE_TAG` on `attributes`), 239 (`REMOVE_TAG` on `sequences`, the #167 private-sequence arm) and 247 (`REMOVE_TAG`, Python-attribute arm). Line 181 is not a corner: `PhiInspector._scan_study` raises `SHIFT_DATE` against `study_date` on a `Study`, and `Study` has no `set_attr`, so every flagged study date in the library goes through that line.

  **On a first remediation each call is redundant. After a reload each is the only thing left, and deleting one strips the PHI while leaving the entity claiming it has nothing to save.** The redundancy comes from `record_phi_status(REMEDIATED)` on the shared success path, which also advances `_revision`. But `record_phi_status` short-circuits when the status is unchanged, and the guard reads the `phi_status` **property**. With the bump present the revision moves first, so the property reads `UNSCANNED`, the guard misses, and the status is recorded. Delete the bump and the revision does not move, the property still reads `REMEDIATED` from the load, the guard fires, and nothing advances. A hydrated entity carries `REMEDIATED` (`SqliteStore.load_all` records the stored conclusion and then marks the subtree persisted), so this is the state every second remediation starts from. Measured on all five: clean code leaves the entity with unsaved changes, each mutant leaves `has_unsaved_changes` false with the PHI already stripped from memory -- the next save skips it and the identifier stays in the database.

  **Five tests in `tests/test_remediation_invariants.py`, one per line, each naming its line and its arm.** That file rather than `tests/test_entity_state_vocabulary.py`, deliberately: it is already in `scripts/mutation_probe.py`'s `TARGETS` for `remediation.py`, and a kill test the probe does not run for that module would leave the probe reporting all five as survivors after they were dead. Each test reaches the post-reload state the way hydration does -- apply content, `record_phi_status(REMEDIATED)`, `mark_persisted()` -- and asserts that precondition explicitly. It is **not** bootstrapped by remediating through a different arm first, which would make each test kill two lines and pin neither. Verified by hand, one mutation at a time: replacing the `entity.mark_modified()` at 148, 181, 218, 239 and 247 with `pass` turns **exactly one** test red each and leaves the other four green, with the assertion message naming the outcome rather than the counter ("the entity reports no unsaved changes after its PHI was stripped, so the next save skips it and the value stays in the database"). The one-to-one mapping is the whole deliverable, so it was checked in both directions.

  **A sixth test in `tests/test_persistence.py` runs the round trip #173 established by reading.** It remediates, `save_all`s, `load_all`s, and asserts the hydrated instance reads `REMEDIATED` with nothing unsaved before remediating a second tag. It kills the same mutant as the line-218 test; its distinct value is that the precondition is produced by a real save and load rather than by `mark_persisted()` standing in for one, so a change to how hydration records a stored status shows up here.

  **Two ways a hand-applied mutation silently measures other code, both hit during this work; the first is #174 and is already documented at length in `scripts/mutation_probe.py`.** (1) Replacing `entity.mark_modified()` with `pass` yields a byte-identical file size at four of the five lines, and CPython's default `.pyc` invalidation is source mtime-plus-size at one-second resolution -- so a fast loop of mutations tests the *previous* mutation's bytecode. The first run of this matrix produced a lagged pairing that still showed exactly one red per mutation, which is what makes it dangerous: the wrong matrix looks like a clean result. The probe sets `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` in `run()` for this reason; a hand-run needs the same or a cleared `__pycache__`. (2) Running a measurement script from a git worktree resolves `isocenter` through the editable install to the **main checkout**, so the mutation is not in the code under measurement at all; `PYTHONPATH=<worktree>` or `sys.path.insert` fixes it. Both belong to the same class -- the measurement quietly ran against code other than the code you edited -- which is now this project's standard form of evidence, so both are filed as **#311** with a proposed CLAUDE.md note.

  `TARGETS` needed no edit: both files are already listed for `remediation.py`, in `scripts/mutation_probe.py` and in CLAUDE.md's table.

  The five test docstrings and CLAUDE.md now carry ten line-number claims that one inserted line in `remediation.py` would falsify silently, since the tests exercise the arms rather than reading the numbers. A guard asserting those five lines still hold `entity.mark_modified()` is filed as **#310** rather than added here.

- **Five operator-facing lines in `io_handlers.py` are best-effort, and the module now says which channel carries each fact instead (#284).** No behaviour change, and deliberately **no test of any of the five lines**. The mutation probe reports five log/print sites in this module as `SURVIVED`; the deliverable is the ruling that settles all five at once, written into the module docstring where the next reader of those lines will find it. One assertion is added to an existing test, and it is not a test of a log line -- it closes the one justification below that was pinned on only one of its two branches.

  **The ruling, stated once.** A log line is contract exactly when it is the only channel that carries its fact to somebody who can act on it. Where a return value, an audit row, a report section or a raised exception carries the same fact, the log line is a rendering of that fact for convenience, and the suite does not pin it.

  **This is not a fresh ruling -- it is the rule the suite already follows in the large, which is what keeps a mixed answer from being taste; the three places it does not are named below.** Counted rather than asserted: 139 `caplog`/`capsys` occurrences across 25 test files, no `caplog.set_level` anywhere, and **30** `caplog.at_level` gates of which 27 set `logging.WARNING`. (An earlier draft of this entry said 27 gates, 24 of them WARNING. The census matched `logging.[A-Z]` and so walked straight past three gates written in the string form `at_level("WARNING")` -- which matters below, because two of them are the counterexamples. The three non-WARNING gates are fine under the ruling: `release_memory` returns nothing and writes no audit row, a zone that fails to apply raises without saying which zone on what array, and `remediation.py`'s failure arm only logs.) Three tests state the rule in their own words. `tests/test_export_loss_audit.py` asserts a warning **because** `write_tree` can never supply a store handle -- "if the warning rode the audit entry it would vanish for every fixture generator in `scripts/`" -- so it is asserted precisely where no other channel exists. `tests/test_legacy_waveform_hydration.py` names the redundant half of its pair out loud ("the store's own audit log already records the loss; the warning is about the heal, not the loss") and asserts only the non-redundant half. `tests/test_redact_error.py` asserts `pytest.raises` first and the log line second, and its docstring records that it used to assert the log *instead of* the exception -- that was #48, and the caplog line is residue of the fix.

  **All five sites come out best-effort, and not because that is the cheap answer: in every one the alternate channel is not merely present but already pinned by a test.** That is also what guards the ruling, and it is the answer to the question this milestone asks of it: the justifications are prose and no test reads prose, but every alternate channel named below is itself carried by an assertion, so deleting one turns a test red rather than quietly evaporating the reason a log line was left unpinned. The justification cannot rot without something going red first. The "Skipping N already imported files" line is carried by `IngestSummary.skipped`, set from the same count on both of the paths that return it and now asserted on both in `tests/test_ingest_failure_audit.py` -- the second assertion is added by this change, because until now only the early return at `io_handlers.py:961` was pinned while the log line fires on both paths, so the weakest of the five justifications held on the branch where no work happens and nowhere else. This line was also genuinely single-channel when #284 was written; **#211 is what changed it**, which is why the issue's reading of it was right at the time and is not now. The superseded-source warning and its "suppressing further" throttle are carried by a per-file `WARNING` audit row written unconditionally *outside* the throttle, whose own comment calls it the compliance trail, plus section 4 of the report -- both asserted in `tests/test_reingest_after_redact.py` -- and `IngestSummary.declined`, which is a channel but is itself asserted nowhere and so does no work in this argument. The scan-gap warning is carried by the `SCAN_GAP` row written two lines below it and surfaced through `get_audit_scan_gaps`, `ComplianceReport.scan_gaps` and section 3.2 with the tag named -- pinned heavily, in `tests/test_data_loss_reporting.py` and `tests/test_private_sequence_implicit_vr.py`. The worker's `ERROR: Export failed` line on stderr is carried by the `ExportOutcome` returned on the very next line, which becomes `ExportSummary.failures`, an `ERROR` audit row, and a `RuntimeError` naming the first failure on the `write_tree` path (`tests/test_export_failure_audit.py`, `tests/test_worker_loss_is_reported.py`). And the `ERROR`-level line in `_report_export_failures` is carried by the `failures` list that same loop builds and returns, plus an `ERROR` audit row three lines below carrying a byte-identical detail string.

  **The stderr line is a fossil, and its own comment describes the mechanism that replaced it.** `git log -S "ERROR: Export failed for" -- isocenter/io_handlers.py` returns exactly one commit, `3c2c401` (2026-01-21), *"Log export errors to stderr and return None on failure to prevent parallel batch abortion"* -- seven months before `ExportOutcome` arrived in `02b1d48` (#126, 2026-08-27) and `ExportSummary` in `574e0f5` (#181, 2026-08-29). It was written when the worker had no return channel at all. It is kept, not deleted: it is what an operator watching a terminal sees during a long export.

  **Three counterexamples exist in the suite and are named here rather than left for a reader to find -- with how each was missed, because the method is the more useful half.** (1) The ingest throttle's sibling at `services.py:215-219`, the burned-in-annotation error and its "Suppressing further individual errors" line, writes an audit row too, so the ruling makes it best-effort -- yet `tests/test_redaction_robustness.py:70-76` pins both its call *count* and that exact suppression string. It escaped the census because it asserts through a `MagicMock` logger, not `caplog`. (2) `tests/test_redact_reports_outcome.py:161-163` pins the WARNING string `"1 of 3"` under the assertion message *"a partial redaction was not reported anywhere"* -- and it **is** reported elsewhere. The same numbers go into a `REDACTION` audit row written by `services.record_redaction_pass` (`services.py:266-270`), surfaced in report section 2 and pinned byte for byte at `tests/test_redaction_audit_accounting.py:77,152,180,218`; the row for that very run reads `Applied 1 of 3 candidate images with 1 zones`. So the assertion message states the opposite of the truth. (3) `tests/test_redaction_attestation.py:141-143` is half an exception: its `"0 of 1"` assertion is redundant with the same `REDACTION` row, but its second assertion -- that the warning names the reason, *"no configured zone that landed inside the image"* -- is genuinely single-channel and correct under the ruling. Both (2) and (3) escaped the census on the string-form gate described above. So the suite is not uniform, and the honest statement of the evidence is that the ruling is what it follows in the large and that these are the places it does not. All three are left alone: they are green, none is this module, and rewriting a passing test to match a ruling written after it is how a rule stops being evidence and starts being enforcement. Pinning anything in `io_handlers.py` *because* of them would be the same move in reverse. And `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-01-private-sequence-implicit-vr.md`'s T8 row names "emitting only a `logger.warning`" as the anti-pattern and requires the report to name the tag: the spec agreeing with the scan-gap ruling, not contradicting it, so nothing there is falsified and no supersession is owed.

  **Testing none of the five lines is the point, not a shortfall.** The probe's own docstring says a survivor is a question, not a verdict; #291 set the precedent of recording what was deliberately not tested and why. A test that pinned any of these five would pin a *rendering* -- it would go red on a reworded message that lost no information, and green on a deleted audit row that lost all of it. The one assertion this change does add goes on the *channel* instead, which is the shape the ruling asks for. So the module docstring says in as many words that **whenever the probe samples one of these five, `SURVIVED` is the correct result, and that the paragraph is the reason not to re-file #284.** That sentence is the durable half of the work.

  **The wording is conditional because the probe's sample is not stable, and an earlier draft of this entry got it wrong in a way this PR itself caused.** The probe picks mutation sites by INDEX -- `scripts/mutation_probe.py:430` computes `step = max(1, total // budget)` and `:432` walks `range(0, total, step)` -- so removing a site anywhere in a file renumbers every site after it and silently changes which lines are sampled. Reconstructed by running the probe's own `Mut` visitor over both revisions at the module's budget of 30: at `b223f6a` `io_handlers.py` had **380** sites and the sample selected `865`, `970`, `1159`, `2062` and `2687` -- all five of these lines, plus the three #285 sites at `192`, `335` and `2576`, which is why the two issues were filed against exactly those eight. Replacing one `and` with an `isinstance` call in this PR removed a `BoolOp`, took the module to **378**, and the sample now reaches only the first two of the five. Nothing about the other three changed. A claim that all five would keep surviving would therefore have been checkably false the first time anyone ran the probe -- and the general lesson is worth more than the correction: a line that stops appearing in a probe report has not been fixed, and one that starts appearing has not regressed.

- **`_is_routed` loses a default no call site read, the export worker's sidecar handoff names the type it means, and the 0.9.0 claim about `read_sequence`'s stream flags is corrected (#285).** Three small changes in `io_handlers.py`, none of them behaviour-changing. The third is the one worth reading, because what shipped in 0.9.0 was backwards.

  **`_is_routed(tag, is_root, has_pixel_data=False)` becomes `_is_routed(tag, is_root, has_pixel_data)`.** Both call sites pass the argument explicitly and there is no other caller in the package, the tests, or `scripts/`. Deleted rather than deprecated, per the pre-1.0 one-spelling rule -- and the default was not merely unused but wrong to take, since `False` asserts that the instance carries no top-level `(7fe0,0010)`. No test changes: the two call sites already pass three arguments, so the existing suite is the regression check.

  **`hasattr(pl, 'sidecar_path') and hasattr(pl, 'offset')` becomes `isinstance(pl, SidecarPixelLoader)`.** The guard read four attributes and checked two, so it was never the guard it looked like -- an object carrying `sidecar_path` and `offset` but not `length` and `alg` would have passed it and raised inside it. Every `_pixel_loader` the package assigns is a `SidecarPixelLoader` (defined in this same module) or a pass-through of one; the only other values that reach that line anywhere in the repository are two test doubles, `tests/test_redaction_optimization.py`'s bare lambda and `tests/test_redaction_parallel.py`'s `ConstantPixelLoader`, and neither carries any of the four attributes. Verified before the change against the six test files that exercise the redaction and export pixel paths. It also removes a mutation site rather than explaining one: `isinstance` has no boolean operator to flip, so the equivalent mutant the probe kept surfacing stops being reported instead of being re-adjudicated every run.

  **The 0.9.0 entry below says both `DicomIO` flags are "required here -- without them `read_sequence` raises `AttributeError: 'DicomBytesIO' object has no attribute '_tag_packer'`". Measured against pydicom 3.0.2, that is false about the read stream in every particular.** `read_sequence` takes implicitness and endianness as positional arguments and threads them explicitly through `read_sequence_item` and `read_dataset` down to the element generator; it consults neither attribute on the stream. The attribute appears in `filereader.py` only on a transfer-syntax UID and on a raw data element, never on a stream. The one place implicitness could be re-derived, `_is_implicit_vr` at `filereader.py:336`, short-circuits at `:368-369` on `is_sequence` before reading a byte, and `read_sequence` always reaches it with `is_sequence=True` from inside a sequence. Empirically, the gate's own fixture parses identically through a read stream whose `is_implicit_VR` is unset, `True`, or `False`, and consumes exactly `len(raw)` in all three -- and parses just as well with **neither** flag set. The independence is structural, not an artifact of the fixture.

  **`_tag_packer` is created by the *other* setter, and belongs to the *other* stream.** `is_little_endian`'s setter builds the packers and unpackers (`filebase.py:121-133`); `is_implicit_VR`'s setter only type-checks and stores (`filebase.py:147-152`). The `_tag_packer` AttributeError is entirely real -- it is raised by `write_tag` on the **write** stream when `is_little_endian` is unset, which is almost certainly how the original claim came to be written and attributed to the wrong half.

  **The genuinely load-bearing flag is the write stream's `is_implicit_VR`, and it is load-bearing in the way that is easiest to miss: not because it is required.** Omit it and pydicom falls back to the datasets' `original_encoding`, which the ones `read_sequence` just produced carry as `(True, True)`, so the write succeeds. It matters because a **wrong** value is not an error. Explicit VR is a valid encoding: `write_sequence` succeeds, the bytes differ from the original, the byte-equality gate refuses, `_sequence_from_un_bytes` returns `None` for **every** sequence, and #167 comes back reported to users as "this vendor block is unparseable" with no exception raised anywhere. The flag is read on the write path at `filewriter.py:633/636/699/714/722`, in `write_data_element` -- reached from `write_sequence` through `write_sequence_item` and `write_dataset` -- and naming the chain rather than the entry point is the point, since misattributing a mechanism to the wrong function is exactly what the 0.9.0 entry did.

  **Two new tests in `tests/test_pydicom_deprecations.py`, both green on HEAD by construction, and their docstrings say so** so nobody reads them as red-first tests of ours. They are characterization tests of pydicom whose value is going red on a bump: one pins that implicit and explicit VR on the write stream produce different bytes for the same sequence, and that a dataset from `read_sequence` still records its own encoding; the other pins that the read stream's VR mode is not the channel implicitness travels on. **That second test needed a control, and the first version of it had none** -- worth recording, because the reason is not obvious. On the gate's ordinary fixture `read_sequence` returns the same dataset whether it is *told* implicit or explicit, so "flipping the stream attribute changed nothing" was uninformative and the test would have stayed green even if pydicom had started honouring the attribute. The reason is not the `filereader.py:368-369` short-circuit, which needs `implicit_vr_is_assumed=True` to fire: with `False` control falls through to the byte sniffer at `:371-379`, which re-derives implicitness and corrects the caller. The test now runs on a fixture built to defeat that sniffer -- a first element whose implicit-VR length field is `0x5A41`, whose low bytes `41 5A` read as the ASCII VR "AZ", which pydicom's own comment at `:376-379` names as the case its recovery cannot handle -- and asserts the control first: on this fixture the implicitness *argument* does change the parse (an explicit read invents a spurious `(5656,5656)` element). Only then does it hold that argument fixed and vary the stream *attribute*, which changes nothing. Verified red by patching `read_sequence` to prefer the attribute over its argument: the test fails on that assertion, with its own message. Between them the two tests watch the only two pydicom changes that would break #167 silently. The existing gate test keeps both names in `REMOVED_IN_V4`, because the write stream still needs them; its docstring carries the correction rather than dropping the old claim, since a reader who half-remembers the wrong version is the failure mode.

  **The 0.9.0 entry is left standing and corrected here rather than rewritten.** It is a shipped, dated record of what was believed and released, and the project's rule about dated records applies to it as much as to a dated spec. The comment in `_sequence_from_un_bytes` -- the copy of the claim that a future reader is most likely to act on -- is rewritten in place, and says out loud that 0.9.0 said the opposite.

- **Graph entities compare and hash by identity, not by field values (#299).** `Patient`, `Study`, `Series`, `Instance`, `DicomItem` and `TrackedEntity` carried the dataclass default `eq=True`. Two consequences, both wrong for a graph of records: `{instance: 1}` raised `TypeError: unhashable type: 'Instance'`, because `eq=True` sets `__hash__ = None`; and `Series("S1", "CT", 1) == Series("S1", "CT", 1)` was `True` for two entirely distinct series. All six are now `@dataclass(slots=True, eq=False)`, so `a == b` is `a is b`, `a == a` holds, and an entity is usable as a dict key. Nothing in the package relied on the old behaviour -- this is a latent-trap removal rather than a bug fix. `__eq__` and `__hash__` are themselves runtime behaviour and they do change; what does not change is any behaviour anything relied on.

  **The issue's own proposed fix -- `eq=False` on the four leaf classes -- was measured and is strictly worse than leaving the default.** `Instance` inherits `DicomItem` and `Patient`/`Study`/`Series` inherit `TrackedEntity`, and both bases were themselves `eq=True` dataclasses. A subclass with `eq=False` inherits the base's `__hash__ = None` through the MRO, so it stays unhashable, *and* inherits the base's generated `__eq__`, which compares the BASE's fields only -- the leaf's own identifiers drop out of the comparison entirely. Measured on the four-leaf form in this repo: `Series("S1", "CT", 1) == Series("S2", "MR", 9)` is `True`, since `TrackedEntity` holds nothing but revision counters. (`Instance` escapes that particular collapse only by accident -- its `__post_init__` mirrors the UID into `attributes`, which *is* a base field -- so it is not a defence and the docs no longer imply it is.) In an isolated repro the same shape reads `C(1,2) == C(1,3) -> True` where `C(eq=False)` derives from `B(eq=True)`. Six classes, not four; the reason is a comment on `TrackedEntity`, because a future reader is otherwise entitled to "simplify" the bases back.

  **`Equipment` is deliberately excluded.** It is `@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)` precisely so that value-hashing works -- its docstring says so ("Frozen=True allows hashing, enabling unique set generation") and `tests/test_verification_logic.py` compares two `Equipment` values for equality. `DicomSequence` is likewise untouched. The rule is about *records with identity*, not about dataclasses.

  **The sweep behind "nothing relied on it", stated because absence of evidence is the usual failure here.** Six `.remove(<entity>)` call sites exist (`tests/test_phi_retention.py:191`, `tests/test_save_reparenting.py:48,66,82,104,120`); every one passes the exact object it took out of the list, and `list.remove` uses `PyObject_RichCompareBool`, which short-circuits on identity before ever calling `__eq__`. No `.index(<entity>)`, no `<entity> in <list>`, no `assertEqual` on a graph entity among 93 candidates, no set/dict/sorted of entities, and nothing in `scripts/` compares graph entities -- membership everywhere is UID-keyed (`persistence.py:2276`, `session.py:3278-3287`). `PhiFinding` is a value dataclass whose `__eq__` does recurse into its `entity` field, but nothing anywhere does `finding == finding`, `set(findings)` or `findings.remove(f)`. There are no subclasses of `TrackedEntity`/`DicomItem` outside `entities.py`. The decisive check was a runtime one rather than a grep: the full suite run with identity semantics forced by monkeypatch on all six classes gave 1225 passed against a 1225-passed baseline, and 1225 passed again under `ISOCENTER_FORCE_THREADS=1` -- that last run is the one that counts, because spawned worker processes re-import `entities` fresh and would not inherit a monkeypatch, so a value-equality dependence living only inside `scan_worker`/`_export_instance_worker` would have read as a false pass without it. The real `eq=False` change was then landed *before* any new test and the whole suite re-run on it (1225 passed), which is the only way to exercise `slots=True` + `eq=False` class construction and the genuine spawned-worker path.

  **Pickling and multiprocessing are untouched, and this was checked rather than assumed.** `eq=False` changes `__eq__` and `__hash__` and nothing else; no entity class defines `__getstate__`, `__setstate__`, `__reduce__` or `__deepcopy__` (the only such methods in `isocenter/` are on `SqliteStore` and `SidecarManager`). `_make_lightweight_copy` constructs fresh entities field-by-field and `_rehydrate_findings` resolves findings through a UID map; neither reads `__eq__` or `__hash__`.

  **Tests.** `tests/test_entity_state_vocabulary.py` gains an identity section extending the existing `make_entities()` fixture, with `DicomItem()` added as a fifth parameter alongside the four graph types -- sequence items are bare `DicomItem`s, are deep-copied into worker clones by `_make_lightweight_copy`, and carry nested findings back, so they are covered directly and not merely through `Instance`. `test_two_entities_with_equal_fields_are_not_equal` and `test_entities_are_hashable_and_distinct_in_a_set` were shown red three ways: reverting `eq=False` on the four leaves (8 failed, 16 passed -- `DicomItem` survives because the bases still carry it); reverting it on the two BASES instead, which is exactly the issue's four-class proposal (10 failed, 14 passed, and `Instance.__hash__` is `None`); and green on all six (24 passed). Five parameters defend six classes because `__hash__ = None` propagates down the MRO. A third test, `test_every_tracked_entity_subclass_hashes_by_identity`, walks the module's members rather than a hand-written list and asserts `__hash__ is not None` for every `TrackedEntity` subclass -- so a class added later with the default `eq` is caught when it arrives instead of relying on someone remembering to extend the parametrization, which is this milestone's own question asked of the fix itself. It carries a `len(subclasses) >= 5` guard so it cannot pass vacuously if the walk ever stops finding anything, and it is red naming the offending class if `eq=False` is dropped from any one of the six (`AssertionError: ['Series'] carry the dataclass default \`eq=True\`...`). The tests' docstrings carry the reason two field-equal entities are the ordinary case here -- the `scripts/` fixture builders and `_make_lightweight_copy` both produce them by design -- and the PR #296 near-miss, where a review prescribed `prepared[inst]` reasoning that entities "hash by identity": under the old default the first lookup would have raised `TypeError`, and had it been hashable by value it would have silently conflated two field-equal instances.

  **`SqliteStore.save_all` keeps `id(inst)`, and its docstring is corrected rather than its code.** That map gave three justifications for `id()`; only the third was about hashability, and only the third is now false. The other two stand untouched -- `series_pk` is unknowable before the transaction and a series can be re-parented in between (`_reparent_series`), and the SOP Instance UID is mutated **in place** by `regenerate_uid()` in `_apply_redaction_outcomes`, so a UID-keyed lookup misses a renamed instance and loses it from the index. Removing `id()` there is not a one-spelling cleanup; it is an unrelated refactor of a one-day-old data-loss fix (#287/#288, PR #296). The docstring now says the object is hashable and identity-comparing since #299 and that `prepared[inst]` would work, and names the re-spelling as **#300** (filed, v0.9.2). No test pinned the old wording -- `grep` over `tests/`, `docs/` and `isocenter/` for `unhashable|__hash__|eq=True|hash by identity` hits only those two docstring lines -- which is recorded here rather than assumed, per the obligation #203 named.

  `scripts/mutation_probe.py`'s `TARGETS` needed no edit: `tests/test_entity_state_vocabulary.py` imports only `isocenter.entities`, which is not a target module, and `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py` passes unchanged.

### Fixed

- **A processes-path redaction no longer leaves the pre-redaction pixels resident, exportable and durable (#322, #326).** `Session._apply_redaction_outcomes` now sets `instance.pixel_array = None` beside the loader rebind, inside the same `_pixel_swap_lock`, and the `instance._pixel_array_unwritten = False` that used to stand there is deleted.

  Under the processes executor -- the default on any build with a GIL, so on the 3.12 gate -- the redaction worker mutates a *pickled copy*. It writes the redacted frame to the sidecar and hands back a `SidecarPixelLoader` for it; the parent rebound that loader and never touched its own resident array. A caller who had previewed the image, run OCR over it, or replaced it with `set_pixel_data()` was left holding two answers to "what are this instance's pixels", and everything that asks the *instance* rather than the loader took the stale one.

  It does not stop at a stale read. `_persist_pixels` hashes the resident array; its dedup guard is `inst._pixel_hash == digest`, and after a redaction `_pixel_hash` is the *redacted* digest while the array is the stale one -- so the guard misses, the pre-redaction frame is appended to the sidecar, and the loader is rewired to it. **The corruption becomes durable**: the store now points at the unredacted frame, and every later session over it reads and exports pre-redaction pixels under a full redaction attestation -- `DERIVED`, Burned In Annotation `NO`, a Derivation Code Sequence saying the pixels were modified, and `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH`. `tests/test_redaction_reaches_the_exported_file.py` measures all three arms, including an exported `.dcm` read back with `pydicom`.

  The whole suite missed it because `tests/conftest.py`'s `reloaded_redaction_session` ends with `inst.unload_pixel_data()`. That one line is the mitigation, and every redaction test inherited it.

  **Rejected: shipping the redacted array back from the worker.** That is a full frame of IPC per redacted instance, on the one path the lazy-pixel design exists to keep lean, and a multi-megabyte payload inside a `return_generator=True` stream. The null costs nothing and makes the processes path converge on the threads path, which already discards the array in `execute_redaction_task`'s `finally` (#228's lesson).

  Three properties make it correct where it stands. It is **inside `if loader:`** -- a mutation carrying `pixel_hash` and no loader has no redacted frame to fall back on, and nulling there would have `_persist_pixels` record the *stale* loader frame stamped with the *new* hash, which is #293's every-check-passing shape. It is **inside the lock** `_persist_pixels` reads under, so the null is atomic against a concurrent background save rather than a second window into it -- it strengthens #274. And it is not a memory hazard whatever #322's own caveat says: nulling the instance's reference does not invalidate an array the caller already holds, it only stops the *instance* serving pre-redaction pixels.

  **#326 is dissolved rather than pinned.** That issue asks for a test on `_pixel_array_unwritten = False` at `session.py:2731`, whose comment said it was "REACHABLE BUT UNTESTED". With the array nulled the line is dead: `unload_pixel_data()` returns True on a `None` array *before* it consults the flag, `get_pixel_data()`'s loader arm clears it on the next read, and `_persist_pixels` takes its `arr is None` arm and never reads it. So the line is deleted, and the state its comment described -- `set_pixel_data()` then `redact()` with no save between -- is constructed by a test anyway (`test_a_replaced_array_redacted_without_a_save_stays_freeable`), because deleting a guard whose stated justification is "untested" without building that state is how #293 would come back. `_persist_pixels`' `arr is None` arm now enumerates three nulling sites instead of two.

- **Float Pixel Data is carried in the sidecar, so a float instance survives its source file going away -- the first half of #183; the icon half is still open (#183).** `ingest_worker` extracts (7fe0,0008)/(7fe0,0009) to the sidecar, `SidecarPixelLoader` gains an explicit element channel, and `set_pixel_data()` keeps it true. `isocenter/io_handlers.py`, `isocenter/entities.py`, `isocenter/pixel_geometry.py`; five new tests in `tests/test_float_pixel_data_export.py`.

  **What was outstanding.** `populate_attrs` skips group 7fe0, and `_is_routed` called the float pair "routed" for a reason that was true but thin: `_export_instance_worker` wrote them back from the array `get_pixel_data()` re-read **out of the source file**. Nothing extracted them at ingest, so the bytes survived exactly as long as that file stayed where it was. Measured: ingest, `save()`, `close()`, delete the source, reopen and export, and the export writes **nothing** -- `0 of 1 instances exported`, one ERROR audit row -- because `Instance.get_pixel_data()` reaches `FileNotFoundError: Pixels missing and file not found`. The metadata index was portable and the pixels were not.

  **The reconstruction needs the dtype, and no descriptor carries it.** `SidecarPixelLoader` derives its dtype from BitsAllocated and PixelRepresentation, and **no DICOM descriptor says "float"** -- a 32-bit float frame and a 32-bit integer frame both declare 32. So the dtype has to be carried. It rides `attributes` under the non-tag key `_ISOCENTER_PIXEL_DTYPE`, the channel `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` already uses: `_merge` skips `t.startswith("_")` and `_split_core_and_private` keeps a non-tag key inline, so it round-trips through `attributes_json` and never becomes an element in an exported file. **No schema change, which #183 also weighs and which this did not need**; `instance_blobs` keeps `kind = 'pixels'`, and the two literal `WHERE kind = 'pixels'` reads in `persistence.py` are untouched.

  **`set_pixel_data()` keeps the carrier true, and that is not optional.** It names the dtype of the frame *currently held*, and the sidecar decodes by it, so the setter writes it for every floating-point array and **deletes** it for anything else. Replacing a float instance's pixels with an integer array and leaving the carrier behind would have the loader read those integers back as floats -- the same silent corruption arriving from the other direction.

  **The carrier is a dtype and not an element, and float16 is why -- a defect free-threading found and the GIL hid.** An element-shaped carrier cannot name float16, because no DICOM element carries it at any width. But the *sidecar* is ours, and a `float16` array written to it came back as `uint16`: same bytes, different numbers, no warning. The export then took its integer path and never filed the `DATA_LOSS` row that says a float16 array has no DICOM element to be written into. `tests/test_export_loss_audit.py::test_a_float16_loss_on_a_failed_write_names_the_missing_file` builds exactly that instance and had been passing for a reason nobody chose: `session.export()`'s `save()` is asynchronous, so the unload in `release_memory()` was usually refused -- no loader had been bound yet -- and the *resident* float16 array reached the export worker untouched. Once the save won that race, the reload returned `uint16` and the row vanished. Measured: green on 3.12 and on 3.14 with the GIL, and reproducibly red on 3.14t at 118 test files and above, green at 93 and below. The dtype carrier closes the round trip, and a new parametrised test pins float16, float32 and float64 through the sidecar directly, so the guard no longer depends on which of the two got there first.

  **Additive by construction: an undecodable float source is left exactly where it was.** The Pixel Data arm rejects a file whose pixels will not decode (`Decompression Failed`, at ingest). The float arm deliberately does **not**: it leaves `p_bytes` as None and lets the failure happen at export, which is where #226's guard lives -- `RuntimeError: Lazy load failed ...` carrying pydicom's own words, one ERROR row, `0 of 1` written, `REVIEW_REQUIRED`. Rejecting at the door instead would have moved that failure and left the report saying `0 of 0 requested`, a weaker claim about a file that was handed to us. Float sources that *can* be decoded gain the sidecar; the ones that cannot lose nothing.

  **`_is_routed`'s two conditions are unchanged and still load-bearing.** Top level only, and only when the instance has no Pixel Data of its own. `ingest_worker`'s arms are `if "PixelData" ... elif` the float pair, so on a malformed file carrying both -- which PS3.5 Section 8.2 forbids -- Pixel Data is what reaches the sidecar and the float half is genuinely lost, which is exactly what `has_pixel_data` reports. One question, one answer, and the same answer it gave before the bytes moved.

  **The second half of #183 is NOT in this release and the comments say so.** Pixel data nested inside an Icon Image Sequence is still skipped and still reported. `instance_blobs` is UNIQUE(instance_uid, kind), so a second pixel blob per instance needs a `kind` naming the sequence item, which the two literal `kind = 'pixels'` reads would have to learn about, and which is a decision #150 also has an interest in. Both the `_ROOT_ONLY_ROUTED_TAGS` and `_FLOAT_PIXEL_TAGS` comment blocks now name which half landed rather than claiming the whole issue.

  **Two things the implementation plan called for and this deliberately does not do.** It does **not** make the export prefer the stored provenance over `arr.dtype.kind == 'f'`: dtype is the honest rule for what *can* carry the bytes, and routing a float array to (7fe0,0010) because the source was integer is precisely the corruption #170 fixed -- a file that reads 1056964608 where the array said 0.5. And it adds **no new `_pixel_array_unwritten` clear-site**: the float bytes reach the sidecar through the same `SidecarPixelLoader` binding Pixel Data already used, so there is no new write path and no eighth clear to test (#293, #326).

- **A private tag now exports under the Value Representation its source file gave it, instead of collapsing to `LO` (#154).** New `DicomItem.attribute_vrs` carrier, recorded at ingest, persisted in `instance_attributes.value_rep`, carried through the `__sequences__` JSON for nested items, and consulted by `DicomExporter._merge` behind a conformance gate. `isocenter/entities.py`, `isocenter/io_handlers.py`, `isocenter/persistence.py`; 34 new tests in `tests/test_private_tag_vr_roundtrip.py`.

  **The defect.** `_merge` asks `dictionary_VR` for a VR; for an odd-group tag it is told there is none, which is what "private" means. `_fallback_encoding` then picks a VR from the **Python type** of the value, and every number lands on `('LO', str(value))`. Measured before the fix, a source block written `SH`, `DS`, `US`, `UL`, `IS`, `UT`, `FL`, `AT` exported as `LO` for all of them but `UT` -- and `(0009,100f)`, an `AT`, came out as the literal text `'(0010,0010)'`, a spelling nothing reads back as a tag. Values byte-faithful, types destroyed, no exception, no `DATA_LOSS` row, `PASS`.

  **The VR never survived ingest at all**, which is why this is a carrier and not a lookup. `attributes` is `{tag: value}` with nothing beside it, so `attribute_vrs` is a parallel map rather than a widened value: `attributes` holds what the element *is*, and pairing every value with a VR would change that shape for every reader of it.

  **Recorded odd-group only, never `UN`, never for a bytes value.** Even-group tags resolve their VR from the dictionary, and a second answer beside the dictionary is one that can disagree with it. `UN` is the absence of an answer rather than an answer -- and it is also what *every* private element resolves to under Implicit VR Little Endian, so an implicit-VR ingest records nothing and behaves exactly as before, **by construction** rather than by a measured non-regression. Bytes are skipped for a reason that is not the one the issue suggests: PS3.5 §6.2.2 makes `UN` right for raw bytes, and `_split_core_and_private` routes an odd-group `bytes` value to `attributes_json` rather than to `instance_attributes`, so a binary private tag has no `value_rep` row for a VR to sit on. Do not "complete" the carrier for them.

  **Restoring the VR meant restoring the type, and that was not in the plan.** The EAV tier stores `str(val)` in a TEXT column, so a reloaded session holds `"7"` where a `US` went in -- and pydicom **refuses** a `str` for the binary-encoded VRs. Measured on pydicom 3.0.2: `US`, `UL` and `FL` warn at `add_new` and then raise `struct.error: required argument is not an integer` from `filewriter.write_numbers`, which fails the **whole export** rather than the offending element. So a VR carrier alone would have fixed the fresh path and left the reloaded one broken in a louder way. `_VERTICAL_VR_PARSERS` reads the numeric VRs back as numbers; the text VRs (`SH`, `LO`, `UT`, `DS`, `IS`) are deliberately absent, because those *are* text on the wire and converting them would invent a Python type the source never had. `AT` needs one more thing: `str(BaseTag)` is `'(0010,0010)'`, which `Tag()` refuses to read, so `_vertical_atom_text` stores the decimal integer and `_vertical_atom_value` is its exact inverse. That pair has to stay a pair. `load_vertical_attributes_bulk`'s docstring said the type question "is #154's, not this method's"; this is #154 answering it.

  **The recorded VR never bypasses the guards, and that is the design rather than a caveat.** A recorded VR is a fact about the value the *source file* carried; anonymisation, redaction or a plain `set_attr` can replace that value with one the VR no longer suits. `_value_fits_vr` therefore gates every use: a `str` containing `\` under a 1-n VR (#195), a value past its VR's per-value cap (#190), a numeric VR over a value that is no longer a number, `bytes` under anything -- all take the existing fallback, unchanged. A `False` from that gate is never worse than no recorded VR at all, which is the pre-#154 behaviour; a wrong `True` is worse, and the paragraph below is what that costs. `_fallback_encoding`'s `bool` arm was pre-placed for exactly this day (#283) and now has a test that kills it: `bool` is an `int` subclass, so `True` under a recorded `US` must not be written as `1`.

  **The gate asks the question in the writer's terms, not Python's, because a wrong `True` costs more than a wrong `False`.** The first draft asked only about the Python type and, for text VRs, the character cap. That is not the question pydicom answers, and five shapes got through it -- each measured on pydicom 3.0.2, each a value a `set_attr`, a redaction or an anonymisation rule can produce against a source element that was perfectly conformant:

  - A **non-numeric string under `IS` or `DS`**. Both are text on the wire, so the cap was the only thing asked, and `"ANONYMIZED"` clears `IS`'s twelve characters easily. pydicom converts on the way in and raises `ValueError: could not convert string to float` from inside `_merge`'s `try`, so an element that had exported perfectly well as `LO` became a `DATA_LOSS` row and a `REVIEW_REQUIRED` grade instead. `IS` goes one step further than `DS` and takes the integer of what it parsed, so `DS` of `"nan"` writes and `IS` of `"nan"` raises.
  - An **out-of-range integer under a binary VR** -- a `US` holding `70000`, a `UL` holding a negative. `add_new` accepts it without a word, `_merge` records no loss, and `struct.pack` raises from `filewriter.write_numbers` **past** `_merge`'s `try`: `OSError: 'H' format requires 0 <= number <= 65535`. That does not lose the element, it fails the whole file -- precisely the trap `_fallback_encoding`'s own docstring names, reopened one layer up. New `_INTEGER_VR_RANGE`.
  - A **float too wide for `FL`**, which is 32-bit: `3.5e38` is an ordinary Python float and `struct.pack('<f', ...)` cannot carry it. Same failure mode, same table's worth of consequence. New `_FLOAT_VR_PACK`, so the gate packs the value the writer will pack.
  - A **number whose rendered text exceeds its VR's cap.** `_TEXT_VR_MAX` was consulted on the `str` arm alone, and pydicom renders a float with `str()` and writes the result without complaint -- `str(1 / 3)` is eighteen characters where `DS` may carry sixteen. #190's cap binds a rendered number too.
  - A **`tuple`.** It is the one sequence shape `add_new` does not convert to an element's multi-value form, so under a binary VR it reached `struct.pack` still a tuple and failed the file. Nothing in the pipeline produces one -- pydicom hands back `MultiValue`, the store hands back `list` -- so it is a hand-`set_attr` shape, and `_fallback_multivalue` has always accepted it. The gate now declines it and it exports exactly as it did before #154.

  `test_the_gate_and_the_writer_agree_across_the_whole_vr_table` is the general form: for every VR in either table and every value shape those five use, a `True` from `_value_fits_vr` must mean pydicom writes the element under that VR *and* keeps every value inside the VR's cap. That is the assertion the `UC` miss above and these five would all have failed, rather than five separate ones each found by hand. It counts the pairings it graded and asserts a floor under that count, because the loop only asserts on what the gate accepts: a gate that accepted nothing would run an empty body and report green, turning the strongest test in the file into the emptiest.

  **`_VERTICAL_VR_PARSERS` and `_INTEGER_VRS`/`_FLOAT_VRS` are two tables that have to name the same VRs**, and nothing made them. A binary VR added to the gate's tables but not to the parsers is not an error anywhere: the fresh export keeps the recorded VR and the reloaded one silently falls back to `LO`, so the two journeys #154 exists to make agree quietly stop agreeing. `test_every_binary_vr_the_gate_accepts_has_a_way_back_out_of_the_store` asserts the equality.

  **The range guard is tested on the reloaded journey as well as the fresh one, because that is where the value is reconstructed rather than remembered.** `instance_attributes` stores `str(val)` in a TEXT column and `_VERTICAL_VR_PARSERS['US']` turns `"70000"` back into an `int`, so the gate is handed a plausible integer with no memory of where it came from -- and measured against the unfixed gate, that export logs `OSError: 'H' format requires 0 <= number <= 65535 for data_element: (0009,1006) Private tag data US: 70000` and reports `Success: 0/1`. Not a mislabelled element: no file at all. `test_the_range_guard_still_runs_on_the_reloaded_path` asserts on the written file rather than on the element, because that is the loss.

  **An uncapped text VR is listed, not inferred from absence, and the first draft got that wrong.** `_TEXT_VR_MAX` gives the per-value character cap PS3.5 Table 6.2-1 sets for each text VR, and a comment claimed the three uncapped ones (`UT`, `UR`, `UC`) were handled by simply not appearing in it. They were not: the membership test read `vr not in _TEXT_VR_MAX and vr not in _VM_ONE_TEXT_VRS`, and `UC` is the one uncapped VR that is **not** VM 1 -- it is 1-n, which is exactly why it cannot live in the backslash set. So a private `UC` element recorded its VR at ingest and was rejected on every path, exporting as `UT` like before, while the comment beside it said otherwise. `_TEXT_VR_UNCAPPED` now names the three explicitly, because "no cap recorded" is also what an unknown VR and every binary VR look like: reading absence as "unbounded text" would have accepted a recorded VR this module knows nothing about. The `UC` row in the table is red on both the fresh and the reloaded journey before the fix.

  **Three journeys, and the tests run all three**, because a carrier that survives one and not another is the likely way this goes half-built. `session.export()` is always processes (#185), so the fresh path pickles `attribute_vrs` with the `Instance`; a save/close/reopen goes through `value_rep`; and a **nested** private tag goes through neither, riding the `__sequences__` JSON, so `_serialize_dicom_item` emits a symmetric `__vrs__` key. `_serialize_item` -- the root -- deliberately emits no top-level `__vrs__`: the root's VRs live in `value_rep`, and a second copy in the JSON would be a second answer that can disagree with it after a partial write.

  **`Instance.__post_init__` inlines `DicomItem.__post_init__` rather than calling it**, so the new field had to be added in both places; the copy now says so. Missing it rejected every file at ingest with an `AttributeError` from deep inside the worker -- which is what an inlined duplicate one field out of date looks like from the outside.

  **One existing expectation flips, and its own docstring called it.** `test_private_tag_export.py::test_a_multi_valued_private_tag_survives_export` expected a private `US [1, 2, 3]` to export as the strings `["1", "2", "3"]`, and said the strings were "#154's, and deciding it here would be deciding it on the wrong ticket". The `US` row now expects integers; the `LO`, `DS` and `IS` rows are unchanged, because those VRs are text on the wire. `test_export_merge_shape.py`'s two `_merge` doubles gained the `vrs` parameter -- a double that does not accept the real signature makes the worker fail and the test measure the failure.

- **A declared Planar Configuration of 1 no longer transposes a reloaded image, and no longer labels an exported file's interleaved bytes as planar (#210).** Two sites: the planar branch and the transpose in `SidecarPixelLoader.__call__`, and the correction in `_write_pixel_geometry`. `isocenter/io_handlers.py`, `isocenter/pixel_geometry.py`; four new tests in `tests/test_planar_configuration_roundtrip.py`.

  **The invariant, written down for the first time: isocenter holds and stores pixels interleaved, always.** pydicom **de-planarises on read**. Measured on pydicom 3.0.2, a 2-frame 3x3 RGB file whose (0028,0006) is `1` and whose Pixel Data really is planar comes back as `pixel_array.shape == (2, 3, 3, 3)` with `ravel() == [0 9 18 1 10 19 ...]` -- interleaved. So `ingest_worker` extracts interleaved bytes from a planar source, `set_pixel_data()` is handed an interleaved-shaped array, `persist_pixel_data` writes `arr.tobytes()` of it, and a `1` sitting in `attributes` describes the *source file* rather than anything isocenter holds.

  **#210's stated mechanism is inverted, and its Option 1 would have made both arms wrong.** The issue says the multi-frame arm is the broken one and the single-frame arm right, and that `ingest_worker`'s normalisation keeps the broken one unreachable. Measured, both halves are false. The multi-frame arm reshapes interleaved bytes as `(frames, rows, cols, samples)` and was correct all along; the single-frame arm reshaped as `(samples, rows, cols)` and transposed, which is what planar bytes would need and what these bytes never are. It is reachable, because `set_pixel_data()` deliberately preserves a declared 1 -- #217 narrowed `planar_configuration_default` precisely so it would stop overwriting one -- and because a reloaded graph carries whatever was stored. A test now pins the multi-frame arm as green on both sides, so "fix the multi-frame arm to honour planar 1" goes red rather than reading as an improvement.

  **The second site is not in the issue and is the higher risk of the two.** `DicomExporter.write_tree()` on the same graph wrote a file whose pixel bytes are interleaved and whose `PlanarConfiguration` element says `1`: `_merge` stamps `attributes` onto the dataset, and `_write_pixel_geometry` -- which runs after it -- corrected the value only when the instance had **not** declared one. That is a corrupt exported DICOM file produced by the serializer, with no error, no warning and no `DATA_LOSS` row. `_READBACK_DESCRIPTORS` does not include this element, so `export(verify_readback=True)` does not see it either. The exported test asserts on `ds.pixel_array`, not on `ds.PixelData`, because the raw bytes matched on both sides of the defect -- it is the meaning the file gives them that was wrong.

  **`planar_configuration_default` is unchanged and keeps its other caller, so this does not reverse #217.** The two sites ask different questions and now spell them differently. `set_pixel_data()` asks "what descriptor should the graph carry when the caller supplied none", writes no file, and leaves a declared value alone. `_write_pixel_geometry` describes the pixel element it has just written, which is interleaved whatever `attributes` says, and so writes 0 for every colour instance. It spells the `samples >= 3` gate out itself rather than dropping the gate: an unconditional write would add (0028,0006) to every monochrome export, an element those files must not carry. `planar_configuration_default`'s docstring claimed `ingest_worker`'s normalisation meant "on every live path there is nothing here to correct"; that is corrected in place -- ingest is not the only way a declared 1 reaches the graph.

  **`SidecarPixelLoader.planar_conf` is deleted, not left unread.** With the branch and the transpose gone nothing consults it, and a stored field that nothing reads is a second answer to "which layout is this" waiting to disagree with the one the reconstruction uses. Nothing ever put `planar_configuration` in the loader's `metadata` dict, so only the `instance` arm ever set it.

- **The lazy pixel re-read now accepts the same files the eager ingest accepted, so a header-less file can produce its own pixels (#289).** `pydicom.dcmread(self.file_path, force=True)` in `Instance.get_pixel_data()`'s third step. `isocenter/entities.py`; two new tests in `tests/test_io.py`.

  **The defect.** There are two reads of a source file. `ingest_worker` reads with `force=True` (#281), so a file with no preamble and no `DICM` prefix -- a raw vendor dump, and the shape `force=True` exists for -- indexes cleanly: `IngestSummary(ingested=1, failures=[])`, nothing declined, nothing audited. `get_pixel_data()`'s step 3 re-read did not force, so the same file could not produce its own pixels: `RuntimeError: Lazy load failed for ...: File is missing DICOM File Meta Information header or the 'DICM' prefix is missing`. A file accepted at one boundary and refused at the next is the defect, whichever boundary would be right on its own; the two reads are of the same bytes and have to answer the same way.

  **The step-3 path is the ordinary one, not a corner.** `import_files` binds a `_pixel_loader` only when a sidecar manager is supplied. Without one the instance carries neither a loader nor a resident array, so the very first `get_pixel_data()` reaches the `file_path` re-read -- no explicit clearing required.

  **The silent half, which is the worse one.** `unload_pixel_data()` promises to clear the resident array only when it can be brought back, and it accepts `file_path` as one of the two routes back. On such a file that route did not work, so the unload returned `True` -- the caller's evidence that the pixels are safe -- and the pixels were then unreachable, with no exception, no audit row and nothing in the report. Both halves are now pinned, the loud one and the quiet one.

  **The cost, stated rather than hidden.** If `file_path` has since been replaced by a file that is not DICOM at all, forcing parses it into a dataset with no pixel element, so the `any(t in ds ...)` guard at the end of that arm returns `None` where this used to raise. Measured rather than reasoned -- the imagecodecs fallback is not reached, and no `AttributeError` escapes from `ds.file_meta`. That narrows #226's rule that "could not decode" is not `None`, for a population that a forcing ingest had already accepted; it is a behaviour change and belongs here rather than in a comment alone. `tests/test_entities.py::test_lazy_loading` pinned the call signature and now names `force=True` in it.

- **A DA-spelled study date no longer renames its own export folder across a store round trip (#189).** `Study.__setattr__` normalises a parseable date string to `datetime.date` on assignment; `persistence._as_loaded_date` is now that same rule under the name that says it is `_as_stored_date`'s inverse. `isocenter/entities.py`, `isocenter/persistence.py`; three new tests in `tests/test_study_date_roundtrip.py`.

  **The defect.** `export_folder_names()` builds the study directory with `str(study.study_date or "NoDate")`, not `format_study_date`. Hydration turns the stored text into a `date`, so a hand-built graph carrying `"20240115"` filed under `Study_20240115_` before a save and `Study_2024-01-15_` after one -- the same study in two directories.
