A re-export into an existing tree therefore wrote a second copy rather than overwriting the first, and nothing reported it, because the (0008,0020) *element* is identical on both sides: both export paths render a `date` as `YYYYMMDD`. #171 fixed the element and recorded the folder as known and accepted; that paragraph is now deleted from `_as_loaded_date`'s docstring, because it is no longer true.

  **Fixed at the assignment, not at the folder.** Routing `export_folder_names()` through `format_study_date` would have renamed every *ingested* study's directory -- ingest already produces a `date`, so those folders are ISO-spelled today and would all move. Refusing the DA spelling at the boundary, the other option #189 offers, would break `DicomBuilder.add_study`'s own documented example and around thirty tests for no gain. Normalising is the only one of the three that leaves `study_date` a single type everywhere, which is `_as_loaded_date`'s stated goal for the other half of the same round trip, and it makes the constructor and hydration agree by construction rather than by two parallel parses.

  **The rule is `date.fromisoformat` and nothing else**, because that is what hydration already used: it accepts the extended form `2024-01-15` and, on the 3.12 floor, the DICOM basic form `20240115`. An unparseable value is still returned exactly as given -- a date we cannot read is a date we do not have, not one we invent and not one we discard (#60). The body lives in `entities.normalize_study_date` and `persistence._as_loaded_date` delegates to it; two copies of "text that names a day becomes a `date`" is exactly how the two ends came to disagree. The `datetime` refusal (#188) still runs first, so the one input the round trip could not restore is still rejected rather than normalised.

  **Accepted blast radius, and it is wider than the issue says.** A hand-built `Study("S1", "20230101")` now files under `Study_2023-01-01_` *fresh* as well as reloaded -- a one-time directory rename for graphs built in Python rather than ingested. It also reaches remediation: `RemediationService`'s SHIFT_DATE arm writes `_shift_date_string`'s eight-character result straight onto `study.study_date`, so a jittered date is now a `date` too. That is the same divergence one layer up -- a jittered study filed under `Study_20221114_` fresh and `Study_2022-11-14_` after a reload -- and it is closed by the same line. `tests/test_remediation.py::test_date_shifting_remediation` asserted `len(new_date) == 8`, which pinned the spelling rather than the date; it now asserts the type, and says in place what it used to pin.

- **`unload_pixel_data()` refuses to drop an array that has been replaced and not yet written, so replaced pixels can no longer vanish with every integrity check passing (#293).** New `Instance.discard_pixel_data()` for the callers that mean to throw the array away. `isocenter/entities.py`, `isocenter/persistence.py`, `isocenter/services.py`, `isocenter/session.py`; ten new tests in `tests/test_pixel_divergence.py`.

  **The defect.** `unload_pixel_data()`'s contract is "clear the resident array only when it can be brought back", and its check was `file_path or _pixel_loader` -- which asks whether there is *a* frame to reload, not whether that frame is the one being dropped. `set_pixel_data()` replaces `pixel_array` and deliberately does not touch `_pixel_loader`, so after a save has bound a loader, replacing the pixels leaves the guard passing while the array and the stored frame have diverged. The unload then discarded the only copy of the new pixels, and the next `save_all` took `_persist_pixels`' `arr is None` arm, which re-records the loader's own offset, length and hash -- the **old** frame -- and marks the instance persisted. Store, sidecar, memory and `_pixel_hash` then all agree on the superseded frame, so every integrity check passes and nothing in the report, the audit log or the grade says a thing. `_persist_pixels`' own comment named this by hand and filed it separately; that comment now states what `arr is None` there does and does not imply -- either an unload of an array equal to the stored frame, or a deliberate `discard_pixel_data()`, both of which make recording the loader's frame correct.

  **The decision: track the divergence and refuse; add a second, explicitly named spelling for a deliberate discard.** A new transient `Instance._pixel_array_unwritten` is set by `set_pixel_data()` and cleared by everything that writes the resident bytes. `unload_pixel_data()` refuses while it is set, through the refusal path that already existed for the no-loader case -- a debug line and `False`, silently, because a correct refusal is not a fault (#108). `discard_pixel_data()` is pre-#293 `unload_pixel_data()` exactly, under a name that says what it does. **Two behaviours, two names**: this is not an alias and must not become one. "One spelling per behaviour" is about one behaviour wearing two names; these answer different questions -- "free this if it is safe" and "throw this away".

  **The field is not called `_pixel_dirty`, and the name is the point.** "Dirty" already means two different things here -- persistence-dirty (`_revision` versus `_persisted_revision`) and PHI-dirty (`phi_status`) -- and both were called "dirty" once, which made them indistinguishable in the code and in the output users read. A third would be worse than either, and this one is narrower than both: it tracks one specific divergence, not a general state of unsavedness.

  **The rejected option, and the source evidence that decided it.** #293 proposes, as the cheaper fix, having `set_pixel_data()` drop `_pixel_loader`. The source contradicts the issue. `tests/test_redaction_failure_is_reported.py:429-430` and `:761-763` both state, as the *design* of tests 14 and 18, that failed-redaction cleanup works **because** `set_pixel_data` does not clear `_pixel_loader`: the loader is what lets the partially-zeroed array be dropped and the original reloaded through the sidecar. Clearing it would make that unload refuse, leaving the partial mutation resident for the next `save_all` to publish -- trading a silent loss for a silent partial redaction, which is strictly worse in a de-identification library. It also throws away the only handle on the stored frame, so a diverged instance could never be reverted. Both docstrings now name #293 so the trade is not re-taken by someone reading only the issue.

  **Also rejected: hashing `arr.tobytes()` against `_pixel_hash` instead of carrying a flag.** Stateless and appealing, but it hashes every resident frame on every `release_memory()` sweep -- a full pass over pixel bytes on the exact path the 100GB scaling story depends on. And `_pixel_hash` is not always populated: hydration wires `_pixel_loader` without it (`tests/test_blob_storage.py:611-615`), so `None` would have to mean either "refuse everything hydrated" or "allow", and "allow" reopens the hole.

  **Keeping arrays resident is not on the table and nothing here does it.** The refusal fires only on an array that has never been written anywhere -- precisely the array that must not be dropped. Two of the ten tests exist to hold that line: `release_memory()` reports only counts, so an over-broad refusal is invisible and turns the one operation this library offers for reclaiming RAM into a silent no-op.

  **The docstring is scoped to what the flag actually tracks, because promising more would be this milestone's defect inside its own fix.** It refuses when the array was replaced **through `set_pixel_data()`** and not since written. An array mutated *in place* -- `arr = inst.get_pixel_data(); arr[...] = 0` -- diverges too and is not detected, and neither is the writeable arm of `RedactionService._redact_instance_pixels`, which zeroes a file-backed array in place and never calls `set_pixel_data`. Both were already this way and neither changes here; the limit is stated rather than papered over.

  **The clear-sites were swept rather than trusted, and the sweep found six of seven unpinned.** Every place that publishes the resident bytes has to clear the flag, or the instance becomes permanently unfreeable. Removing each site one at a time and re-running the pixel, memory, geometry, blob, redaction-failure and save/redact-race files initially killed **only** the `_persist_pixels` rebind; the other six survived deletion untested. Four more tests were added until each of `get_pixel_data()`'s loader and pydicom arms, `persist_pixel_data()` and the `_persist_pixels` de-duplication arm is killed by exactly one test; the `_persist_pixels` rebind is killed by three (its new test plus both `tests/test_save_redact_race.py` cases, which already covered it). Two sites still survive deletion and say so in their own comments rather than being quietly left: `get_pixel_data()`'s imagecodecs fallback (reaching it needs a transfer syntax pydicom cannot decode and imagecodecs can, on a diverged array) and `_apply_redaction_outcomes`. That second one is **reachable but untested**, which is not the same as redundant: nothing stops a caller from `set_pixel_data()` and then `redact()` without a save in between, and the processes path pickles the whole `Instance` -- there is no `__getstate__` -- so the worker redacts the resident replacement and hands back a loader for it. Deleting the clear would leave that instance permanently unfreeable. On the threads path the worker mutated this very instance and `persist_pixel_data` had already cleared the flag. No test constructs the first sequence; writing one is filed rather than done here.

  **Two sites the issue's own list named need no clear, verified rather than assumed.** Hydration (`load_all` and `load_patient`) and ingest build fresh `Instance` objects, whose field defaults to `False`; measured on both load paths and on an ingest, the flag is `False` with the loader wired. And `compact()` deliberately does **not** clear it: compaction rewrites where a frame lives, not which frame it is, so a diverged array is still diverged afterwards. That one carries a comment, because the next reader will otherwise "fix" it.

  **Call sites.** `session.release_memory()` keeps `unload_pixel_data()`, with a comment saying not to "optimise" it to the discarding spelling. That comment does **not** vouch for `release_memory()`'s own docstring, which promises flatly that nothing is discarded: #293 narrows that gap, it does not close it, because an array mutated in place is still dropped there silently. The overclaim is left standing in the docstring and filed rather than rewritten here. The two redaction cleanup blocks (`isocenter/services.py`) move to `discard_pixel_data()`, byte-for-byte their previous behaviour, so nothing in `tests/test_redaction_failure_is_reported.py` changes. Each call site says which of the two questions it is asking.

- **The audit worker no longer keeps its store alive forever (#316).** `isocenter/persistence.py`, plus three tests in a new `tests/test_audit_worker_does_not_pin_its_store.py`.

  `SqliteStore.__init__` started its writer with `target=self._audit_worker`. A running `Thread` holds its target and a bound method holds `self`, so every `SqliteStore` ever constructed was immortal for as long as its worker ran -- and the worker's only exit condition is `stop()`, which nothing calls on a store its owner simply dropped. Measured over one full suite run while #250 was being instrumented: **149 threads alive at interpreter exit, 147 of them audit writers**, each holding a store, its sqlite handles and its sidecar descriptors. #250 fixed the same shape one level up for `PersistenceManager`, and its own argument named this as the remaining half.

  The loop is now the module-level `_audit_worker_loop(store_ref, stop_event, wakeup, audit_queue)`, taking a **weak** reference and returning when it resolves to `None`. `__setstate__` gets the byte-identical construction -- one spelling, and forgetting the second site is how half a fix ships.

  **Why the exit condition is safe, and it has a one-line answer.** `flush_audit_queue()` calls `_drain_and_write()` on the *caller's* thread, and `_drain_and_write` takes `_audit_write_lock` itself. The read barrier #218 built does not depend on the worker existing at all -- the worker only removes background latency. So a worker that exits cannot weaken the barrier, cannot invert `_audit_write_lock` -> `_memory_lock` (same locks, same order), and does not touch the untimed wait's semantics. `stop()` and `__getstate__` are unchanged.

  **Three things the code now says out loud, because each is a plausible "simplification".** The `del store` before returning to the wait is load-bearing: a strong reference held across the one-second wait restores exactly the immortality this fixes, a second at a time, forever. The Events and the Queue *are* held strongly and that is safe -- `threading.Event` references nothing, and audit rows are plain string tuples carrying no entity graph and no store. And **there is a new loss**: a store collected with rows still queued drops them, which today cannot happen only because the store is immortal. Holding the queue strongly is what lets the exit path say so, with a `WARNING` naming the count and the issue, so the loss is visible rather than silent.

  Two of the three tests are the pair that keeps the fix honest: one asserts the *store* is collectable (polled, not asserted once, because the worker holds a transient strong reference between resolving its weakref and dropping it), the other that this store's own worker **thread** is no longer alive -- without which the fix could trade a leaked store for a leaked spinning thread and the first test would still pass. That second one asserts thread identity rather than a count, and the first draft got it wrong in a way only the free-threaded interpreter showed: `len(_audit_workers()) == baseline` reads process-global state, and every *other* abandoned store in the run is now also free to exit, so on 3.14t a baseline of 5 leftover workers had gone to 0 by the time the poll ran and the test failed while the fix was working perfectly. Naming this store's own worker cannot drift that way. Neither calls `stop()`, since an un-stopped store is exactly the population the 149 came from. The third test -- that the new loss is *reported* -- matches the count by its phrase (`"1 audit row"`) rather than by the bare digit: `"1" in message` was satisfied by the `#316` the message ends with, so it would have held just as happily for a report of seven rows, an assertion about the count that could not see the count. Measured: `3 failed` before, `3 passed` after, and the full suite is `1284 passed` -- the blast radius this change could have had (any test dropping its last store reference and expecting a background-written row) turned out to be empty.

- **Compaction rebinds each loader under `_pixel_swap_lock`, and refuses outright if a save is outstanding (#295).** `isocenter/session.py` and `isocenter/persistence_manager.py`, plus three tests in a new `tests/test_compact_rewiring_is_locked.py`.

  `compact()`'s rewiring loop rebinds `offset` and `length` on every sidecar loader in the graph, and did so **outside** `SqliteStore._pixel_swap_lock`. Two assignments is a torn read a reader can land inside: a loader on a new offset with an old length reads the wrong bytes or runs off the end of the sidecar. That is the same shape as #274, where a loader and its pixel hash were swapped separately and the instance read back unredacted pixels under a full redaction attestation, self-consistently, because the stale hash matched the stale frame. The loop was safe only by the convention `compact()`'s own docstring stated -- "nothing else may be writing pixel state while this runs" -- which nothing enforced.

  **Three changes, and no new lock.** (1) The loop is extracted as `Session._rewire_sidecar_loaders(updates, wave_updates) -> int`, because it is the part under test and needs to be callable without running a compaction: through the front door, `compact()`'s own `save(sync=True)` also takes `_pixel_swap_lock`, so a test could not tell the loop's acquisition from the save's. (2) The lock is taken **per instance, around both rebinds together** -- the offset map is fully in hand before the loop starts, so the critical section is two attribute assignments and never spans a sqlite read, and the lock is a leaf, preserving the documented `_pixel_swap_lock` -> `sidecar._lock` order trivially. (3) `PersistenceManager` gains `has_pending_saves()` and `compact()` raises `RuntimeError` when it is true.

  **Filed under Fixed rather than Breaking, deliberately.** A previously-working `compact()` call can now raise `RuntimeError("compact() requires that no pending save be outstanding: ...")`, which is the shape of a breaking entry. But the calls that newly raise are exactly the calls that were already violating the PRECONDITION the docstring has always stated, and whose observable outcome was a rewritten sidecar with loaders on offsets that no longer exist. Converting silent corruption into a refusal is a fix; the exception is named here so a reader gets the breaking-entry depth either way. The load-bearing half of that argument is that a **single-threaded** caller cannot reach the refusal at all: since #294 `compact()` leads with a `save(sync=True)` that drains the manager, so anything queued before the call is gone before the check runs. Only a caller that was already concurrent with a writer -- the caller the PRECONDITION has always excluded -- sees the `RuntimeError`.

  **The refusal's placement is the load-bearing part**: after `save(sync=True)`, before `compact_sidecar()`. Refusing after the rewrite would leave the file compacted and every in-memory loader on a pre-compaction offset -- strictly worse than not checking.

  **A `_compaction_lock` was considered and rejected, and the audit that clears it is recorded because the next reader will ask.** All three `persist_pixel_data` call sites are in `services.py`, which contains no `_get_connection` block, and `_persist_pixels` is reached only from `_prepare_pixel_frames`, which `save_all` runs *before* the connection opens -- so no pixel writer holds `_memory_lock` while touching pixel state and such a lock would be deadlock-safe. It is still wrong here: `compact()` leads with `save(sync=True)`, so both taking it is one thread acquiring a non-reentrant lock twice; and scoped to the prepass alone it would not be correct, since a writer can release and then commit, and a compaction slipping into that window reads a blob index missing those rows and reclaims live frames as dead space. Correctness needs it held across the whole `save_all` -- a coarse global save lock, ordered *above* the documented `_audit_write_lock` -> `_memory_lock` pair. That is a real design and a bigger one than this issue.

  **What is still not closed, said plainly.** A save queued *after* the check runs concurrently with the rewrite. The per-instance lock removes the torn rebind entirely; it does not make a compaction safe against frames appended during the rewrite. `compact()`'s docstring now separates the enforced part from the part that remains convention, and the residual race is filed rather than claimed. Since #294 that residual is also the only way the refusal fires at all, because `compact()`'s opening `save(sync=True)` drains the manager -- so the test exercises the guard's wiring by forcing `has_pending_saves()` true rather than by queueing a save that would be gone before the check. Measured: `2 failed, 1 passed` before, `3 passed` after.

- **`save(sync=True)` drains the persistence manager before it writes, so "synchronous" means what it says (#294).** `isocenter/session.py`, plus one test in a new `tests/test_sync_save_does_not_overlap_an_async_one.py`.

  It called `store_backend.save_all` on the caller's thread with no regard for what the manager's worker was doing, and the worker may be inside `save_all` over the same object graph at that moment. `save()` followed by `save(sync=True)` is an ordinary sequence, and `compact()` opens with a synchronous save of its own, so this is the documented path rather than a corner. Since #287 the two sidecar prepasses run fully in parallel, which turns #287's bound on orphaned frames from a per-store property into a per-concurrent-save one.

  The fix is the shape `audit()` and `redact()` already use -- `persistence_manager.flush()` first. **The price, stated in the docstring and here:** `save(sync=True)` thereby inherits `flush()`'s deliberate never-return-early property, so a genuinely wedged worker wedges a synchronous save instead of letting it race one. That is the trade `flush()`'s own docstring argues for; callers ask for synchronous precisely so they can read or close afterwards. `compact()` inherits it too, and its docstring now says so.

  **This makes `save(sync=True)` a third `flush()` call site, and it is on the save path.** `flush()`'s own comment argued its per-call waiter thread was cheap because "`Session` calls `flush()` from exactly two places, `audit()` and `redact()`, and not on the save path" -- a sentence this change falsifies, and the #309 and #315 entries below still state as it was then. The bound survives, but for a different reason, and the comment now says which: all three sites are per *caller invocation* and none is per queued item, and each waiter is created and reaped inside its own call. `compact()` inherits the third site through the synchronous save it leads with.

  The test tags each `save_all` by the thread it runs on rather than by call order -- session setup can already have driven one through -- and asserts the two never interleave. Measured: `1 failed` before, `1 passed` after.

- **`close()` writes the save its worker never finished, instead of walking past it (#314).** `isocenter/persistence_manager.py`, one comment in `isocenter/session.py`, plus five tests in a new `tests/test_close_does_not_drop_an_orphaned_save.py`. **This is a behaviour change to `close()` and to the `atexit` handler**, and it is the sentence #309's entry had to retract.

  `PersistenceManager.shutdown()` returned on its first line whenever the worker was already dead. That is exactly the state #309 describes -- an item taken off the queue and never counted off, sitting in `_inflight` -- and it is also where an item still in the deque sits when its consumer stops. `Session.close()` is what `__exit__` calls and is the one call a user makes for the express purpose of having their work on disk, and it dropped both, silently, with no log line and no audit row.

  **`close()` was not made to flush, and that was the design call.** `flush()` documents that it never returns early, on purpose: a bounded wait that gave up would turn a visible hang into a silently dropped save. A `with` block that does not exit is worse than the bug it would fix. So `shutdown()` reconciles instead, from a `finally` so it covers the early return too, and the reconciliation is **bounded at one `save_all`** -- never a wait on the queue's unfinished count. That it goes as far as writing, rather than only reporting, is because the caller's thread is already blocking on the join anyway, and a report about a save nobody can retrieve is a worse deliverable than the save.

  **A live worker's state is never touched, and the reason is a regression this fix could otherwise have shipped.** If the join timed out, a save is genuinely running: its `_inflight` entry is the item that worker is inside `save_all` with, so writing it here would double-write and race. Its *queue* is the sharper half -- `shutdown()` has already posted the sentinel that stops that worker, and a drain that swallowed the sentinel would leave a `while True` worker with nothing left to end it. That is #250's leaked consumer, reintroduced by its own fix, and it would ship under a green suite unless a test asserts the worker still exits. One does.

  **What that branch leaves unwritten is now stated exactly, because "a live worker's queued items are never saved" is not the truth.** The sentinel sits where it was `put`, so items *ahead* of it are still the live worker's and it writes them before it stops -- deferred, not lost. Items *behind* it were queued after `shutdown()` began; that worker exits on the sentinel without reaching them and nothing here writes them either. Those are the accepted hole. The report names its number a **queue depth**, sentinel included, rather than a count of lost saves, and the in-flight number is `len(self._inflight)` over *every* owner -- so it can include a prior worker's orphan alongside the running save. Both are diagnostics for a human reading a teardown log, and over-reporting is the right direction to err on a channel whose job is to say something was left behind.

  **`task_done()` exactly once per item consumed.** A reaped orphan was `get()`-ed by its dead owner and owes one; an item still in the deque owes one for the `get_nowait()`. Miss one and `unfinished_tasks` stays above zero on a manager `save_async` can restart, so the next `flush()` never returns -- #309's hang, reintroduced. Count one too many and the queue raises `ValueError: task_done() called too many times`. A `None` found in the drain is a **stale sentinel** from an earlier `shutdown()` whose worker died without taking it; `_worker` special-cases those, and a drain that did not would run `patients, prune = None` and raise `TypeError` out of `close()`.

  **Nothing escapes, and that is now a property of the code rather than of an argument.** `Session.close()` re-raises the first exception any of its steps produced and `__exit__` returns None, so an exception from the reconciliation would replace whatever the user's `with` body was unwinding with a persistence one. Every write is inside `except Exception` -- but `task_done()` sits in the `finally` beneath it, and it is the one statement in the drain whose safety rests on the arithmetic above rather than on construction. Its answer to a wrong count is `ValueError: task_done() called too many times`, which a bare `finally` would let escape. It now has its own `except ValueError` and logs instead, so "it never raises" holds even if the arithmetic is ever broken by a later change.

  **Failures are reported on two channels, on both paths.** A `logger.error` and an `ERROR` audit row. `log_audit()` only enqueues, so the row needs a drain behind it: `close()` runs `store_backend.stop()` *after* `shutdown()` and `stop()` flushes the audit queue -- the ordering now carries a comment saying so. That covers `close()` and not `atexit`, which is precisely the path #314 newly exposes: `_flush_at_exit` calls `shutdown()` with no `stop()` behind it, and the row would sit in a queue whose daemon writer the interpreter is about to stop at finalization. `_report_unreconciled` therefore calls `flush_audit_queue()` itself. It drains on the reporting thread under `_audit_write_lock`, takes no manager lock, and is bounded by one `log_audit_batch`, so it neither touches the invariant below nor turns the teardown into a wait.

  **Two things to know about the new exposure.** `_flush_at_exit` calls `shutdown()`, so the **`atexit` path can now perform sqlite writes it never performed before**. That is intended and it is new; the ordering it relies on is the one #250 already checked, namely that `atexit` callbacks run while daemon threads are still alive. And the bound is *one `save_all` under `_SQLITE_BUSY_TIMEOUT_S = 120.0`*, not an absolute wall clock: a stuck external writer can make `close()` take that long. Bounded and diagnosable, which a wait on the queue would not be.

  `shutdown()`'s join timeout is now `_SHUTDOWN_JOIN_TIMEOUT_S = 30.0` at module scope rather than an inline literal -- same treatment as `_FLUSH_REPORT_INTERVAL_S` and `_SQLITE_BUSY_TIMEOUT_S`, no env var, so tests can shorten it without a test-only seam. Measured: `5 failed` before, `5 passed` after. Four of those five are the drop itself; the fifth is the reporting half of the boundedness test, whose *boundedness* assertions are green on both sides by design.

- **A restarted worker reaps the orphan itself, so recovery no longer lives only in `flush()` (#315).** `isocenter/persistence_manager.py`, plus one test in `tests/test_flush_orphan_recovery.py`.

  #309 gave the manager a way to recover a save its worker took and never finished, and put it in exactly one place: `_recover_orphaned_item`, reachable from `flush()` and nowhere else. `Session` flushes from exactly two places -- `audit()` and `redact()`. So a session that saves, loses a worker and then keeps saving, or closes, never calls it: the payload sits in `_inflight` with `unfinished_tasks` stuck above zero, and it is the *next* flush that finds it, if one ever comes.

  **The reap now also runs at the top of `_worker`, before it consumes anything**, because a worker restart is the event that always accompanies the recovery being needed -- `save_async` starts one precisely because it found the previous worker dead. The orphan therefore drains on the ordinary save path, with no flush anywhere.

  **What is called there is `_reap_orphans()`, never `_recover_orphaned_item()`, and that is the trap of this change.** `_recover_orphaned_item` takes `_recover_lock` and then calls `_start_worker`, which since #313 takes `_worker_lock`. Putting the reap in `_start_worker` instead would give `_recover_lock` -> `_worker_lock` -> `_recover_lock` on a non-reentrant lock -- a self-deadlock on the ordinary restart path. Extracting a reap that takes `_inflight_lock` alone, restarts nothing (this thread *is* the restart) and calls nothing, and calling *that*, makes the inversion impossible by construction rather than by a liveness argument about `self.thread`. The extraction is two helpers, not one: `_requeue_orphans` holds the `put()`-then-`task_done()` ordering that #309 argued for, in one place, because it now has two callers and duplicating that ordering is exactly how it gets reversed later.

  **A weakref-keyed `_inflight` was considered and does not work.** The payload is a `(list, bool)` tuple and `weakref.ref([])` raises `TypeError: cannot create weak reference to 'list' object`. A `WeakKeyDictionary` keyed on the owning `Thread` is constructible but drops the payload when the dead thread is collected, converting retention into silent loss -- the failure mode #309 exists to prevent. Reaping where the restart always runs keeps the payload.

  The test is deliberately `test_a_save_queued_after_the_orphaning_does_not_bury_it` with the flush removed, and its docstring says so. Measured: `1 failed, 5 passed` before, `6 passed` after.

- **Two callers that find the same dead worker no longer both start one (#313).** `isocenter/persistence_manager.py`, plus two tests in a new `tests/test_worker_start_is_serialised.py`.

  `_start_worker` is reached from two places and both did their own check-then-act on `self.thread.is_alive()`: `save_async` (unlocked, on the ordinary save path) and `_recover_orphaned_item` (under `_recover_lock`). Neither check is an answer once it is separated from the act -- two threads can observe one dead worker and both construct a replacement. The result is two consumers on one queue, and from there it is #250's shape exactly: `shutdown()` posts one sentinel, whichever worker eats it is usually not the one `self.thread` names, and the `join(timeout=30)` burns its full thirty seconds -- at teardown and again from `atexit`, after pytest's summary line, where nothing is watching.

  **The guard and the create-and-start are now one critical section**, under a new `_worker_lock`. `save_async` keeps its unlocked pre-check as a fast path and stops being the authority; that is the difference from the rejected alternative of having `save_async` take `_recover_lock`, which fixes two call sites and leaves the third one to get it wrong. `_worker_lock` is a **leaf**: nothing is acquired while it is held, and nothing under it calls into `SqliteStore` -- the body is a liveness check and a thread construction. The order against the manager's other lock is `_recover_lock` -> `_worker_lock` and never the reverse, since `_recover_orphaned_item` already holds `_recover_lock` when it restarts.

  The worker thread is also **named** `PersistenceWorker`. `tests/conftest.py`'s stall watchdog counts `threading.enumerate()` by name, so nine leaked workers used to be reported as nine `Thread-N` entries indistinguishable from anything else -- the diagnostic #250 needed and did not have. The name is a diagnostic, not a test seam, and the test asserts it on *this manager's own thread* rather than on a process-wide count of named workers -- a count reads state every other test in the run contributes to, which is the shape that made #316's first draft fail on 3.14t while its fix was working perfectly.

  **The test opens the window rather than racing for it.** Both callers are gated on a `threading.Barrier` placed *around* `_start_worker`, outside whatever serialisation the method itself has, and the first construction is then held until the second caller has made its own liveness check. Unfixed code constructs twice, deterministically; fixed code never reaches the second construction, because the second caller is blocked on the lock and finds a live worker when it is let through. Timing is one-directional -- a slow machine makes the unfixed race *more* reliable and cannot fail the fixed code, whose exclusion is a lock and not a race. Measured: `2 failed` before, `2 passed` after, with the failure reading `two concurrent saves started 2 workers on one queue`.

- **`flush()` reconciles a save its worker took but never finished, instead of waiting on a count nothing will decrement (#309).** `isocenter/persistence_manager.py`, plus five tests in a new `tests/test_flush_orphan_recovery.py`. `Session` flushes from exactly two places -- `audit()` and `redact()` -- and before this, one worker stopping in a two-bytecode window wedged both permanently. (An earlier draft of this entry named `close()` as a third; it is not one. `close()` calls `shutdown()`, which returns immediately when the worker is already dead, so an orphan that reaches `close()` is dropped *silently* rather than hanging it. That is a different exposure, it is not fixed here, and it is not claimed to be.)

  **The state, constructed and measured rather than inferred.** `_worker` takes an item with `queue.get()` and reaches `task_done()` from the `finally` of its inner `try`. That covers every ordinary exception *and* every `BaseException` -- `KeyboardInterrupt`, `SystemExit`, pytest's own `Failed` and `Skipped` all unwind through it. What it does not cover is the item: once `get()` has returned, the payload is out of the deque and `unfinished_tasks` is 1, and a worker that never reaches the `finally` leaves both facts standing. So:

      queue.empty()          is True    -- the item is out of the deque
      queue.unfinished_tasks == 1       -- nobody counted it off

  `flush()` guarded its restart on `not self.queue.empty()`, so in exactly this state it skipped the restart and went straight to `queue.join()`, which cannot return. Reproduced deterministically, no timing and no `ctypes`: stop the worker cleanly, `put` an item, `get` it from the test thread as the stand-in for a worker that died after `get()`. `STATE: empty=True unfinished=1 alive=False`, `flush returned within 5s: False`.

  **A restart alone does not fix it, which is what forces the design.** Measured the same way: `worker restarted: True`, `join() returned: False`, `unfinished: 1`. The payload is already out of the deque, so a fresh worker has nothing to `get()` and the count never moves. Reconciliation is required, not a restart.

  **The fix.** `_worker` records the item in `self._inflight` immediately after `get()` returns, under its **own thread as the key**, and pops that key inside the existing `finally`, *before* `task_done()`. `flush()` reconciles against that mapping, re-queueing every entry whose **owning thread** is dead -- a dead `Thread` is a stable observation and cannot resume, whereas a worker still holding its own entry is simply mid-save, and re-queueing under it would both duplicate the write and unbalance the count. Recovery does `queue.put(payload)` and *then* `queue.task_done()`: reversed, the count reaches zero between the two calls, a waiting `queue.join` wakes, and `flush()` could return before the payload was back -- a dropped save wearing a clean return. Both orderings are recorded as comments at the site, not only here.

  **The owner key is the fix, not a detail, and this is a correction to what was published first.** The first shape of this change used a single unowned `_inflight` slot and gated recovery on whether `self.thread` -- the manager's *current* worker -- was alive. Those read as the same thing only while a manager has had exactly one worker ever, and the sequence that separates them is ordinary rather than a corner: a worker dies holding a save, the caller queues another save (`save_async` restarts the worker, so `self.thread` is alive from then on), and only then does something flush. `Session.save()` followed by `audit()` or `close()` is exactly that shape. Measured on the single-slot code: the restarted worker's own `get()` overwrote the orphan, `saved: ['P_NEW']` with `P_ORPHAN` never written, `unfinished: 1`, `inflight: None`, `flush returned within 8s: False` -- the orphaned save **destroyed** and the flush hung anyway, reached *through* a restart rather than avoided by one. Per-owner keying closes it while preserving the property the gate was there for, because a live worker's own entry still has a live owner. `tests/test_flush_orphan_recovery.py::test_a_save_queued_after_the_orphaning_does_not_bury_it` pins it; verified red one mutation at a time against each half (restore the `self.thread` gate: red; restore the single slot: red).

  **The wait reports instead of going silent.** The bare `queue.join()` becomes a loop over a short-lived daemon waiter joined with a 30s timeout; each expiry logs the unfinished count, whether the worker is alive and whether an item is in flight, then re-attempts recovery -- so a worker that dies *after* the flush began is recovered too. `_FLUSH_REPORT_INTERVAL_S = 30.0` sits under `pytest.ini`'s `faulthandler_timeout = 300`, so a wedged flush explains itself before any dump. The cost is one short-lived thread per `flush()` *call* on a healthy flush, and one more per report interval while it is still waiting -- earlier waiters stay blocked until the queue drains, so a wedged flush accumulates a thread every 30s. Never one per queued item: `flush()` is per call site -- `audit()` and `redact()` -- not on the save path. `unfinished_tasks` is read for the message only; nothing in the recovery path depends on it and nothing anywhere touches `all_tasks_done`, both being CPython implementation details.

  **What this preserves, and what it gives up.**

  - *Preserved*: `flush()` still never returns before the queue is drained. No early return, no dropped save. **A bounded-wait-then-return design was rejected**: it loses saves silently, which is strictly worse than hanging, because callers flush precisely so they can read or close afterwards. `test_flush_still_waits_for_a_save_that_is_genuinely_running` exists to hold that line -- it parks a save inside `save_all` and asserts `flush()` does not return while it is parked -- and it **passes on unfixed code by design**, its job being to stay green across the fix rather than to go red before it. Its 0.5s negative assertion can only fail towards a false *pass*, never a false failure.
  - *Preserved*: ordinary exceptions and `BaseException`s raised inside `save_all` remain covered by the existing `finally`. Nothing about that path changed.
  - *Residual*: **two** narrow windows survive, and they differ in consequence. A thread killed between `get()` returning and its `_inflight` entry being written loses the save *and* hangs the flush, exactly as before -- that is the original defect, shrunk from *the whole duration of a save* to a couple of bytecodes. A thread killed between the `finally` popping its entry and `task_done()` returning loses nothing (the save has already committed) but still hangs the flush permanently, because no entry with the count at 1 is indistinguishable from a healthy queue. That second window is the price of clearing before counting off, and clearing before counting off is what stops a later recovery re-queueing an already-saved payload and leaving the count one above zero for good; it is also what makes the fourth test's second assertion race-free. Closing either window needs atomicity inside `Queue`. Both hangs are now self-describing through the 30s warning rather than silent. Two is the count *after* the owner-key correction above; the shape published first left a third and much wider hole, and an entry claiming two while that one stood would have been the sort of claim this milestone exists to retire.
  - *Given up*: a re-enqueued payload may be saved **twice**, if the worker died after `save_all` committed but before `task_done()`. `save_all` is idempotent for a given graph, so that is a redundant write, not corruption -- said out loud rather than left for a reader to discover.

  A fourth test was added afterwards for a gap the first three left: they write the `_inflight` entry by hand -- which is what makes them deterministic -- and so say nothing about the two lines in `_worker` that record and pop it for real, both of which survived deletion with the whole suite green. It parks a save inside `save_all` and reads the mapping while it is provably parked. Verified one mutation at a time: dropping the record leaves its first assertion red, dropping the pop leaves its second red, and nothing else in the suite moves. The fifth is the owner-key regression named above.

  Red as committed: 2 failed, 1 passed in 20.97s for the first three, both failures being the 10-second bounded wait. Every wait on `flush()` in the new file runs on a bounded helper thread, because a test for a hang must not add one.

- **The chaos test's 30 seconds, correctly explained: a live worker was being joined by a second one (#250).** One guard in `_start_worker`, two structural tests. This is the measured half of #250; it is **not** a fix for #250 (see the next entry).

  `_worker` is a `while True`. Setting `running = False` does not end it, and nothing else does either -- only the sentinel `shutdown()` posts does. `_start_worker` guarded on `if not self.running:`, so a save arriving while a live worker sat with `running is False` -- an entirely ordinary state, being the whole window between `shutdown()` setting the flag and the worker draining to the sentinel -- started an **additional** consumer on the same queue and rebound `self.thread`.

  Instrumented, `test_persistence_chaos` run alone: nine toggles, **nine live workers on one queue**. `shutdown()` posts exactly one sentinel and joins `self.thread`; any of the nine may eat it and usually it is not that one, so `join(timeout=30)` ran to the full 30.01s -- and then `atexit` ran `shutdown()` a second time for another 30.01s, *after* pytest's summary line, where nothing is watching.

      [M] shutdown: workers ever started=9 alive now=9
      [M] shutdown took 30.01s; self.thread alive after join=True; other workers still alive=8
      ... 1 passed in 33.44s ...
      [M] shutdown took 30.01s; ...                    <-- atexit, after the summary

  The guard becomes thread liveness, restoring `self.running = True` when a worker is already alive. That composition is what keeps `test_stale_sentinel` working, and it is written into the comment: a sentinel left pending by an earlier shutdown is read as *stale* precisely because `running` is True again, so it is counted off and the worker continues.

  `--durations` on the four persistence-adjacent files, same 25 tests, `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 PYTHONPATH=<worktree>`:

      before: 30.01s teardown tests/test_concurrency_stress.py::test_persistence_chaos
              ... 25 passed in 39.50s
      after:  (no teardown entry at all)
              ... 25 passed in 12.73s

  plus the second 30s at interpreter exit, which pytest never counted. `test_persistence_chaos` still passes; it asserts zero data loss, which this does not affect. Of the two new tests, only the thread-identity one goes red against the old guard -- which sentinel a leaked worker eats is a race, so the shutdown test passes either way and is a guard on the property rather than a second red. Said here rather than counted as evidence it is not.

- **The `atexit` flush holds a weakref, and half of what that was expected to buy did not materialise (#250).** `atexit.register` holds its arguments for the life of the process, so `atexit.register(self.shutdown)` made every `PersistenceManager` ever constructed immortal, and with it its `SqliteStore`, that store's sqlite handles and its sidecar descriptors. The registration becomes a module-level function taking `weakref.ref(self)`.

  Nothing is lost, and the reason is structural rather than a judgement call: a manager with a live worker cannot be collected anyway, because the worker holds `self` through `target=self._worker`. Only a manager whose worker has already exited becomes collectable, and it has nothing left to flush. The ordering it depends on was checked, not assumed -- `atexit` callbacks run while daemon threads are still alive; interpreter finalization, which stops them, comes afterwards.

  **Measured over one full suite run, before and after.** The earlier figures in this entry family -- 642 managers and 212 threads -- were taken at `58ff235`, before the worker-leak guard above; these two runs bracket this change alone.

      live PersistenceManager at interpreter exit: 647 -> 1
      live threads at interpreter exit:            149 -> 150
        before: {'AuditWorker': 147, 'MainThread': 1, 'IsocenterStallWatchdog': 1}
        after : the same three, plus one live 'Thread-N (_worker)'

  **The thread count went the wrong way by one, and it is a category change rather than noise, so it is named rather than rounded off.** Before, every one of the 647 managers' exit handlers ran and stopped its worker before the measurement (which is registered inside `pytest_configure`, so LIFO puts it last). After, one manager survives to be measured, and it is measured with a live worker. Instrumented on a further run, that manager reads `(thread alive=True, qsize=0, unfinished_tasks=0, running=True)`: it is uncollectable for exactly the documented reason -- its worker holds it through `target=self._worker` -- and it has **nothing left to flush**, which is the other half of the same argument. So the datum fits the reasoning rather than contradicting it, and the +1 thread is that worker.

  The 147 that did not move are `AuditWorker`, and the reason is worth more than the number: `SqliteStore` starts that thread with `target=self._audit_worker` -- a bound method, so each store with a running audit writer holds *itself* alive by exactly the mechanism this change removed one level up. Those stores, and their sqlite handles and descriptors, survive regardless of who else refers to them. So what is reclaimed is 646 manager graphs; the lifetime of a store whose audit writer was never stopped is a separate problem this does not touch, and an entry claiming otherwise would be the sort of claim this milestone exists to retire.

  Not undone either: the `atexit` list still grows by one small closure per manager. Unregistering at `shutdown()` was rejected -- a shut-down manager is restartable through `save_async`, and unregistering would cost it its exit-time flush.


- **#250 -- what was ruled out, and the statement that it is still open.** No fix is claimed. What this milestone adds is instrumentation (`tests/conftest.py`) and the retraction of two claims that outran their evidence.

  **#309 is real, and #309 is not #250.** Established by reading rather than by sampling: `_worker`'s inner `try/except/finally` reaches `task_done()` for `BaseException` too, so `KeyboardInterrupt`, `SystemExit` and pytest's `Failed`/`Skipped` all unwind through it. The only uncovered window is the two bytecodes between `get()` returning and entering that inner `try`. Reaching it requires a thread killed without unwinding, and nothing in-process can do that except `ctypes`/`PyThreadState_SetAsyncExc` -- `grep -l ctypes tests/` returns nothing. `test_persistence_chaos` does not kill workers; it leaked nine of them, which is the previous entry and is a 60-second cost, not a hang.

  **Two provenance claims are retracted.** The `main.py:330` attribution and the SIGTERM provenance both fail on inspection; neither is evidence for anything and both are withdrawn rather than left standing as background.

  **Reproduction attempts today: 0 for 2.** Two full runs, both green, 5m57s and 5m27s. A stall detector armed at 90s never fired. The exit-time figures above -- 642 immortal managers, 212 live threads, roughly 30 seconds of interpreter exit running past pytest's summary line -- are the measured state of the suite at that point; `RLIMIT_NOFILE` is 1048576 locally, so descriptor exhaustion is not a local suspect.

  **The blind spot the watchdog closes, established by reading `_pytest/faulthandler.py`.** `faulthandler_timeout` arms `dump_traceback_later` inside `pytest_runtest_protocol` and cancels it in that function's `finally`. It therefore covers setup, call and teardown of one item and *nothing else*: not collection, not the gap between items, not `pytest_sessionfinish` or `_ensure_unconfigure`, and not the `atexit` phase after the summary line -- which is exactly where this suite was already spending real time. A hang in any of those produces #250's signature: killed by an outer cap, with no failing test named.

  **A background diagnostic that writes to `sys.stderr` does not exist -- measured.** pytest's fd-level global capture has already `dup2`-ed over fd 2 by the time `tests/conftest.py` is imported, so a direct write to a dup taken at import time produced no output anywhere in the run. The dup that works is taken inside `pytest_configure`, where global capture is suspended: three background writes from a daemon thread appeared live and in order in the run's output, and nothing private to `_pytest` is touched. The rule is written into the file, because it generalises: **a background diagnostic channel must own an fd duplicated while capture is not in place, or it is not a channel.**

  The watchdog is one daemon thread on a 10s tick that reports after `_STALL_S = 120` seconds of nothing and **again every 120s while still stalled**, so a forty-minute hang leaves a series of dumps and a deadlock (identical stacks) can be told from a livelock. Each dump names the phase (`collection`, the nodeid, `sessionfinish`, `unconfigure / atexit`), how long it has been there, total elapsed, `threading.active_count()` with a `Counter` of thread *names* -- 212 names is noise, `{'AuditWorker': 90}` is signal -- `multiprocessing.active_children()`, whose churn across successive dumps is what a respawning pool looks like, and every thread's traceback. Verified end to end with the thresholds temporarily lowered: a sleeping test dumps twice under "running test item", and a test registering a slow `atexit` callback dumps twice *after* the "1 passed" summary line under "unconfigure / atexit". Being a daemon thread it ticks through the `atexit` chain and stops when interpreter finalization begins, which is afterwards.

  120s clears the longest legitimate gap either way -- the chaos teardown, 30.01s before the guard above and ~0s after -- and sits far inside CI's 20-minute Run Tests cap; `tests/test_packaging_contract.py` pins that inequality beside the existing `faulthandler_timeout`, `_SQLITE_BUSY_TIMEOUT_S` and `_WORKER_FAULTHANDLER_TIMEOUT_S` assertions. No change to any of those, to `pytest.ini`, to the CI matrix or to `.github/workflows/tests.yml`.

  **The cause of #250 is still unknown, the issue stays open, and the watchdog is instrumentation rather than a fix.** What changed is that the next occurrence should arrive with a stack instead of a silence.

- **`audit()` drains the persistence manager before it records what it found (#297).** Two lines at the top of `Session.audit()`, mirroring `redact()`'s existing flush. A scan *ends* by advancing `_revision` on every entity it touched -- `_record_scan_results` calls `record_phi_status`, and a new status is a change the store should hold -- and `save()` without `sync=True` returns with `save_all` still running on the persistence manager's thread. Since #287 that window is as long as all of the save's pixel I/O, not just its SQL. An instance dirtied inside the window is not in the dirty set the save froze, so the save neither writes it nor marks it persisted: it is left dirty, and nothing saves it afterwards, because `close()` shuts the manager down without enqueuing a save. Reproduced before the fix: the instance ends `has_unsaved_changes` true with its row already written, and the frame the save had prepared is discarded to all-`None` under the `COALESCE` on the update. The direction is safe -- a lost status, never a false clean claim -- but the loss is permanent and `close()` is silent about it.

  **This is reachable on the documented order, which is why a docs-only fix was rejected.** `README.md` and `docs/quickstart.md` both show `session.save()` and, further down, `report = session.audit()`. The quickstart documents an asynchronous save followed by an audit; that is exactly the window.

  **The cost, stated because it is not zero.** `save(); audit()` used to overlap the scan with the save's pixel write; it now serializes them, and since #287 that wait is all of the save's pixel I/O rather than just its SQL. The trade is the same one `redact()` already makes for the same reason: a silently dropped PHI status is worse than a slower audit, and the alternative -- letting the scan stamp statuses into a running save -- has no safe version. A caller who wants the overlap back can order the pipeline the other way round, or save with `sync=True` and pay the wait explicitly. One further consequence is filed rather than fixed: `flush()` can block unboundedly if the manager's worker thread dies between `get()` and `task_done()` (empty deque, `unfinished_tasks` still 1, so the restart guard does not fire and `queue.join()` never returns). The bug predates this change and `redact()` already carried it; what this change does is move it onto the documented order, which is why it is **#309** now.

  **Entry is the right place only because nothing inside `audit()` enqueues a save**, which is stated in the comment along with what to do if that ever changes (the flush moves to immediately before `_record_scan_results`). Re-grepped before writing it: `session.py` has one other `flush()`, in `redact()`, and `save_all` is reached from `save(sync=True)` and from the manager's worker, neither of which `audit()` calls.

  **The close-time-warning alternative was measured and rejected.** A guard in `close()` warning about entities still dirty would fire after every successful save: `save_all` calls `mark_persisted` for **instances only** (`persistence.py`, the `saved_instances` loop), so every patient, study and series in the session reads `has_unsaved_changes` true the moment a save completes. An unscoped warning is therefore pure noise on a healthy session. An instance-scoped version is worth having and is filed as **#307** (v0.9.2) rather than smuggled in here.

  **The test is deterministic, not timed** (`tests/test_save_all_contract.py`, alongside the other capture-window tests). `save_all` is parked inside a wrapper until an `Event` is set, and the only thing that sets it is `audit()`'s own flush -- so a run that reaches `_record_scan_results` with the save still parked is a run where the flush is missing, not a run that lost a race. `record_phi_status` is wrapped to record, for each call, whether the save was inside `save_all` at that moment; the assertion is that no observation is true. Two vacuity guards, because both failure modes pass green without them: the test asserts that the background save actually entered `save_all` (a timed-out wait would otherwise leave every observation trivially false), and that at least one status was recorded at all. Both waits are bounded so a wedge fails rather than hangs. Verified red by deleting the two new lines: the observation list comes back with a true in it and the failure message names the outcome.

- **`UN` is the VR for an unknown value because of PS3.5 §6.2.2, not A.1; three places said A.1 and every citation of it for this rule is corrected (#225).** Documentation only -- no code, no behaviour, no test outcome changes. What changes is whether a reader who opens the standard finds the rule where we sent them.

  **§6.2.2 is titled "Unknown (UN) Value Representation"** and is the rule. **Annex A is "Transfer Syntax Specifications", and §A.1 is "DICOM Implicit VR Little Endian Transfer Syntax"** -- it contains no UN rule at all. Verified against the NEMA primary source rather than from memory. The three sites: `DicomExporter._fallback_encoding`'s docstring in `isocenter/io_handlers.py`, the 0.8.x entry below that first documented it, and a test docstring in `tests/test_private_tag_export.py`. All three now read `§6.2.2` in the house style already used at `isocenter/io_handlers.py:1621` and in this file's Section 8.2 correction -- naming what A.1 actually is, so the same wrong citation cannot be reintroduced by someone who half-remembers it.

  **This file already carried the right reference and the wrong one for the same rule.** The `BINARY_RETENTION_MAX_BYTES` entry cites §6.2.2 correctly, for that section's *second* clause: an explicit-VR encoder must relabel as `UN` any value longer than a 16-bit length field can carry (65534). One changelog holding both the correct and the incorrect citation of a single section is the sharpest available statement of why this milestone exists -- neither citation was checkable, so both survived.

  **The correction strengthens the design's footing rather than relocating it.** That second clause is the cousin of `_fallback_encoding`'s `UT` escape hatch: both are about what happens when a value outgrows its declared VR. The retention threshold and the fallback encoding now rest on one paragraph of the standard instead of appearing to rest on two unrelated ones, and the docstring says so.

  **This deliberately gets no test.** A guard grepping for `6.2.2` would pin the current *text*, not its *truth* -- it would pass just as happily if §6.2.2 were also the wrong section, and would go red on a legitimate rewording. No test can check a DICOM section number; the check is reading the standard, which is what was done. Distinguish this sweep from the earlier one: the Section 8.2 correction below covers which pixel elements may coexist, and says of itself that "every citation of it for this rule is corrected". The same is now true of the UN rule, and the two sweeps are separate.

- **The project made claims about itself in six places and nothing read any of them; two of those places now have a checker, and the four that cannot have one are fixed, deleted or relocated (#234).** One defect class, not six bugs: a fact about the code written down somewhere the code does not look. No behaviour changes -- the two corrected runtime tips change printed text only.

  **The instances, and what each one got.**

  - **Method names in strings the package emits.** `tests/test_documented_api_exists.py::test_every_method_named_in_a_package_string_exists` walks every `ast.Constant` string in `isocenter/**.py` for `.name(`: **46 mentions, 28 distinct**, of which 6 resolve to a stdlib or third-party method correctly named in prose (`date`, `wrheader`, `lower`, `upper`, `get`, `tobytes`) and are declared in a `NOT_OURS` frozenset whose comment states that adding to it *claims* the name is not ours. The remaining 2 were defects, and both are corrected. `session.py`'s load-config tip said `.redact_pixels()`, renamed to `.redact()` in #227.

    **The second was not in the issue.** `session.py`'s `auto_remediate_config` tip said `.configuration.save_config()`. `IsocenterConfiguration` has **`save()`**; `git log -S save_config -- isocenter/` shows `save_config` has never existed under the `isocenter` name -- the tip has been wrong for the entire life of the package under that name, and was carried across the rename. **The guard found it on its first run**, which is the whole argument for having one: the issue knew about one wrong tip and there were two.

    Correcting it was not a rename. `save()` takes **no arguments** and writes to `configuration.config_path`, returning silently when that is unset -- and only `load_config()` sets it, so a session configured through `create_config()` reaches that tip with nothing to save to. A bare `.save()` would have replaced a loud `AttributeError` with a quiet no-op, which is strictly worse and is this milestone's own failure mode. The tip now names `.save()` *and* says `config_path` must be set first.

    Resolution is by name only, deliberately, and the test's docstring says so: a string naming `.save()` on the wrong receiver passes. Receiver-aware resolution needs a maintained third-party allowlist, and a maintained allowlist is a fresh instance of this defect.

  - **Calls in the documentation.** `test_every_isocenter_call_in_the_docs_resolves` parses every ` ```python ` fence in `README.md` and `docs/**.md` and resolves attribute calls whose receiver root is a session, config, store or exporter: **39 fences, all 39 parsed, 71 calls checked, ZERO allowlist entries**, 2 unresolved and both real. `docs/ocr.md` told users to call `session.configuration.save_config("updated_priv_config.yaml")` -- the same non-existent method, plus an argument `save()` does not take, so the obvious rename would have swapped one false claim for a `TypeError`. It now sets `config_path` and calls `save()`.

    **The fences are `textwrap.dedent`ed before parsing**, and that is not a nicety. A fence nested inside a list item is indented, so `ast.parse` raises `IndentationError` and the "skip what does not parse" arm swallows it. `docs/quickstart.md`'s multi-zone repair snippet is exactly that shape: without the dedent the guard silently skipped a real fence containing two real calls, while this entry claimed every fence parsed. The escape hatch exists for a future pseudo-code fence, not for a fence the parser simply could not read. With the dedent, 39 of 39 parse and none is skipped.

    The receiver restriction is the reason there is no allowlist: `df.head()` in a fence is pandas' claim, not ours. The wider every-attribute-call variant needs 13 allowlist entries for pandas and matplotlib and was measured and rejected. `ROOTS` is a **name** list, not a type check -- `docs/migration.md` binds a session as `with Session("store.db") as s:`, so `s` is in it, and a future short alias would go unchecked until someone adds it. The cost is stated in full in the file rather than understated: **seven** documented calls sit on receivers outside `ROOTS` and are values of ours -- six `result.*` calls in `docs/ocr.md` and one `filtered.to_zones()` -- and all seven were hand-resolved against `isocenter/discovery.py` and `isocenter/privacy.py` and exist. The remainder (`plt`, `df`, `re`, and calls on unnamed chained receivers) are third-party or expressions and are none of our business.

  - **README §5b, "Advanced Discovery (Data Science)" -- deleted (#303).** The guard caught `session.scan_for_discovery()`, which a grep of `isocenter/` and `tests/` shows has never existed. Reading the section revealed two further false claims the guard structurally cannot see: it showed a `Tag / Count / Keyword / VR` dataframe where `DiscoveryResult.to_dataframe()` returns `pd.DataFrame([vars(c) for c in self.candidates])` over `DiscoveryCandidate(text, confidence, box, source_index, classification)`, and a per-tag density dict where `get_density_matrix(bins=(10,10))` returns a `List[List[int]]` -- a spatial histogram of text-box centres. Three claims, three wrong.

    It is deleted rather than corrected because the nearest real method, `session.discover_redaction_zones(serial_number, sample_size=50, min_confidence=80.0)`, OCR-samples pixel data for burned-in text regions: a different feature in kind, not a corrected version of this one. **#303** proposes a genuine Discovery section written against it. Stated plainly so the limit is not mistaken for coverage: **the guard checks the method name and only the method name.** Neither output-shape claim is a name; catching those needs an executable-docs check, filed separately as **#304**. The README's step-2-to-5 cross-reference is updated from "including 5a and 5b" to "including 5a".

  - **`CLAUDE.md`'s `# full suite, ~600 tests` -- deleted, not updated.** `pytest --collect-only -q` reported **1248** at `8720b7e`, the commit this work branched from (1251 on the branch, which adds three tests), so the claim was 2.08x off. Updating it would only reset the clock: `99e924e` already hand-corrected this number from 414 to 600 as a drive-by, and it was wrong again within a week. A tolerance test was considered and rejected -- it buys a maintenance obligation and a CI-visible failure for a fact nobody needs, when the count's authority has always been running the suite. Replacing it with a duration was also considered and rejected, because today's honest number would be "about 18 minutes and sometimes hanging" (#250). After the deletion there is no claim left to check, which is the only outcome that cannot go stale.

  - **A packaging test that recommended shipping a Finder artefact -- fixed, not guarded.** `tests/test_packaging_contract.py`'s `_data_files_in_package()` did a bare `PACKAGE.rglob("*")` and kept every non-`.py` file outside `__pycache__`. A gitignored `.DS_Store` under `isocenter/` therefore failed both consumers with *"Declare them in setup.py's package_data"* -- advice that, followed, ships a macOS Finder artefact in the wheel forever. The walk now intersects with `git ls-files isocenter` (one subprocess, `cwd` pinned to the repository root), and the failure message says what is now true by construction: every name it lists is a tracked source file, so declaring it is the right fix.

    **The original guarantee is kept.** The issue proposed narrowing the check to `resources/*.json`; that replaces a walk with a maintained enumeration, which is this very defect class again, and `setup.py` already declares `resources/*.yaml`, so a future `.yaml` would escape it. Demonstrated both directions: a `.DS_Store` fails before the change with the misleading advice and passes after, and a git-tracked `isocenter/marker.txt` still fails after it, naming the file with the corrected advice. Fallbacks are the interesting part -- `git ls-files` returning a **non-zero status or an empty set** is treated as "git could not answer" and falls back to the walk minus dotfiles, because an empty tracked set is never a valid answer for a package that must ship four JSON resources, and intersecting against nothing would pass while checking nothing. The trade is commented: an unstaged brand-new resource is invisible to this test, which is correct semantics -- an untracked file is not in the sdist and cannot reach a user -- but a future reader would otherwise "fix" it back to a bare `rglob`. `pathspec` was rejected: a new test dependency to filter a dotfile, where one subprocess answers exactly.

  - **A falsified design spec -- relocated, not rewritten.** `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-29-pixel-geometry-authority.md` §6 promised `apply_redaction_to_array` a "new keyword argument, defaulted; old behaviour when omitted"; #217 made `geometry` a required positional, so omitting it raises `TypeError`. The clause is struck in place and a `**Superseded in part:**` line added to the front matter -- placement *is* the fix, since the clause sits nine hundred lines below where a reader enters. The record model is that both are visible: the spec said something, a later issue changed it.

    **And the convention is now written into `CLAUDE.md`**, which is the part that generalises: specs under `docs/superpowers/` are historical records, never silently rewritten after their date, because a spec quietly edited to match today's code cannot be told apart from one that was right all along. The existing `§11 Amendments` logs are a different thing -- corrections made *during* implementation -- and stay separate.

  **All three new guards assert they actually swept something**, following the precedent this changelog set two entries above for #299's `len(subclasses) >= 5`: each was shown to pass vacuously against an empty input -- no package sources, no documentation files, no sweep roots -- which a package rename or a move under `src/` would produce silently. They now assert `len(sources) > 30`, `len(files) > 10` and `compiled > 200`, and the escape sweep asserts each root directory exists rather than skipping a missing one. A guard for stale claims that goes green when its own walk breaks would be the joke telling itself.

  **What deliberately has no guard, and why.** The spec correction and the #225 citation correction below get none. A test grepping for the corrected string would pin the current **text**, not its **truth**: it would pass just as happily if the correction were wrong, and it would fail on a legitimate rewording. A dated design record cannot be diffed against code that has legitimately moved past it, and no test can check a DICOM section number.

  **`CLAUDE.md` is also unswept, and that is a scope choice rather than an oversight.** One of the six instances lived there and the file is dense with API claims; Test A's own regex and namespace were run over it -- 5 mentions, 4 distinct names, **0 unresolved** -- so there is no live defect hiding behind the choice. Reading it would mean a third parser for a third grammar (prose backticks, not string literals or fences), and its claims are overwhelmingly about architecture and process rather than method names -- the kind a name resolver cannot check anyway. Worth revisiting if a defect ever does turn up there. This paragraph and the one above it are recorded so no absence reads as an oversight.

  **`scripts/mutation_probe.py`'s `TARGETS` needed no edit.** `tests/test_documented_api_exists.py` imports no target module, and -- the reason it is a new file rather than a section of `tests/test_api_coherence.py`, which #234 names as its natural home -- `test_api_coherence.py` is listed under `io_handlers.py`, so sixty lines that never touch the exporter would be re-run against every one of its mutants for zero kill signal. The new file also spells no dotted module name anywhere, since `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py` scans file *text* rather than imports.

- **An invalid escape sequence in a shipped docstring was one CPython release away from making `import isocenter` fail, and every `.py` file under `isocenter/`, `scripts/` and `tests/` is now compiled under a filter that records them (#292).** No behaviour changes: the docstring's rendered text is byte-identical, because a backslash followed by a backtick and a doubled backslash followed by a backtick produce the same two characters today. What changes is what happens when they stop doing so.

  CPython's own account, quoted rather than paraphrased: an unrecognised escape in a non-raw string literal has been a `DeprecationWarning` since 3.6 and a `SyntaxWarning` since 3.12, and the documentation says "In a future Python version they will raise a SyntaxError". **No release is named**, so the only honest reading is that it happens and the date is not ours to know. This project's `python_requires` floor is 3.12 -- exactly the version where the `SyntaxWarning` begins -- so the new guard behaves identically on every interpreter in the support matrix, floor and free-threaded build alike.

  The single site was `isocenter/io_handlers.py:3066`, inside `DicomExporter._fallback_encoding`'s docstring. The paragraph carrying it explains why `UT` takes any string containing a backslash whatever its length, since under `LO` the backslash reads as the value delimiter and the value comes back split (#195) -- so the one paragraph in the package about handling a backslash correctly was itself mishandling one. It is fixed by doubling the backslash rather than by making the docstring raw: it is long and contains other backslashes in the `LO`/`UT` multiplicity prose, and a raw conversion would silently change how each of those reads.

  It was counted among the `4 warnings` of an otherwise clean `pytest` run, which is the whole reason it survived: a warning line in a green summary is not a thing anyone reads.

  **`tests/test_packaging_contract.py::test_no_shipped_module_carries_an_invalid_escape_sequence`** compiles every `.py` file under `isocenter/`, `scripts/` and `tests/` -- 224 files, about 0.2s -- and asserts nothing raised a `SyntaxWarning`, naming file, line and message, with shipped modules reported separately from unshipped ones because only the former is an `import isocenter` failure. The roots are wider than the shipped package deliberately: #292's goal is that a new one cannot land, not that today's one is gone.

  **The shape of the check is load-bearing and the docstring says why**, because the obvious alternative hides defects. `simplefilter("error", SyntaxWarning)` plus catching `SyntaxError` detects the problem, but the escalated warning aborts the compile at the *first* site in a file, so a second invalid escape in the same module stays invisible until the first is fixed. The recording form (`catch_warnings(record=True)` with `simplefilter("always")`) reports every site in every file in one pass. `always` is not decoration either -- the default per-location dedup can suppress a repeat. And under an escalating filter the problem surfaces as `SyntaxError`, not `SyntaxWarning`, so a guard written as `pytest.warns(SyntaxWarning)` around an escalated compile passes vacuously. The guard was therefore run red on the unfixed tree first, naming `isocenter/io_handlers.py:3066`, before the escape was corrected.

- **A DICOM DT whose fractional second `strptime` cannot parse is now shifted rather than silently left alone, and the coupling that makes remediation's `mark_modified()` calls redundant is checked rather than assumed (#132).** Test-only plus two comments; no behaviour changes. (One of those comments was landed wrong first and is corrected below.)

  **The issue's measurement was re-run at HEAD and its numbers are stale.** #132 records 14 survivors of 35 sampled sites; `remediation.py` now has **71 mutable sites**, and at budget 30 the probe reports `control PASS, killed 24/36, SURVIVED 12/36` at lines 94, 123, 148, 167, 194, 201, 218, 239, 247, 258, 392, 403. The sampling **stride is 2**, so 35 of the 71 sites were never tried: a line's absence from that list is not evidence its mutant is killed, and the two survivors the issue names as still open were argued dead individually rather than inferred from the list (`item.set_attr("0008,0102", "DCM")` deletion is killed by `tests/test_remediation_invariants.py:59`; `_resolve_patient_id`'s `return <value> -> return None` by `:103` and `:112`). Line 181 was not sampled, and treating that as "no claim" was itself a mistake, corrected below: it is a fifth member of the `mark_modified()` cluster.

  **All twelve were read, and exactly one has a PHI consequence.** Line 392, `if len(parts) >= 3:` -> `< 3`, in `_shift_date_string`'s dotted-DT fallback. DICOM DT allows a fraction of any precision; `%f` accepts one to six digits, so a real-but-messy value like `20230515.104822.1234567` misses every entry in the `formats` loop and depends on that fallback. Under the mutant it returns `None`, `_apply_single_remediation` takes its `else` arm at line 191, logs `"Invalid date format"` and **returns without shifting** -- so the true date survives in the graph, is exported, and no exception and no audit row records it. Nothing was watching that branch.

  **`tests/test_remediation_dates.py::test_a_fractional_second_the_format_list_cannot_parse_still_shifts_its_date`** closes it, and **replaces a dead stub**: `test_shift_date_dt_millis`'s body was comments ending in `pass`, asserting nothing, and this is exactly the question it was reaching for. It is a PAIR, and the differing fractions are the point -- `"20230515.104822.677"` is parsed by the `formats` loop and comes back with its fraction normalised to six digits (`"20230525.104822.677000"`), while the seven-digit `"20230515.104822.1234567"` comes back with all seven preserved, which is only possible via the fallback. That is what proves the second input genuinely reached the code under test rather than passing vacuously (the same guard against a vacuous fixture that #281 applied), and it means a future addition to `formats` that swallowed the second input would flip its expected value and fail loudly instead of going quiet. Hand-applied demonstration, not a probe re-run -- any edit renumbers the probe's sites, which #291 established:

  ```
  remediation.py:392:  if len(parts) >= 3:  ->  if len(parts) < 3:
    mutated:   1 failed  -- AssertionError: assert None == '20230525.104822.1234567'
    real code: 7 passed
  ```

  **`tests/test_entity_state_vocabulary.py` gains the revision coupling, and kills none of the twelve.** That is stated in the test's own docstring and here, because a reviewer would otherwise read it as a survivor-killer and find it is not: the file does not import the remediation module, so the probe never runs it for that module. What it does is convert an **assumed** coupling into a checked one. CLAUDE.md says `record_phi_status()` also advances `_revision`, and that this is why the `mark_modified()` calls in `remediation.py` are individually redundant and survive deletion -- and nothing tested it. If the coupling silently went away those calls would become load-bearing while every one of them still survived deletion in isolation, with nothing noticing. `test_recording_a_new_phi_status_leaves_the_entity_needing_a_save` (red with `self._revision += 1` hand-deleted from `entities.py`: 5 failed, 29 passed) is paired with `test_recording_the_status_an_entity_already_carries_changes_nothing`, which pins the short-circuit, so neither half of the contract can drift alone -- pinning only the bump would let someone make it unconditional and stay green while every re-scan dirtied the whole graph.

  **The `mark_modified()` cluster is FIVE sites, not the three CLAUDE.md names**: 148, **181**, 218, 239 and 247. Line 239 is the private-sequence arm added by #167 and postdates both CLAUDE.md's paragraph and #132's text. Line **181** is the `SHIFT_DATE` `else`/`setattr` arm, reached when the entity has no `set_attr` -- and that is not a rare path: `privacy.py`'s `_scan_study` emits `SHIFT_DATE` proposals targeting `study_date` on a `Study`, which is a `TrackedEntity` with no `set_attr` (verified: `hasattr(Study(...), "set_attr")` is False, `mark_modified` is True), so 181 is the executed path for every study date the inspector flags and is arguably the most-exercised of the five.
