It was outside the probe's stride-2 sample, and this entry first recorded that as "no claim made" -- but "the cluster is four sites" IS a claim about 181, by exclusion. Deleting 181 alone leaves the full suite green (1246 passed), a survivor of exactly the same shape and for the same reason as the other four. CLAUDE.md is deliberately not edited here -- #173 is queued to rewrite that exact paragraph and touching it now would collide for no gain -- so the corrected count is recorded here and on both issues instead.

  **`tests/test_remediation_invariants.py::test_removing_a_private_sequence_leaves_the_instance_needing_a_save`** is a third parametrization of an existing invariant over an arm nothing was running: the existing test drives REMOVE_TAG/REPLACE_TAG at `0010,0010`, which lives in `attributes`, so it stops at the first branch and the `elif proposal.target_attr in getattr(entity, "sequences", {})` arm (lines 221-242, #167) was never reached at all. It **kills no survivor** -- deleting that arm's `mark_modified()` alone stays green for the coupling reason above -- and its docstring says so. Red is demonstrated the joint way the sibling test's docstring already prescribes, with BOTH the arm's `mark_modified()` (239) and the shared `record_phi_status()` (257) deleted: 1 failed, 10 passed, on the assertion that the instance looks saved after its private sequence was stripped.

  **Deliberately NOT tested, with the reason for each, since "no test" is otherwise indistinguishable from "not looked at":**

  - **94, 123, 167, 194, 201, 258 -- log-only. Six of the twelve.** No state changes on any path; a test would pin a log string, fail on a reword and catch nothing. 94 (`self.store_backend and audit_buffer` -> `or`) differs only when a backend exists and the buffer is empty, since `audit_buffer` is only appended to under `if self.store_backend:` -- it then emits `"Flushing 0 audit logs..."` and calls `log_audit_batch([])`, which returns immediately. 123, 167 and 201 are deleted decline-to-remediate traces, 258 is `logger.info(details)` on the success path. Individually cosmetic; collectively an audit-trail gap, filed as **#301** (the same shape #126 names on the export side).
  - **194 -- and #132's own classification of it is corrected here.** The issue files the dropped `not` under "Likely to matter" ("Inverting it swaps which values get shifted"). It does not. Lines 191-206 are the `else` arm, reached only when `new_date` is already falsy, so nothing is shifted either way; both branches `return` with no state change, differing only in `logger.info("Skipping jitter for empty date")` versus `logger.warning("Invalid date format")`.
  - **148, 181, 218, 239, 247 -- redundant by the coupling, and #173's territory.** Pinning each call *individually* needs #173's precondition: an entity already at `REMEDIATED` after a reload, where `record_phi_status()` short-circuits and `mark_modified()` is the only thing left bumping the revision. Writing those tests here would pre-commit #173's open decision -- whether to make the bump unconditional -- which is precisely what would change what they pin (the call, or the behaviour). The two issues stay separate.

  **403 was misclassified in the first pass of this work, and the correction is the more interesting finding.** It was filed here as an equivalent mutant on the reasoning that any bad `parts[0]` raises `ValueError` in `strptime(base_date, "%Y%m%d")` and falls through to the same `return None`. **`strptime` with `%Y%m%d` is not length-strict**, which is what that reasoning missed: `"2023051"` parses as 2023-05-01 and `"230515"` as 2305-01-05, raising nothing. So an all-digit `parts[0]` of the wrong length sails past `isdigit()` into `strptime`, and without `len(...) == 8` the branch shifts a date nobody wrote and re-attaches `date_str[8:]`, which is misaligned at any other length. Measured with `or` substituted:

```
"2023051.104822.1234567"  ->  "20230511104822.1234567"   (real code: None)
"230515.104822.1234567"   ->  "2305011504822.1234567"    (real code: None)
```

  Both are fabricated values that still look like a DT, produced from input the real code declines -- a shape *worse* than line 392's, since 392 leaves a real date unshifted whereas this invents a plausible one and writes it into the graph as though the shift succeeded. It is now pinned by `test_a_malformed_date_part_is_declined_rather_than_shifted_into_a_fabricated_one` (red under the hand-applied `and -> or`: `1 failed -- assert '20230511104822.1234567' is None`, 7 passed; green on real code, 8 passed), and the comment at the line states the real mechanism instead of the false one. The false version was worse than no comment: it was a do-not-re-file instruction in production source, resting on an analysis that does not hold. The other five log-only survivors were re-read against the source after this, rather than left on the first pass's word.

  **A finding about the probe's own guard, worth writing down.** `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py::_importers` scans whole test files for the text `isocenter.<module>`, not their import statements -- so naming the remediation module in dotted form inside a *docstring* was enough to make the check demand that `tests/test_entity_state_vocabulary.py` be added to `TARGETS`. The docstring names it without the package prefix and says why. No `TARGETS` or CLAUDE.md-table edit was needed otherwise: `test_remediation_dates.py` and `test_remediation_invariants.py` are already listed for `remediation.py`, and `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py` passes.

- **A redaction pass concurrent with a background save can no longer leave an instance reading back its pre-redaction pixels under a full redaction attestation (#274).** `session.save()` without `sync=True` returns with `save_all` still running on the persistence manager's thread, against the same live instances -- and a redaction started in that window was a second writer to each instance's pixel state. Both the save (`_persist_pixels`) and the redaction swap (`persist_pixel_data`) read the resident array, append a sidecar frame, and rebind `_pixel_loader`/`_pixel_hash`; when the save read the bytes *before* the redaction zeroed them and rebound *after* the redaction's rebind, last write won. What a user was exposed to: the instance thereafter served unredacted pixels while carrying a new SOP Instance UID, `DERIVED` flags, and an `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` that matches the configuration -- so `export()` shipped the burned-in identifier under an attestation saying it was gone, a subsequent `redact()` skipped the instance because the hash matched (only `redact(force=True)` would have looked again), and the stale `_pixel_hash` matched the stale frame, so the loader's own integrity check passed: self-consistent corruption, invisible to every existing safeguard. This is the intermittent CI failure of `test_redaction_memory_swap` ("Instance 0 was not redacted!", twice in two days on unrelated commits); a three-event lockstep pin reproduced it deterministically on 3.12, 3.14 and 3.14t, and `tests/test_save_redact_race.py` now forces the interleaving on every run. The fix is one invariant, held four ways: at most one writer may traverse "read this instance's pixel bytes -> publish a loader for them" at a time, per store. `SqliteStore._pixel_swap_lock` makes both writers' read->write->rebind sections mutually exclusive (never held across a sqlite write; dropped and recreated across pickling per the #218 pattern); a revision guard in `_persist_pixels` -- the same capture-before-write discipline as `mark_persisted` -- makes a save that lost the race publish nothing and leave the instance dirty for the next save to write the truth; `_apply_redaction_outcomes` takes the same lock for its rebind, closing the process-executor arm; and `Session.redact()` flushes the persistence manager before dispatching, so the pipeline path never opens the window at all. What changes observably: a redaction that overlaps an in-flight save can now briefly block on the lock (or, for the pipeline, wait for the save to drain) instead of silently corrupting; no API moves and no new exception is raised.

- **Neither pixel writer can be killed by a concurrent `unload_pixel_data()` landing between its `None` check and its read of `pixel_array` (#288).** Both writers asked `inst.pixel_array is None` *outside* `SqliteStore._pixel_swap_lock` and then re-read `inst.pixel_array` *inside* it. Nothing holds that lock while nulling the field -- `Instance.unload_pixel_data()` assigns `None` under no lock at all, and is called from `release_memory()` sweeps and from redaction paths' `finally` -- so the null landing between the two reads produced, in `_persist_pixels`, `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'tobytes'` raised inside `_build_instance_writes` inside `save_all`'s open transaction: the whole save rolled back, nothing was marked clean, and a memory-managed pipeline lost a save round it had no reason to lose. The issue names only that half; the twin in `persist_pixel_data` has the identical split and a later corpse -- the re-read lands in a local, `hasattr(None, 'tobytes')` is False, and the `else` branch runs `hashlib.sha256(None)` for `TypeError: object supporting the buffer API required`, logged by that method's own `except Exception` and re-raised into the redaction swap. Both are fixed. **This is degradation, not a #274: no wrong bytes are written, no attestation is issued over stale pixels, and nothing exports PHI under a clean grade.** The two failures are loud, and their cost is a lost transaction and a failed swap. The fix is one line of discipline in each method: take the snapshot **once, inside the lock**, and branch on the local from there. The lock's span now starts before the first read of `pixel_array` -- it has to, because the early-return arm asks the same question the locked body asks and reads `_pixel_loader` to answer it, and splitting those two reads across the lock boundary *is* the bug -- and still ends at the loader/hash rebind, with no sqlite work inside it (the #280 order, `_pixel_swap_lock` before `sidecar._lock`, is unchanged). Holding a local reference is what makes this correct rather than merely non-crashing: the unload drops only the instance's reference, numpy keeps the buffer alive for ours, and unload never mutates contents, so the bytes written are still the instance's true pixels. Rejected, and recorded here so it is not re-proposed: taking the store's lock inside `entities.py`. `_pixel_swap_lock` is per-`SqliteStore` and `Instance` is deliberately store-agnostic -- deep-copied into workers, pickled, built by `scripts/` generators with no session behind them -- so wiring it into `unload_pixel_data`/`get_pixel_data` means either a store back-reference on every instance or a second, differently-scoped lock: a new lock-ordering surface on the exact path #280 warned produces self-consistent corruption. What changes observably: every instance now acquires `_pixel_swap_lock`, including non-resident ones that previously returned before it -- a few hundred nanoseconds each, uncontended, unmeasurable at the stress suite's 4,000 instances. Pinned in `tests/test_save_redact_race.py` by a lock **proxy** rather than a thread: the window is too narrow for the #274 pin's `write_frame` seam, so the proxy fires `unload_pixel_data()` once from `__enter__` before delegating, forcing check -> unload -> re-read as three events on one thread. Nothing is scheduled, so the pin is interpreter-independent -- identical on 3.12, 3.14 and 3.14t. Explicitly out of scope, and filed separately: the case where the resident array has *diverged* from the loader's frame before the unload (a `set_pixel_data` after a save). The fixed code then takes the loader arm, records the old frame, and `save_all` still marks the instance persisted -- dropping the new pixels. That is `unload_pixel_data()`'s precondition being wrong (its docstring promises the data "can be restored", which is false once the resident array has diverged), not this TOCTOU.

- **`save_all` appends every sidecar frame *before* it opens its transaction, so the SQLite write lock is held for row upserts alone (#287).** The walk used to open the connection and then, deep inside it, compress and append each dirty instance's pixel frame -- so the write lock was held for as long as the save's whole dirty resident pixel payload took to reach disk, a duration set by pixel bytes and storage throughput rather than by row count. Measured on a 400-instance / 200MB-resident save of incompressible frames: the longest `_get_connection` window was **4.570s before and 0.011s after**, a 415x reduction, while `save_all`'s own wall-time was **4.570s before and 4.575s after** -- the I/O moved, it did not vanish, and that is the honest accounting. Read the 415x with its scope attached: it applies to saves carrying **dirty resident pixel arrays** -- `set_pixel_data` from user code, or the `scripts/` graph builders -- not to "the save path" in general. The real ingest -> redact -> export pipeline is unchanged by this commit (measured: max window 0.120s -> 0.129s, total save wall-time 0.251s -> 0.266s over 600 instances / 302MB), because its saves take `_persist_pixels`' loader arm almost throughout: ingest is lazy, and redaction writes its frames through `persist_pixel_data`, which was already off the transaction. A pipeline run instrumented during review appended **zero** frames in the prepass for six dirty instances, with zero resident arrays at save time. So this is not a pipeline speedup and should not be quoted as one. What a user was exposed to: a healthy concurrent writer -- another thread, another process, the audit-log writer -- blocking for that whole window and, on slow enough storage or a large enough dirty set, exceeding `_SQLITE_BUSY_TIMEOUT_S` and failing with `sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked` from a save that was merely slow. That is the #250 shape arriving without a stuck writer anywhere. The restructure: a new `SqliteStore._prepare_pixel_frames(patients, tally)` runs the whole Patient -> Study -> Series -> Instance walk once, before any connection exists, capturing each dirty instance's revision and calling `_persist_pixels`; it returns an `id(instance) -> (revision, _StoredFrame)` map that `save_all` threads through `_save_patient` to `_save_unsaved_instances`, which no longer computes its own dirty list or revision. **Keyed by object identity, and that is load-bearing rather than incidental.** Position cannot key it, because `series_pk` is unknowable before the transaction and a series can be re-parented between the two walks (`_reparent_series` exists because that happens). Neither can the SOP Instance UID, which is the sharper trap because it looks like the natural key: redaction mutates `sop_instance_uid` **in place** (`Instance.regenerate_uid()`, `Session._apply_redaction_outcomes`), so a UID-keyed lookup misses a renamed instance -- and the miss is not a skipped optimisation but data loss, because `_delete_removed_instances` then computes `stored - {i.sop_instance_uid for i in series.instances}`, finds the instance's OLD row orphaned, and deletes it, cascading `instance_blobs`. The instance ends up absent from the index under either name, with its source file untouched (index loss, not file loss), the prepass's frame orphaned, and `_log_save_summary` still counting it as saved. Nothing repairs that: the instance stays dirty, but `Session.close()` shuts the persistence manager down without enqueuing a save, so a session that closes after the losing save leaves the row permanently gone. `id()` is used rather than the instance itself because `Instance` is a dataclass with the default `eq=True`, so Python sets `__hash__ = None` and it is unhashable -- and its generated `__eq__` compares by VALUE, so a hashable version would have conflated two distinct instances carrying equal fields. `id()` is safe here for the reason it usually is not: `patients` holds the whole graph alive for the duration of `save_all`, and the map does not outlive that call. Pinned by `test_an_instance_renamed_after_the_prepass_is_still_written`, which renames the UID in place from a `_get_connection` wrapper -- the seam that opens after the prepass and before the walk -- and asserts the row exists under the NEW UID and the instance came back clean. Under UID keying that test finds an empty `instances` table. `_build_instance_writes` now does **no I/O of any kind**, and its docstring says so where it previously said the opposite. `persist_pixel_data` is untouched: it already released the lock before `record_blob_ref` and held no connection during its sidecar write.

  **`_SQLITE_BUSY_TIMEOUT_S` is unchanged at 120, deliberately, and the constant is not inlined or removed.** What changed is only its justification. It used to rest on a measurement -- the longest transaction window across the stress pipeline was 1.6s, and 120 is ~75x that. It now rests on something stronger: the window contains no bulk I/O at all, so it is bounded by row count rather than by pixel bytes. The tempting cleanup ("the window is milliseconds now, the timeout is moot") is wrong twice over: `test_packaging_contract.py::test_a_locked_database_errors_inside_one_faulthandler_window` asserts both that the value is under the faulthandler window and, by `inspect.getsource`, that `_get_connection` still *reads the name* -- a re-inlined literal is exactly how `900.0` survived unquestioned -- and the timeout's diagnostic property is about a STUCK writer (another process, a stale WAL), which this change does nothing to prevent.

  **The orphan-frame BOUND widens; orphan frames themselves are not new.** An earlier draft of this entry claimed the rollback orphan was a newly-possible artifact, and that was simply false. `sidecar.write_frame` is a plain `r+b` append under `flock`, and a SQLite rollback has never undone it -- on the previous code a save that rolled back mid-walk after one frame had already been appended left exactly the same orphan (measured during review at 65562 bytes). What this change does is move the bound: from *the frames written before the failure point* to *the whole save's dirty resident payload*, because every frame is now appended before the transaction that might roll back has even opened. Reclamation is unchanged and was verified: the sidecar is append-only, `compact_sidecar` reads `instance_blobs` and rewrites only the frames it names, and an orphan is reclaimed as dead space (85 -> 65647 -> 85 bytes across a save, an orphaning failure, and `compact_sidecar()`). "Acceptable" therefore means **bounded and reclaimable on demand, not self-healing**: `session.compact()` is manual and nothing reclaims automatically.

  Two things widen it further, both filed rather than fixed. First, an orphan can now survive a **successful** commit: the prepass appends a frame for every dirty instance, but the transaction skips instances whose parent upsert returns `None` (a `patient_pk`, `study_pk` or `series_pk` that does not resolve), so their frames are referenced by nothing even though the save committed. Previously `_persist_pixels` ran only for instances that actually reached the write, so an orphan implied a failure. A consequence worth naming rather than quietly fixing: `tally.pixel_frames`/`pixel_bytes` are incremented in the prepass, so `_log_save_summary`'s `Sidecar: N frames (X MB)` counts frames the transaction may never have referenced. That line is left as it is because it reports what was appended to the sidecar, which is exactly what it says and remains true; it is the pairing with the instance count on the same line that can mislead. Second, `Session.save(sync=True)` calls `store_backend.save_all` on the caller's thread without flushing the persistence manager, and nothing excludes the manager's worker, so two saves can overlap -- they partly serialised at the SQLite write lock before, and now the two prepasses run fully in parallel, making the bound per-concurrent-save (#294).

  **The #274 guard is re-checked at the last moment**, because the capture -> commit window is now as long as all of the save's pixel I/O. A redaction landing inside it rebinds the loader to the redacted frame while the prepared map still holds the pristine one; committing that row would leave `instance_blobs` lagging `instances`, and the next compaction would copy the stale frame forward and discard the redacted one -- the resurrection `_build_instance_writes`' own comment warns about. So immediately before emitting the row, `_build_instance_writes` re-evaluates `inst._revision != revision` and, when it trips, substitutes `_StoredFrame(None, None, None, None)` and skips the `instance_blobs` entry. The all-None shape, **not** dropping the instance's row: that is exactly what a raced instance got before this change (the upsert's COALESCE leaves the stored reference alone, and the instance stays dirty because `mark_persisted` receives the stale capture), and dropping the row would be a semantic change beyond a restructure's remit. The re-check is pinned by `test_an_instance_changed_after_the_prepass_keeps_its_stored_frame`, which moves `_revision` from inside `_serialize_item` -- the seam at the top of the same loop iteration, inside the transaction and after the prepass -- and asserts all four halves of the claim: the row is still written, `instances.pixel_offset` is unchanged, `instance_blobs` is unchanged, and the instance is still dirty. Deleting the re-check leaves the rest of the suite green, so without that test the claim in this paragraph would have had nothing enforcing it. The "still written" half is asserted through a core attribute edited before the losing save and read back out of `attributes_json`, not through the row's mere existence -- the row already exists from the previous save, so a presence check cannot tell "written with an all-None frame" from "not written at all", and replacing the substitution with a `continue` that drops the row passes the entire rest of the suite. Capture-before-write is otherwise untouched: the capture simply moves earlier, which is the safe direction -- `mark_persisted` receives an *older* revision, so anything changing in the window leaves the instance dirty rather than falsely clean. It still runs only after `conn.commit()` returns, still per-instance, still never backwards; no setter was added and `mark_subtree_persisted` is not involved. The one visible semantic consequence is that the dirty SET is frozen at prepass time: an instance dirtied after the prepass is not saved this round and stays dirty for the next. That interacts with one unflushed caller, named here rather than left to be discovered: `Session.audit()` does not flush the persistence manager -- `redact()` holds the only `flush()` in `session.py`, added by #274 -- and `record_phi_status()` advances `_revision`, so an asynchronous `save()` followed by `audit()` now lands inside a capture -> commit window as long as all of the save's pixel I/O and trips the late re-check for those instances. The direction is safe (they stay dirty rather than falsely clean), but combined with the frozen dirty set it means `save(); audit(); close()` can close with unsaved changes and no warning, where before this change the window was one loop iteration wide. Filed as #297, not fixed here.

  **This does not make #288 redundant.** `_persist_pixels` still takes `_pixel_swap_lock` and the lock still spans first-read-of-`pixel_array` -> `write_frame` -> loader/hash rebind; only the absence of an open SQLite connection around it changed. `unload_pixel_data()` is indifferent to where the transaction sits, so #288's snapshot fix remains necessary and exactly sufficient -- and its two tests pass **verbatim, unmodified**, after this restructure, which is the evidence. One improvement comes free: with no connection open during the locked section, #280's "no sqlite work under `_pixel_swap_lock`" invariant is now structurally guaranteed rather than merely observed. Pinned by `tests/test_save_all_contract.py::test_no_sidecar_frame_is_written_while_the_database_transaction_is_open`, which is structural rather than timing-based -- a depth counter on `_get_connection` (a counter, not a flag: `record_blob_ref` and `save_vertical_attributes` are handed the open connection) and a recorder on `sidecar.write_frame` -- and asserts every frame was written at depth 0, plus that EXACTLY four frames were written (2 patients x 2 instances) so that neither a fixture regression nor a partial hoist whose remaining frames happened to dedup can make it vacuously green. Before the restructure the depth list read `[1, 1, 1, 1]`. Also documented, not fixed: `session.compact()` rewires every loader outside `_pixel_swap_lock` and is safe only by the convention that it leads with `save(sync=True)` and is not run concurrently with redaction; that precondition is now stated in its docstring and filed as a follow-up.

- **The SQLite busy timeout is a named, measured constant -- `persistence._SQLITE_BUSY_TIMEOUT_S = 120` -- instead of a bare `timeout=900.0` on every file connection (#250).** The 900 bought nothing and cost the diagnosis: a writer that cannot get the lock in two minutes is not going to get it at second 890 (the #250 stalls proved it -- the forked child errored at exactly 900s every time), and the old value held each hit open long enough that CI's job cap killed the run mid-stall as "cancelled", with no failing test named, three times. The invariant, pinned by `test_packaging_contract.py` alongside the existing timeout-inequality family: a lock that will not clear surfaces as `sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked` *inside* one faulthandler window (300s), where the dump shows a thread still waiting with a stack -- never as a stall for an outer timeout to kill. The floor is measured, not guessed: the longest single transaction window observed across the full stress-test pipeline (4,000 instances, ~2GB of pixel data; the maximum was the persistence worker's `save_all` transaction) was 1.6s, so 120s is ~75x that -- room for a save holding an order of magnitude more resident dirty pixel data than the benchmark's. No environment variable -- one spelling, and tests monkeypatch the constant. Deliberately unfixed here and noted for a follow-up: moving sidecar writes outside the transaction window would dissolve the tension that makes any large number tempting.

- **Every pool worker can now dump its own stack when it stalls: `ISOCENTER_WORKER_FAULTHANDLER=1` arms `faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(240, exit=False)` inside each worker process at start (#250).** Occurrence four of #250 was diagnosed by pytest's `faulthandler_timeout` -- and the dump showed every *parent* thread idle, because faulthandler cannot see into pool children and the 900-second lock holder was a child. The initializer (`parallel._worker_init`, module scope because it must pickle; resolved once by `parallel.resolve_worker_initializer`, which also absorbs the old `_gc_off` so the recycling pool, the per-call executor and `Session._executor` cannot drift on what a worker's first act is) delivers the missing half: the next child-side stall of any mechanism lands in the log as the child's own traceback, before the parent's 300s window closes and long before any timeout kills the run. `exit=False` is load-bearing -- the dump is diagnosis, and a slow-but-healthy worker finishes its task rather than being killed by its own instrumentation. Off unless the variable is set; only CI's tests.yml sets it, so production users never pay for it.

- **Both prose claims of the #198 disclosure union are now pinned by test; no behaviour changes (#203).** Verifying #198 by mutation found the union correct and measured, but two sentences it commits to in its docstring, commit message and CHANGELOG entry had nothing enforcing them -- each survived a hand-applied mutation against the four test files that cover the path. First, "an instance the worker wrote is delivered even if the file has since been removed": `delivered = set(written_uids)` mutated to `set()` stayed green, and this half stopped being redundant the moment #199 removed the readable partial a late failure used to leave -- the filesystem half no longer covers the worker's answer, so a later edit deleting the wrong half of the union was one plausible simplification away. `test_an_instance_the_worker_wrote_is_disclosed_even_if_its_file_is_gone` calls `_report_recoverable_identities` directly with nothing on disk and asserts the "2 of 2" row. Second, "a clean export does no filesystem work here and a failed one does one call per failure": dropping the `not in delivered` short-circuit changes zero observable behaviour and turns a 100k-instance clean export into 100k stats on whatever filesystem the cohort leaves through. A parent-process `os.path.exists` spy pins the counts the issue measured -- zero calls on planned paths for a clean export, exactly one, on the failed instance's named path, for a single late failure. The zero is guarded against vacuity twice: the clean test ends by proving the spy was live, and the exact-one count in the failure twin is the backstop a wrong path filter cannot fake. All three tests were shown red under their hand-applied mutations and green on the real code. One side effect worth naming: the new tests import `isocenter.io_handlers` (for `ExportContext`), which makes `test_check_reversibility.py` the first file to pay the #140 obligation below -- it is added to the io_handlers `TARGETS` entry and CLAUDE.md's row in the same change, exactly the way the mapping guard forces. (#203)

- **`io_handlers.py` is a mutation-probe target, and every `TARGETS` entry now carries its own sampling budget (#140).** The largest module in the de-identification pipeline -- 380 mutation sites today, against crypto's 6, privacy's 71 and remediation's 71 -- was the one module the probe never measured, which the probe's own docstring calls "unmeasured, not clean". The entry lists every test file that imports `isocenter.io_handlers` (53 at the time of the measurement) plus exactly two curated extras with *measured* kills (`test_redaction_export.py` via the `apply_redaction_to_array` call, `test_reversibility.py` via the `(0400,0510)` write); no file is listed on faith, because every listed file is pure runtime for every sampled mutant and an unearned entry is how the #106 phantom-gap shape returns. `TARGETS` values become `(tests, budget)` tuples because the old single global budget did two jobs at once: raising io_handlers' sampling density to stride 2 needs `budget >= 190`, which would simultaneously drive privacy and remediation to stride 1 -- you could not buy density in the unmeasured module without re-paying for two already-measured ones. The positional CLI budget keeps its meaning as a one-run override for every module (`python -m scripts.mutation_probe 10` stays the cheap pass). What it costs, stated because it is the real change to a contributor's day: the default run goes from ~8 minutes to ~57 (a surviving io_handlers mutant pays the whole 55-file list, ~165s, where a kill exits at the first red test, ~45s), the probe runs in no CI workflow, and every future test file that imports `io_handlers` fails `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py` until it is added to both `TARGETS` and CLAUDE.md's table -- a deliberate recurring cost, since the drifted copy of that mapping is the defect the guard exists for. The first honest reading (budget 30): control PASS, killed 21/32, survived 11/32 -- **a sample from a 12.6%-density stride, not a score**; the survivors are classified and filed as follow-up issues rather than encoded anywhere the stride's next reshuffle would falsify. `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py` gains two tests pinning that `main()` samples each module at its own budget and that the CLI override reaches every module -- a `(tests, budget)` tuple whose budget nobody reads is decoration. (#140)

- **The mutation probe's freshness guard now inspects the bytecode cache of the interpreter that runs the tests, and a probe that cannot ask that interpreter refuses to run (#201).** `assert_fresh()` located the `.pyc` to inspect with the parent's `importlib.util.cache_from_source`, which builds the filename from the *launching* interpreter's `sys.implementation.cache_tag` -- while `PYTEST` hardcodes `.venv/bin/python`. Under the documented invocation (`python -m scripts.mutation_probe`) on a pyenv machine those are routinely different interpreters (a 3.12 shim beside a 3.14 venv, reproduced), so the guard opened `privacy.cpython-312.pyc`, a file the test subprocess never reads or writes, found nothing there, and returned. It degraded in one direction only -- never a false abort, always a false pass -- so nothing about running the probe would ever reveal the check had been off the whole time; the #174 entry above records the hole this closes. Now `subprocess_cache_path(path)` asks `PYTEST[0]` itself to name its cache entry, memoized per source path -- the full path rather than just the tag, because the subprocess honours `PYTHONPYCACHEPREFIX` and a hand-assembled `__pycache__` name looks in the wrong directory under a cache prefix -- and `assert_fresh(path, cache)` takes that answer as a **required second parameter with no default**, because a `cache_from_source` default is precisely the spelling that resurrects parent-tag behaviour without anyone choosing it. A `PYTEST[0]` that is missing -- a bare worktree without its `.venv`, which used to die with a raw `FileNotFoundError` out of the first subprocess call -- or that cannot answer is now a `SystemExit` naming the interpreter: the probe prefers aborting over reporting. The #174 fix itself never depended on which interpreter launched the probe (`PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE` is inherited either way); only the guard went quiet. `tests/test_mutation_probe_freshness.py` pins the derivation with a stub interpreter whose answer no local `cache_from_source` call could produce, pins that the guard opens the file it is handed rather than the parent-tag one, pins both refusal arms, and its wiring test asserts every guard call -- the control included -- received the subprocess's answer. The seven pre-existing guard tests now pass the cache path explicitly, each declaring whose cache it fabricates. (#201)

- **The eager ingest's acceptance of a preamble-less file is pinned by test; no behaviour changes (#281).** `ingest_worker`'s one read is `pydicom.dcmread(fp, stop_before_pixels=False, force=True)`, and `force=True -> force=False` survived the whole 55-file io_handlers list in #140's first honest run (budget 30 over 380 sites, killed 21/32, survived 11/32). `force=True` is what lets ingest accept a file with no 128-byte preamble and no `DICM` magic -- raw exports and some vendor dumps are written that way -- and nothing in the suite ingested one, so the acceptance was a behaviour the suite could not see: a dataset that ingested yesterday would start raising `InvalidDicomError` file by file, each counted in `IngestSummary.failures` with an `ERROR` audit row (#211), with every listed test green throughout. `tests/test_io.py::test_a_file_with_no_preamble_is_accepted_by_the_eager_ingest` writes a plain `Dataset` rather than a `FileDataset` -- which is what makes the file header-less -- via `dcmwrite(..., implicit_vr=True, little_endian=True, enforce_file_format=False)`, proves the fixture is genuinely unreadable without force, then puts it through `DicomImporter.import_files` into a bare `DicomStore` and asserts `failures == []`, `ingested == 1`, and that the SOP Instance UID the fixture wrote is the one in the graph. Two traps ride as fixture comments: the unreadable-without-force assertion is load-bearing (a fixture that quietly regained its preamble would pass under the mutant too), and `SOPClassUID` is set on the dataset so `ingest_worker`'s `"MediaStorageSOPClassUID" in ds.file_meta` fallback short-circuits -- with force and no header that attribute is an *empty* `FileMetaDataset` -- which keeps the test about the preamble alone. There is exactly one ingest path, so `import_files` **is** the eager arm; the only other `dcmread` on a source file, `Instance.get_pixel_data()`, is the lazy pixel load, has no `force=True` at all, and is filed as #289 -- a production gap rather than a test one -- instead of being fixed here. Shown red under the hand-applied mutation (the summary names the path and the missing-header reason) and green on the real code. **This and the two entries below land in test files already listed for `io_handlers.py` -- `tests/test_io.py` and `tests/test_private_tag_export.py` are both in the `mutation_probe.TARGETS` entry and CLAUDE.md's row -- so neither needs an edit; #203 named that obligation in the change where it incurred it, so its absence here is measured rather than assumed, with `tests/test_mutation_probe_targets.py` run after each commit.** (#281)

- **A series carrying only one of manufacturer/model keeps its `Equipment`, and that is now pinned by test; no behaviour changes (#282).** `import_files`' linkage arm builds equipment under `if meta['man'] or meta['model']`, and `or -> and` survived the whole 55-file io_handlers list in the same #140 run. Only the both-present and neither-present cases were exercised, and partial equipment metadata is routine in the wild: under the mutant `series.equipment is None` for exactly those series, silently, so the equipment inventory and every equipment-keyed rule under-report with nothing anywhere saying they did. `tests/test_io.py::test_a_series_with_a_manufacturer_and_no_model_still_gets_its_equipment` ingests one minimal file through `DicomImporter.import_files` into a bare `DicomStore` and asserts **both** `Equipment` fields rather than just that it exists -- `is not None` alone would survive an argument swap in the construction. It is parametrized over both directions of the partial case to document the issue's "or the reverse"; either direction kills the mutant alone, so that buys documentation and not extra kill power, which the docstring says. The new `_write_minimal` fixture adds only the fields it is passed: `ingest_worker` reads `ds.get("ManufacturerModelName", "")`, so "the model is absent" has to mean the element is missing from the file, not present and empty -- an empty element exercises pydicom's empty-value handling instead of the default the `or` guards. Shown red under the hand-applied mutation (`assert None is not None`, both directions) and green on the real code. The same predicate exists twice more, in `persistence.py`'s two hydration paths; those copies are out of scope here and the one-spelling question is filed as #290. (#282)

- **The `bool` arm of private-tag VR inference is asserted on export, and its comment stops claiming a distinction the code does not make (#283).** `return 'LO', str(value)` -> `return None` in `DicomExporter._fallback_encoding`'s `bool` arm survived the whole 55-file io_handlers list in the same #140 run: no test exported an instance carrying a bool-valued private tag, so the VR, the `"True"` spelling and the arm existing at all were prose. Two tests in `tests/test_private_tag_export.py` close that, and both kill the named mutant. `test_a_bool_private_value_is_written_as_the_LO_string_True` calls `DicomExporter._merge` -- the export merge itself, the one `_export_instance_worker` runs -- and asserts `("LO", "True")` on the element, plus `_fallback_encoding(False) == ('LO', 'False')`, so what is pinned is the arm's `str(value)` rather than a hardcoded "True"; under the mutant no VR fits, `_merge` files the tag as data loss, no element is written and the subscript raises `KeyError`. `test_a_bool_private_value_round_trips_as_True_not_1` takes the full ingest -> export -> re-read path. Nothing in a DICOM file ever yields a Python `bool` -- only `set_attr` produces one -- so `_roundtrip` gains a defaulted `mutate=None` callback, called with the session after `ingest()` and before `export()` and documented in the helper as the only way to put a value into the graph that no source file can carry; every existing caller is unaffected. Its kill was measured, not assumed: the export worker reads the in-memory graph, so `_fallback_encoding` sees a genuine `bool` and not the EAV tier's flattened text, and the element is simply absent under the mutant. The VR is deliberately *not* asserted in the round-trip, and the reason is worth stating precisely because the obvious one is wrong: it is **not** that the VR would be environment-dependent. `_write_src` carries pixels and `_export_dicom` defaults to `use_compression=True`, so that path always takes the J2K branch, the file meta is `1.2.840.10008.1.2.4.90`, and the element reads back `VR='LO'` deterministically -- nor could codec availability make it *selectively* flaky, since a missing J2K codec raises in `_compress_j2k` and fails the whole export, taking every test in the file with it. The real reason is narrower: a VR assertion there adds no kill power against this mutation and would couple the test to the `use_compression` default, so a change to that default would fail a test about a bool's value. The VR is pinned in the first test instead, at the element, with no export defaults in the way; that reasoning is a comment in the second.

  **The measurement, stated rather than softened.** `_fallback_encoding(True) == ('LO','True')`, `_fallback_encoding(1) == ('LO','1')`, `str(True)` is already `'True'`, and `isinstance(True, int)` is True -- so the `bool` arm returns *exactly* what the `int`/`float` arm below it would, and nothing in the code as it stands produces `"1"` for a bool. The named mutation is killable and both tests kill it; **deleting the arm entirely is an equivalent mutant that no test can kill**, which is the probe docstring's own warning that some mutants are equivalent and each one has to be read before it is acted on. The arm is kept anyway, and the only production change is its comment, which previously asserted a distinction the code does not make (`"True"` is a better round-trip than `"1"`). It now says what is true: the arm exists as a pre-placed guard for #154 (open, this milestone), whose subject is private tags ceasing to collapse to `LO` -- the moment the numeric arm emits a numeric VR, a bool falling through to it becomes `1`, and ordering above `int` is the whole mechanism, since `bool` is an `int` subclass. That matters because under this project's one-spelling rule a future reader is entitled to delete a redundant arm, and the old sentence was precisely the justification they would have used. (#283)

## [0.9.1] - 2026-09-02

Thirty-seven issues under one heading, which is the milestone's own name:
the session reported success while wrong. A verification pass attested
pixel coverage the zones did not have; the compliance report counted an
export that failed, or missed one that succeeded, or described the
previous run; audit rows were dropped, raced, or filed against files that
were never written; the grade asked whether the audit log had heard
*anything* rather than what this session had actually done; and exports
parsed cleanly while carrying the wrong multiplicity, an illegal date, a
nonconformant float element, or one twelfth of a waveform.

Almost nothing here is a new capability. The work is making every
sentence the library writes about its own output true -- and where a
true sentence cannot be written, refusing loudly instead of succeeding
quietly. Stores damaged by earlier releases are detected at open
(`check_pixel_geometry`), repairable on request (`redact(force=True)`,
`reconcile_private_tags()`), or healed at hydration (hollow multiplex
items); the write path stopped leaving readable partial files; and the
report finally comes last in the documented order, where it can see what
the export did.

### Breaking

- **A multi-sample float pixel export is refused: an instance whose pixels are float32/float64 and whose geometry resolves to `SamplesPerPixel > 1` now fails with `RuntimeError: Refusing to write <path>: ... there is no conformant way to write a multi-sample float pixel element` instead of writing a file (#222).** The refusal takes exactly the treatment a GUESSED geometry gets (#215): `ExportOutcome(ok=False)`, an `ERROR` audit row naming the instance and the reason, a `REVIEW_REQUIRED` grade -- and on `write_tree()`, the raised `RuntimeError` that path has always used for a failed instance. The newly-failing export is the one that used to succeed: `ingest -> export` of a Parametric Map declaring three samples (any declared Photometric Interpretation, or none) wrote `(7fe0,0008)` with descriptors no conformant reader is required to accept. PS3.3 C.7.6.24 and C.7.6.25 both fix Photometric Interpretation at `MONOCHROME2` (Type 1, enumerated), C.7.6.3.1.2 permits `MONOCHROME2` only at `SamplesPerPixel = 1`, and Planar Configuration appears in neither module's attribute table -- so every value the exporter could write was barred, and the old behaviour picked between nonconformances (`RGB` plus a `PlanarConfiguration` the IOD does not define, or a relabelled declaration) while the run graded `PASS`. The narrow #224 guard -- pass a declared monochrome value through on the float path -- is superseded and removed: it stopped the exporter inventing a value but still shipped a file both modules bar. The shared resolvers (`resolve_photometric_interpretation`, `planar_configuration_default`) are untouched: on the integer path `RGB` and `PlanarConfiguration = 0` are exactly right, and a conformant single-sample float instance exports byte-identically to before. The float16 arm is deliberately outside the refusal -- it writes no pixel element, so its multi-sample shape keeps its existing `DATA_LOSS` route.

- **`Study.study_date` refuses a `datetime`: assigning one -- through the constructor or later -- now raises `TypeError: Study.study_date holds a date, not a datetime: call .date() on it, and put the time of day in Study Time (0008,0030) -- Study.study_time -- instead.` (#188).** A `datetime` used to be accepted, stored as `isoformat()` text (`'2024-01-15T10:30:00'`), and *not* restored by hydration -- `date.fromisoformat` rejects the `'T'` -- so it came back a `str` and walked into #171's original defect: stamped into (0008,0020) unconverted, pydicom warns and writes it anyway, and the file leaves with a ten-plus-character value in a VR PS3.5 Table 6.2-1 fixes at eight digits. No library path produces a `datetime` here (ingest parses (0008,0020) with `.date()`), so the only calls this breaks are hand-set fields -- which were exactly the calls that produced illegal exports. The old behaviour was wrong because it was the worst of the three options: not the time-of-day kept (impossible in a DA element), not a refusal, but a value that *looked* stored and surfaced as a nonconformant file two stages later with nothing audited in between. Truncating to `.date()` at the boundary was considered and rejected: silently discarding a time-of-day the caller supplied is the same quiet lossy normalisation #60 forbids for unreadable dates, and the discarded half has its own element to go to. The check lives on the field's one write boundary (`Study.__setattr__`, which the dataclass `__init__` also routes through), so there is no second spelling to keep in step.

- **`session.reconcile_private_tags()`: the opt-in repair for a store de-identified before the #158 upgrade (#172).** #158 wired the `instance_attributes` tier into hydration -- the fix that makes `remove_private_tags: false` survive a reload -- and that made an old defect reachable: the pre-#158 writer did not mirror deletions, so a session that ran `remove_private_tags: true`, anonymized and saved left the entire stripped vendor block sitting in the tier, inert because nothing read it. The first open of such a store after upgrading resurrects those rows onto the graph, and an export taken from that session carries private tags the site deleted. The resurrection itself stays, deliberately: a stale row and a legitimate one are byte-identical, the tier holds values rather than tombstones, and an automatic migration at open would either drop every site's vendor block (undoing #158) or keep every stripped one -- which a store needs depends on what the site ran, which the site knows and the library does not. `tests/test_private_tag_reload.py` pins both halves: the resurrection as behaviour, and that nothing reconciles automatically.

  The call reads the core `attributes_json` as the complete answer -- which, for a pre-#158 store, it provably was: nothing consulted the tier, so the core is exactly what every pre-upgrade session saw, scanned and exported. It deletes every tier row whose tag is absent from its instance's core attributes, removes the same tags from the live graph (undoing the resurrection this session's open performed, with direct dict mutation so nothing reads as unsaved and stored PHI statuses survive), writes one `RECONCILE_PRIVATE` audit row per affected instance -- the repair belongs in the compliance trail, and the new action type lands in the report's Processing Audit table without moving the grade, since nothing was lost that a pre-upgrade export ever carried -- and returns the number of rows deleted. **The cost is stated on the method and pinned by test:** for a store that legitimately keeps its vendor block, the tier IS the private data, held out of the core by design, and this call deletes all of it. Same grain as `redact(force=True)`: the repair exists in the API, nothing changes silently, and the caller is choosing. A site unsure of its history re-runs the privacy pipeline instead -- since #158 the writer mirrors deletions, so anonymize + save heals the tier without trusting the core. No schema-version stamp decides this automatically: a stamp records which code wrote the store, not what the site ran, which is the half that matters here -- and the version-stamped attestation is already filed to be decided once for #168, #172 and #237 together (see #237's entry below). (#172)

- **`session.export(verify_readback=True)` re-reads every file it writes before delivering it (#209).** The export has always reported success on "`save_as` did not raise", which is a weaker claim than the one the compliance report presents: that a file exists that decodes to what was meant. #186, #205 and #170 were all files written, counted as successes and graded `PASS` that a single re-read would have caught -- one that pydicom refuses outright, two that decode cleanly and describe a different image than the source. Fixing those three removed those three causes; this is the check that catches the next one.

  What it promises: with the option on, each worker runs `pydicom.dcmread` on the file it just wrote and compares `Rows`, `Columns`, `SamplesPerPixel`, `NumberOfFrames` and `BitsAllocated` against the dataset it serialized. An unreadable file or any mismatch fails that instance's export through the same channel a write that raised takes (#181): an `ERROR` audit row naming the descriptor and both values, exclusion from `Instances Written`, and a `REVIEW_REQUIRED` grade. The check runs against the temporary file, before the rename that publishes it (#199), so a file that fails verification is never delivered at all -- there is no state in which the folder holds a file the report disowned. The baseline is deliberately the serialized dataset and not `inst.attributes`: the worker *corrects* stale declared descriptors on the way out (#186, #216), so the raw attributes are the one baseline guaranteed to disagree with a correctly written file, and comparing against them would fail every export those fixes made right. What it costs: a second parse per instance, in the worker, so the cost parallelizes with the export -- which is why it is opt-in and off by default; a run that does not pass it behaves byte-for-byte as before, verified with a booby-trapped `dcmread` in `tests/test_export_readback.py`. It does not decode pixels: comparing pixel values catches everything and costs a full decompression per file, and `verify()` remains the deeper post-export step. One spelling, no alias, documented on `_export_dicom` where the export options live. (#209)

- **A broken same-page anchor is a test failure now.** The five Quick Reference links in `docs/configuration.md` pointed at headings that had been renamed and were dead for months (#152); every gate that ran on them was green throughout. `mkdocs build --strict` is not the missing gate and cannot be made into one -- strict mode fails on a broken *file* reference, and a `#fragment` that resolves to nothing is not an error to it. So there was no zero-code option: `tests/test_doc_anchors.py` renders every root and site markdown page with Python-Markdown and asserts that each fragment link lands on an id the renderer actually emits. Run against `4fa1635`, the commit before the fix, it reports exactly those five; against `main` it reports none. 22 pages, 8 fragment links, ~115ms.

  **It lives in `tests/`, next to the two checks of the same shape.** `test_api_coherence.py` and `test_packaging_contract.py` both pin defects that would look identical from the outside and that nothing else would report, and both run on every PR rather than on a path filter. A `docs.yml` home would have been cheaper -- that workflow is already filtered to `docs/**` and `mkdocs.yml` -- and it would have graded the docs only on pushes to `main`, after review, on a workflow that deploys straight to the live site. A code-only PR that renames a heading in a docstring-generated page would pass its gate and break the site on merge. The check costs ~115ms on a suite that takes four minutes.

  **The renderer is the real one, deliberately.** A stdlib-only slugifier was written and run during the #152 review and reproduced both counts exactly -- and its emphasis-stripper ate underscores, producing `addrule` where Python-Markdown produces `add_rule`. Neither name was a link target, so it reached the right verdict for the wrong reason: a second answer to "what is the slug" that can disagree with the real one and did. That is the argument CLAUDE.md already makes about the retired `text_index`, so `markdown>=3.4` is declared in the `tests` extra (it arrives transitively with the `docs` extra via mkdocs, but the suite runs on `.[tests,ocr]`). It is a test-only import in `tests/`, which `test_packaging_contract.py` does not scan -- that test walks `isocenter/` -- so it is not an `install_requires` obligation.

  Two things the check gets right because #152 got them wrong first. It parses **rendered HTML rather than raw markdown**: a link whose *text* wraps across two source lines is invisible to a line-based regex, and `docs/architecture.md:95` is such a link. And it **reads `mkdocs.yml`'s `markdown_extensions` rather than assuming them**: today there is no `toc` slugify override and no `pymdownx.slugs`, so the default slug applies, but an extension this check does not recognise -- or a `toc` option that can replace the slug function -- stops the test with a message rather than being graded against slugs the site never emits. `pymdownx.snippets` is the one deferral: an anchor inside a `--8<--` include resolves against the including page, there are zero includes in the tree today, and the deferral is written down in the module docstring rather than left to be discovered. (#162)

### Changed

- **Whether an unrouted binary value survives ingest is now decided by its size, not its wire VR: one documented constant, `io_handlers.BINARY_RETENTION_MAX_BYTES` (65534), applies to `OB`/`OW`/`OF`/`OD`/`OL` and `UN` alike (#151).** The old gate dropped `BINARY_VRS` and kept `UN` ("for safety, usually small private tags"), and under Implicit VR every private element *is* `UN` -- so the identical bytes from the identical source were dropped with a `DATA_LOSS` row when the sender chose Explicit VR and silently retained, exported, and unreported when it chose Implicit. De-identification outcome, memory footprint and the compliance grade all tracked the transfer syntax rather than the data: `remove_private_tags=False` was honoured in one syntax and unhonourable in the other, "usually small" put megabyte implicit-VR blobs resident in the graph (and, base64-encoded, in `attributes_json`), and an implicit-VR ingest could grade `PASS` where its explicit twin graded `REVIEW_REQUIRED`. Both directions change. A value at or below the threshold is retained on the graph whatever its wire VR -- so a small explicit-VR `OB` vendor block, Overlay Data, or palette LUT that used to vanish with a loss row now survives ingest, reaches the export, and files nothing (this closes the transfer-syntax divergence and the small-blob half of #125's documented limitation; a suite pin that asserted the old drop had to flip, which is the visibility that pin existed to provide). A value above it is dropped with the existing `DATA_LOSS` treatment whatever its wire VR -- so a large implicit-VR `UN` blob now files the loss it silently kept before, scoped by the same parity rule as every other loss row (#146). The threshold is PS3.5's own boundary, not an invented one: 65534 is the largest length a 16-bit explicit-VR length field can carry (§7.1.2), and §6.2.2 obliges every conformant explicit-VR encoder to relabel anything longer as `UN` -- the last size at which the wire itself still preserves an element's identity across syntaxes, and a per-element cap that keeps the 100GB+ memory guarantee intact. Exempt on purpose: routed elements (pixels and waveform samples go to the sidecar, the float pixel pair rides the export re-read -- none of that moves), and recovered implicit-VR private *sequences* (#167), which are structure rather than bulk and whose encoded length is the one place the two syntaxes genuinely differ. Retained bytes round-trip the store: `_split_core_and_private` already keeps every `bytes` value in the JSON tier, whose encoder restores them byte-exact on reload.

- **A session that discarded a waveform multiplex group now grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED`; the same ingest read `PASS` before.** No exception changes and no API moves -- what changes is the verdict on the report a compliance reader signs. Since #146 a `DATA_LOSS` row's grade is decided by the scope its emitter recorded: `PRIVATE` costs the run its `PASS`, `STANDARD` does not, because standard-group losses -- overlays, palette LUTs -- are dropped on most of the world's imaging and flipping the grade on them would turn it into noise. The multiplex discard (`ingest_worker` keeps Waveform Sequence item 0 and drops the rest, #36) was scoped `STANDARD` because Waveform Sequence `(5400,0100)` is an even group -- and that is the case the parity proxy was wrong for: an overlay is an annotation layer with a defined home elsewhere, a discarded multiplex group is **acquired signal that was in the source file and is not in the export**. A 12-lead ECG stored as multiple multiplex groups came out the far side holding group 0 under a report that said `PASS`.

  The fix is one scope, written where #146 put the classification: the emitter now files this loss as `SIGNAL`, a third value alongside `PRIVATE` and `STANDARD`, and `generate_report` grades by membership in `GRADED_LOSS_SCOPES = {PRIVATE, SIGNAL}` instead of naming `PRIVATE` itself. The report still never re-derives a scope from prose, and the grading test is deliberately not widened to `STANDARD` -- that would take every overlay with it. Rows written by earlier releases keep their stored `STANDARD` scope and keep grading `PASS`: the scope is the emitter's classification at the time of the loss, and rewriting history by re-grading old rows is exactly the re-derivation #146 refused. Two neighbouring losses deliberately stay `STANDARD`: the export-side "Waveform Sequence present but no samples" row (nothing was discarded by this pipeline -- the source never carried samples) and the WFDB bridge's dropped-annotation row (the samples those marks described already cost the run its `PASS` via the ingest-side row; grading the bookkeeping too would double-charge one loss under two entries). Multi-rate support -- the fix that would make the loss stop existing -- remains open and is not attempted here. (#150)

- **`session.redact()` returns a smaller number than it used to, and the old number was wrong.** It counts instances whose pixels a configured zone was actually applied to. Previously it also counted an instance a rule matched and applied **nothing** to -- the other skips (already redacted under this configuration, no pixel data, a worker that failed) were already excluded, so this one shape was the whole of the discrepancy: a call that redacted nothing at all returned `1` and printed "Redaction complete: 1 of 1 images updated". No exception is raised where none was before -- a previously-working `assert session.redact() == n` over a configuration one of whose rules matches an image that none of its zones fits now sees a smaller `n`, and the shortfall warning explains it. For a configuration every one of whose zones lands, nothing changes. There is deliberately no second count and no `applied_including_skipped`: pre-1.0 this project deletes a spelling rather than doubling it, and "how many images did you change" is the question the sentence a user reads is asking.

  **What the run used to write onto an untouched image.** Measured on `84113ab`, identically on 3.12.13 and 3.14.7t: one 32x32 image, one zone `[100,108,100,108]` entirely past the edge, saved and reopened so the array arrives read-only through `SidecarPixelLoader`. `applied: 1`, pixels untouched at `204800` -- and `(0028,0301)`, `(0008,0008)`, `(0008,2111)` and `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` all **created, with the value `None`**. #235 reported that no redaction hash was written; the key is written, with `None`, which is not a second seal -- the skip check is `current_hash == config_hash` and `None` never equals a hex digest -- but the other three are not harmless. `save()` puts four `null`s into `instances.attributes_json`, and `export()` writes `BurnedInAnnotation` (`CS`), `ImageType` (`CS`) and `DerivationDescription` (`ST`) into the file as **zero-length elements**. BurnedInAnnotation's enumerated values are `YES` and `NO` (PS3.3 C.7.6.1.1.6); zero length is neither, and "absent" and "present but unanswered" are not the same claim to a compliance reader.

  **It also crashed the safety scan it fed, which #235 did not predict.** For an instance whose source carried `(0028,0301) = YES` but no `(0008,0008)` -- the shape most worth getting right, since the scanner has already said identifiers are drawn into the pixels -- the null `ImageType` write made `scan_burned_in_annotations` read `inst.attributes.get("0008,0008", [])` and receive the stored `None` rather than the default, then iterate it. `redact()` raised `TypeError: 'NoneType' object is not iterable` from `services.py`, after the mutations were applied and before the console summary. Measured on `84113ab`, both executor levers. Fixed by not writing the null, not by hardening the reader: a guard at the read site would make the scan survive a value the graph should never hold. `(0028,0301) = YES` on an instance nothing was applied to was checked separately and **never** downgraded -- the mutation carried the worker's own value, so the `None` only ever appeared where the element was absent. That hypothesis is recorded because it is the one a reader would assume, and it is false.

  **One gate, and it is the deletion of a divergence rather than a new mechanism.** `execute_redaction_task` now builds its mutation dict **inside** `if modified:` and otherwise returns `mutation=None` -- the shape it already used for its two other legitimate skips, "already redacted under this configuration" and "no pixel data". "No configured zone landed inside the image" is a legitimate skip of exactly that kind and now says so in the same vocabulary. Everything derives from that one `if`: the count, the four attributes, the Derivation Code Sequence, the hash, and the identity. `redact_machine_instances` -- the serial path -- **never had the defect**: it builds no mutation, and when `modified` is false it does nothing. So the parallel path was not missing a gate, it had a divergence from the serial one, and the parallel path is the one that moved. #235 was deferred out of #229 precisely to avoid adding a second gate answering the same question as #228's `sop_uid != original_sop_uid`; there is now one.

  **#228's gate is deleted, and its entry below is corrected in place rather than left to disagree.** With the mutation built only inside the gate, `new_uid != sop` is provably always true where it is evaluated. `_apply_redaction_outcomes` reads `if new_uid:` -- a `None`-safety guard on a `dict.get`. The strongest objection is that #228's comments told the next reader not to do this, so nothing at the assignment site now guards a re-widened mutation construction. **Measured, and the objection resolves better than expected**: re-widening the construction while keeping `if new_uid:` was applied to this tree and the suite re-run. Four new tests go red (`applied == 0`, the four absent attributes, the absent exported elements, the risk-scan crash) -- and so does `test_an_instance_nothing_was_applied_to_keeps_its_identity`, on both legs, on `assert inst.file_path == str(stale)`. The two UID assignments in that block become no-ops under a re-widened mutation; `instance.file_path = None`, the third statement beside them, does not. So the selectivity is not merely relocated, it is wider than before, and it is stated in that test's docstring with the measurement.

  **The boundary this does not move, pinned so nobody closes it by accident.** The gate is "a zone was applied", not "the bytes changed". A zone that is in bounds but selects zero pixels -- `[[0, 0, 20, 20]]` on a 32x32 image -- was, at this change, counted, renamed and fully attested with no pixel touched, because `apply_redaction_to_array` sets `modified = True` after the assignment without comparing. Redefining `modified` as "the bytes changed" is the fix that would close it and it is refused: a correct re-run of an already-redacted instance changes nothing either, so it would never re-earn its attestation, and the `current_hash == config_hash` skip -- the thing that keeps a second `redact()` off every pixel of a 100GB store -- would stop working. It also changes the contract of the most heavily commented function in `services.py`, whose bool return is load-bearing for #66, #186, #205 and #217. Filed as #244 -- and closed in this same cycle at zone *validation* rather than at the gate (see that entry above); this paragraph is corrected in place rather than left describing a shape that no longer exists, the way #228's entry was.

  **An off-image redaction stops appending pixel bytes nothing references.** Both paths' `finally` called `persist_pixel_data` whenever the task had not *failed*, and that function does not deduplicate -- it hashes, writes a frame and re-points the loader every time it is handed a resident array. Measured on `84113ab`: the sidecar grew `17 -> 34` bytes on an off-image run, on the parallel path under both levers **and on the serial path**, where `instance_blobs` was left pointing at the second copy so the first was the orphan. Both `finally` blocks now carry `modified and not failed`; #213's argument for the `not failed` half is untouched and its comment stays, this narrows the condition rather than replacing it. `unload_pixel_data()` stays unconditional -- **and one shape changes because of that, named rather than left to be found.** An instance with neither a `_pixel_loader` nor a `file_path` -- a graph built in memory and redacted in the same session, never saved -- used to have a loader attached as a *side effect* of the unconditional persist, after which `unload_pixel_data()` succeeded and the array was freed. Measured on `84113ab`: after an off-image `redact()` such an instance came back with a `SidecarPixelLoader` and `pixel_array is None`; on this commit it keeps `_pixel_loader is None` and its array stays resident, and `unload_pixel_data()` declines, which is that method's documented refusal to make a silent discard. Nothing is lost and nothing is incorrect -- the pixels are the ones the caller put there and `save()` still writes them -- but a redaction that changed nothing no longer quietly writes an unsaved in-memory array to the sidecar on its way past, and no longer frees it. That side effect was never the persist's purpose; a `redact()` that applied nothing is not a `save()`. The **in-bounds** double-persist is a separate defect and is *not* fixed here: `execute_redaction_task` persists once inside `if modified:` -- the call that supplies `mutation["pixel_loader"]`, which is why it cannot move -- and once again in the `finally`, so 23 of 46 appended bytes are unreachable. `compact()` reclaims them, so it is dead space rather than a leak. Filed separately, with a comment at the inner call saying why it must stay.

  `tests/test_redaction_attestation.py` is new: 13 test functions, 22 cases; 8 functions / 13 cases are red on `84113ab` and green after, on both gate interpreters. Counts are given as functions *and* cases throughout, because parametrising over the executor lever makes the two differ by nearly a factor of two and a bare number invites the reader to check the wrong one. The parallel-path ones are parametrised over `ISOCENTER_FORCE_THREADS`/`ISOCENTER_FORCE_PROCESSES` because `redact()` picks its executor from those, and the serial path is asserted alongside on every property so the two public spellings of one behaviour cannot drift again. The absence assertions use `key not in inst.attributes`, never `not inst.attributes.get(key)` -- `test_redaction_multizone.py`'s existing hash assertion is written the loose way and passes on `None` as readily as on absent, which is one reason this survived. The remaining 5 functions / 9 cases are labelled selectivity guards and are green on both sides: a rule one of whose zones lands still counting, the surviving `YES` flag, the serial path's existing correctness, the second `redact()` staying a no-op, and the zero-area zone. A guard that is green on both sides states nothing about the fix, so each says in its docstring what it is holding still and why. (#235)

- **`session.redact(force=True)` re-redacts an instance whose attestation already matches, which is the only way to repair a store #229 damaged.** New keyword, defaulting to `False`; default behaviour is unchanged. It suppresses the `current_hash == config_hash` early return and nothing else, on both call sites -- `prepare_redaction_tasks` carries it into each task dict and `redact_machine_instances` takes it as a keyword, last in the signature and defaulted, because four test files call that method positionally with two arguments. `redact_by_machine` does not get it: it is an interactive one-ROI helper that discards the count, and a caller wanting the repair sets rules and calls `redact(force=True)`.

  **The damaged population is real and it is outside this repository.** `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` is `md5({"serial": ..., "rois": sorted(rois)})` -- the *configuration*, with nothing about the array in it. So a store whose pixels #229 left wrong carries a hash byte-identical to the one the corrected code computes, and the corrected code skips it. The per-zone loop behind #229 shipped in **every release up to and including 0.9.0** (`git show v0.9.0:isocenter/services.py | grep -c "for roi in rois"` -> `3`, same on `v0.8.2` and `v0.7.0`). Replayed across two versions, one database carried between them: `v0.9.0` extracted into a scratch tree redacts `[[0,8,0,8],[16,24,16,24]]` on a 32x32 image and leaves `zone1 = 12800` under `applied 1`, hash `d106238c...` and `BurnedInAnnotation = NO`; the same database reopened on `84113ab` with the same rule returns **0** and changes nothing. Both gate interpreters. The burned-in identifier stays in the pixels under an attestation and a flag that both say it was removed.

  **Who is affected, what it looks like, and the repair.** Affected: anyone who redacted with a rule carrying **two or more zones**, against a store that had been **saved and reopened**, on **0.9.0 or earlier**. Symptom: `redact()` returns 0 and the pixels do not change, on a store whose instances carry a redaction hash. Repair: `session.redact(force=True)` then `session.save()`. It works with no source file, because the identifier is still in the store's own pixels. Measured on both interpreters against the two `v0.9.0`-damaged databases above: `zone1 12800 -> 0`, `total 192000 -> 179200`, a new UID and a rewritten hash.

  **Its cost is documented on the parameter, because the user is choosing it.** Every instance the rules match is redacted again, and every one takes a new SOP Instance UID, a new exported filename (#78) and `file_path = None` -- which widens #238's exposure to instances that had already been redacted once.

  **Stated plainly: this does not repair a damaged store. A user who never calls `force=True` keeps one, and keeps it silently.** What changes is that the repair exists in the API instead of requiring the user to know an internal private-tag key name. The automatic fix -- a version or epoch stamp on the attestation, so a release that knows its predecessor was wrong declines to honour the predecessor's claim -- was considered and refused on a promise this project shipped three commits ago, in the #228 entry below: "re-running `redact()` with the same rules is a no-op and renames nothing". Because `modified` is true whenever a zone is in bounds, an epoch bump re-redacts every healthy already-redacted instance too, handing each a new UID, a new filename and a cleared `file_path`. For a beneficiary population of "ran `<= 0.9.0`, **and** used a multi-zone rule, **and** against a saved-and-reopened store", that is disproportionate, and it would mean amending a shipped promise in the release that made it. The version-stamped attestation is the same migration lever #172 and #168 want and is filed to be decided for all three at once.

  **`sorted(rois)` stays, and #237's collision argument is stale.** That issue reads the hash as colliding because zones are *applied* in config order while hashed in sorted order, citing `[[0,8,0,8],[100,200,100,200]]` at `204800` against the reverse at `192000` under one hash. Re-measured on both trees: that divergence was #229, not the sort. On `84113ab` both orderings give `192000`, and an overlapping pair `[[0,8,0,8],[4,12,4,12]]` gives `182400` both ways. Redaction zeroes; zeroing is commutative and idempotent; the applied pixels cannot depend on zone order, which makes `sorted()` *correct* rather than a collision. Changing that input would be worse than leaving it: it is a silent partial epoch bump, re-redacting and renaming exactly the stores whose config order differs from sorted order and no others, with nothing to explain it. The comment at the hash site now carries the measurement and the warning.

  **What remains unfixed, named.** The general shape #237 describes survives: the attestation is still independent of what it attests to, so any *future* redaction defect is equally self-sealing -- the first run writes the hash, every later run declines to look. `force=` is a lever, not detection. Folding a pixel digest into the hash was considered and does not help here, since the digest would be written by the same run that wrote the wrong pixels; it buys a different, real property at the cost of hashing every array on the skip path, and is filed rather than smuggled in. #237 closes as "repairable + documented" only on that framing. (#237)

- **A redacted instance now takes a new SOP Instance UID on every interpreter, not only on the free-threaded one -- so `session.export()` names its redacted output differently than it did on 3.12.** `execute_redaction_task` calls `inst.regenerate_uid()` in the worker and returns the result as `mutation["sop_uid"]`. The parent read that key **only as a fallback lookup key** and never assigned it: `mutation["attributes"]` carries exactly `0008,0008`, `0028,0301`, `0008,2111` and `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH`, and `0008,0018` is not among them. So the regeneration landed only where the worker *was* the parent's object. Under threads -- 3.14t's default -- the new UID, the new `0008,0018` and `file_path = None` were already in place; under processes -- 3.12's default and every GIL build's -- the child mutated a copy and the parent kept the **source's** identity and its `file_path` while picking up `ImageType = DERIVED`, the Derivation Code Sequence and the redaction hash from that same mutation. Two different pixel sets under one SOP Instance UID is the collision `regenerate_uid()`'s docstring says it exists to prevent.

  **It was decided by the executor, not by the interpreter, and it was measured that way.** One 32x32 `MONOCHROME2` instance, saved, reopened, one zone `[0,8,0,8]`, `redact()` then `export()`, under `ISOCENTER_FORCE_PROCESSES` and `ISOCENTER_FORCE_THREADS` on both gate builds (`sys._is_gil_enabled()` asserted `False` inside the process on 3.14.7t). The processes leg was identical on 3.12.13 and 3.14.7t: `sop_instance_uid` still `1.2.3.phi`, exported as `1.2.3.phi.dcm`, one `instances` row under the source UID and **two** `instance_blobs` rows -- 46 referenced bytes against the threads leg's 23. The threads leg was identical on both builds too, with the regenerated UID everywhere and one row in each table. Every one of the four combinations now produces the threads leg's shape.

  **The identity is applied in the parent, gated on a mutation having come back.** `Session._apply_redaction_outcomes` assigns `sop_instance_uid`, `attributes["0008,0018"]` and `file_path = None` -- all three or none -- for every mutation it receives.

  *Corrected in place by #235, which landed in this same release.* As merged, #228 read "gated on the UID having actually changed", assigned those three only when `mutation["sop_uid"] != mutation["original_sop_uid"]`, and instructed the next reader not to widen the condition to `if new_uid:`. That was right for the code it shipped against: `execute_redaction_task` built its mutation dict *outside* the `if modified:` block, so an instance whose zones all started past the edge of the image returned a mutation having had nothing applied to its pixels, and the inequality was the parent's only honest signal. #235 moved the mutation construction **inside** the gate, which is where the serial path had always kept it. A mutation now exists only for an instance the worker regenerated, the inequality is provably always true where it is evaluated, and it is deleted rather than left standing as a condition that reads as a gate and decides nothing. The comments on both sides say so; see the #235 entry for the gate and for where its selectivity moved.
