  **Regenerating is the ruling, and it does not rest on 3.14t's behaviour.** It rests on `regenerate_uid()`'s docstring, written before either path existed; on PS3.5 section 9.1, where a SOP Instance UID identifies exactly one SOP Instance; on PS3.15's action **U** for `0008,0018` under the Basic Application Level Confidentiality Profile, which is what this library implements; and on the fact that the redacted instance already writes `ImageType` starting `DERIVED`, `BurnedInAnnotation = NO`, a Derivation Description and a Derivation Code Sequence (`113062`) -- it is already asserting, in three tags and on both interpreters, that it is a different SOP Instance. The cost was weighed rather than assumed: Isocenter does **not** remap references (`grep -rn "0008,1155\|0008,1150\|ReferencedSOP" isocenter/` returns nothing), so an RTSTRUCT, SR or presentation state pointing at a redacted image points at a UID that is not in the export. That harm is real, is already shipped on 3.14t, and is orthogonal to an interpreter divergence; it is filed separately rather than fixed here.

  **What a caller sees.** Code holding a **reference** to the instance is unaffected -- which is why `tests/test_redaction_wildcard.py` and `tests/test_session.py::test_execute_config_integration` are untouched and pass on 3.12 unchanged, the second of them reading `get_pixel_data()` after `redact()` through the loader the worker's `persist_pixel_data` left behind. Code that re-looks-up an instance by its **pre-redaction** UID after `redact()` now finds nothing on every interpreter rather than on one. `Session` and `DicomStore` expose no lookup-by-UID method, so on those two objects this reaches only callers walking the graph themselves; the **backend** is a different matter. `SqliteStore.load_vertical_attributes(instance_uid)`, `load_vertical_attributes_bulk()`, `get_blob_ref(instance_uid, kind)` and `log_audit(entity_uid, ...)` all take a UID, and `docs/analytics.md` documents `sop_instance_uid` as an emitted DataFrame column. None of them changes behaviour -- they read whatever the store holds, and after `save()` the store holds the new UID -- but a caller who cached a pre-redaction UID and later passes it to one of them gets an empty result rather than an error. **Exported filenames change on GIL builds**: both DICOM write paths name files by the SOP Instance UID (#78), so a pipeline that redacts and then matches exported filenames against source filenames stops matching on 3.12, and starts agreeing with 3.14t. **`file_path = None` reaches one more consumer**, and it is worth naming because the consequence is not a stale lookup: `DicomStore.get_known_files()` is the de-duplication set an incremental `ingest()` compares against (`io_handlers.py`), and it is built from `Instance.file_path`. A redacted instance therefore stops contributing its source path, so re-ingesting the same folder after `redact()` re-adds the **un-redacted** source alongside the redacted copy, and an export writes both. Measured: two instances on `dddb659`/3.14t and on this commit/3.12, one on `dddb659`/3.12 -- the free-threaded build's existing behaviour becoming uniform, not a new one. Tracked in #238, which is orthogonal to this fix.

  **The orphan `instance_blobs` row goes with it, as a side effect worth naming.** The worker regenerates *before* `persist_pixel_data`, so on the processes path the blob went in under a UID no instance in the parent's graph carried, and the parent's own `save()` then wrote the pixels a second time under the source UID: two rows, one of them referencing bytes nothing could reach. `session.compact()` collects exactly such rows, so it was dead space rather than a permanent leak -- and it grew with every redaction for anyone who never compacts. `tests/test_redaction_identity.py` pins one `instances` row and one `instance_blobs` row, both under the new UID, read out of sqlite **after** `close()`, since the persistence manager drains on a background thread. Narrowly with respect to #197: a redacted export no longer carries the SOP Instance UID of the data it was derived from, which removes one way two different pixel sets appear under one identity. It does not implement that issue's accounting.

  **Migration, for a store redacted on a GIL build under the old behaviour.** It holds its redacted instances under their **source** UIDs, plus one orphan `instance_blobs` row each. Neither is repaired retroactively, deliberately: `execute_redaction_task` skips an instance whose `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` already matches the configuration, so re-running `redact()` with the same rules is a no-op and renames nothing -- already-redacted instances keep the identity the old run gave them. Redacting such a store under a *different* rule set does regenerate, at that point. Run `session.compact()` once to reclaim the orphan rows. There is no schema change, no data loss, and no downgrade hazard: a store written after this change opens unchanged on an older Isocenter.

  `session.py`'s `sop = mutation.get('original_sop_uid') or mutation.get('sop_uid')` loses its fallback in the same commit. The map is keyed on **pre**-redaction UIDs, deliberately and with a comment; `sop_uid` is the **post**-redaction one, so the fallback could only ever miss -- and with the identity now assigned three lines below, one name meaning both the lookup key and the new value is how this defect reads wrong in the first place. Pre-1.0: deleted, not aliased. Behaviour for a mutation missing `original_sop_uid` is unchanged (`instances.get(None)` -> the existing "does not match any targeted instance" error log and discard). `tests/test_redaction_consistency.py` is **not** coverage for that line and is not cited as such: it replicates the expression in its own test body instead of calling `_apply_redaction_outcomes`, so it stays green whatever that line says.

  `tests/test_redaction_identity.py` is new and every test in it is parametrised over both executor levers, because a single leg cannot state a property about two executors disagreeing. Verified against `dddb659`: the parent's identity, the exported filename and the store's row counts are **red on the processes leg and green on the threads leg, on both gate interpreters**; that asymmetry is the defect. The off-edge-zone test is a selectivity guard -- green on `dddb659` on both legs -- and is detection only with respect to the gate. The `applied` count and the null-valued attributes an all-off-edge rule writes are unchanged and unasserted here; they are #235, deliberately left so that its fix and this gate can be decided as one gate rather than two that can disagree. *#235 landed in this same release and did decide it; that test kept every assertion and its docstring is rewritten to the gate that now exists.* (#228)

- **Private tags with a binary VR are a documented limitation now, not an open question.** Nothing about the behaviour changes: `populate_attrs` still skips every element whose VR is in `BINARY_VRS` (`OB`, `OW`, `OF`, `OD`, `OL`), those bytes still never enter the object graph, and `remove_private_tags: false` still cannot keep what was never held. What changes is that `docs/configuration.md` now says so where the flag is configured, and says it as a decision with reasons rather than as a caveat awaiting a fix. #125 closes on that basis.

  **Warn-plus-audit is the answer, not a placeholder for one.** The reporting half of #125 added the warning and the `DATA_LOSS` entry and left "whether these bytes should be retained at all is still open" in the docs; #146 carried those entries into section 3 of the compliance report. A reader who found the note in that state had no way to tell whether to plan around the limitation or wait for it to go away. The note now names both storage options and why each was rejected. Holding vendor binary in `attributes` puts an arbitrary blob permanently resident, which is the exact failure `BINARY_VRS` exists to prevent -- memory scaling on 100GB+ datasets rests on heavy arrays never being resident by default, and that guarantee outweighs an unread vendor block. Routing them to the sidecar means giving private tags an offset/length representation the EAV table does not have, plus a lazy loader and an export re-merge path, and `session.compact()` rewrites the sidecar and rewires every offset it knows about -- a class of offset it does not know about is silent corruption after the first compaction. Reporting the loss was cheap; that is not, and it is spec-first work rather than a docs note.

  **The note draws the line, because the previous one only drew half of it.** It said binary-VR private tags cannot be kept and left the reader to infer what happens to the rest, immediately under a bullet reading "`false`: Retains them" -- which reads as "private tags do not work". A table now states both directions: a private tag read with any VR outside `BINARY_VRS` is governed by the flag exactly as documented, swept when `true` and kept and written to the exported file when `false`. The table names that row "text, numeric, and `UN`" rather than by VR family, because `UN` is neither -- it is Unknown, raw bytes, and it is kept out of `BINARY_VRS` deliberately, on the assumption that a private `UN` is a small value rather than a blob. Verified end to end rather than read off the source: a file carrying `0009,0010` (`LO`), `0009,1001` (`LO`), `0009,1002` (`OB`), `0009,1003` (`SH`), and `0009,1004` (`UN`) ingests to a graph holding all but `0009,1002`, exports with all but `0009,1002` present, and files exactly one `DATA_LOSS` row.

  **The note also records a case the same test found by changing one constant: the drop depends on the transfer syntax.** Rewrite that fixture as `ImplicitVRLittleEndian` and `0009,1002` survives -- graph, export, and no `DATA_LOSS` row. Implicit VR carries no VR field, so pydicom resolves it from the standard dictionary, which has no entry for a private tag, and returns `UN`; `UN` is not in `BINARY_VRS`, so the blob is held in `attributes` in full. It is not only resident, it is *stored*: querying the index after that ingest finds the bytes base64-encoded in `instances.attributes_json`, the column the architecture guide's storage table labels "All Standard Tags (Even Groups)", while the EAV table that same row assigns to private tags holds only the private creator. That is the behaviour today and the docs say so rather than promising a drop that only half of real inputs get. It is also the exact case that undercuts the reason `UN` is excluded -- the exclusion assumes `UN` means "small private value", and under implicit VR it means "any private value, including the megabyte one". Documented, not changed: whether `UN` should stay out of `BINARY_VRS` is a behaviour question with a resident-memory blast radius, and this entry only closes the documentation. Filed as [#151](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/151).

  It also says where the loss surfaces, which the old note did not. "Written to the audit log" is not an instruction: the reader who hits this reads a compliance report, not `sqlite3 audit_log`. Three places are named -- the session log, **section 3, Data Loss** of `session.generate_report(path)`, and `store_backend.get_audit_losses()` for the rows -- because a limitation whose evidence you cannot find is indistinguishable from a silent one.

  `docs/architecture.md`'s hybrid-storage table gains two paragraphs for the same reason. The three rows describe where standard tags, private tags, and pixels go, and a reader counting them concludes private tags are stored, full stop; the absent fourth row is the point, and resident memory -- the guarantee section 2 already makes about the pixel array -- is why it is absent. That file then hedges its own paragraph rather than delegating the hedge to `docs/configuration.md`, because a link reads as *more detail* and not as *the opposite*: it says outright that "stored nowhere" holds only when the VR is read as binary, and that "Private Tags (Odd Groups)" in the table describes where the *text* private tags go, since the real split is whether a value serializes to text. Neither is a wording problem and neither is fixed here; both are #151. (#125)

### Fixed

- **The export worker no longer writes its own output back onto the live graph (#184).** `_export_instance_worker` ended its dataset assembly with `populate_attrs(ds, inst)` -- the *ingest* reader, pointed at the dataset the worker had just built. `add_sequence_item` appends, so every sequence item duplicated per export (1 -> 2 -> 3 across exports in one session, and each later export's *file* then carried the accumulated copies), every patient/study/series tag stamped onto the dataset landed in `inst.attributes`, and both writes bump `_revision` -- so the next `save()` persisted the duplicates and a reload had them for real. Latent through `session.export()`, whose `maxtasksperchild=25` pins the workers to subprocesses on every interpreter (only a pickled copy was mutated; that pinning also makes `ISOCENTER_FORCE_THREADS` inert for `session.export()`, which is #185 and is deliberately not touched here), but real through the public `DicomExporter.export_batch()`/`write_tree()`: their `maxtasksperchild` defaults to `None`, so on a free-threaded build the worker runs on the caller's own objects -- measured on 3.14.7t, one `(0008,1140)` item in, two out. The fix is deletion, proven safe by measurement first: with the call disabled, the exported file is byte-identical for a hand-built pixel instance with a sequence, an ingested CT with a sequence and a private tag, and an ingested ECG waveform -- the call contributed nothing to the output, because it only ever wrote in the wrong direction. Its one live side effect -- the worker's two later modality reads (`Pixels missing for Image Modality ...`) seeing the *merged* series-level value via the writeback -- is preserved by reading `ds` instead of `inst.attributes`, which is where the merged view already lives; an instance whose modality exists only at series level is still judged by it, in both directions (a pixel-less CT is still refused as CT, and a pixel-less SR is no longer at risk of being judged by the `"OT"` fallback, which sits in `_IMAGE_MODALITIES`).

- **A per-file ingest failure now reaches the caller, the audit trail, and the compliance report (#211).** Ingesting a file pydicom refuses -- the filing case was a 16-bit `YBR_FULL` whose colour conversion raises `Invalid ndarray.dtype 'uint16'`, but *any* per-file failure took the same path -- used to produce one console line (`ERROR: Import Failed`), zero patients, no exception, no audit row and a `None` return: a run that rejected 40 of 500 files reported exactly like a clean one. This is #181's hole on the other end of the pipeline, and it gets #181's treatment. Each rejected file now writes an `ERROR` audit row carrying the path (a file that failed to parse has no SOP Instance UID to be named by -- the same fallback the export side uses) and the reason; the rows land in `get_audit_errors()`, so the report's Exceptions section names them and the grade cannot read `PASS` over a cohort that silently lost files. `DicomImporter.import_files` and `session.ingest()` now return an `IngestSummary` -- `ingested`, `failures` as `(path, reason)` pairs, `declined` (#238's refusals), `skipped` (already indexed) -- so "ingested 460 of 500" is discoverable programmatically; both returned `None` before, so no existing call breaks. The scoping question the issue left open is decided as **failure, not loss**: `ERROR`, not `DATA_LOSS`, because #146's loss scopes describe elements missing from data that *was* ingested, and a file that never entered the store has nothing there to scope -- nothing the store holds is smaller than it claims to be. The route is general by construction: the worker's blanket `except`, its decompression arm and a parent-side linkage failure all return through the one recording path, so the fix does not depend on which conversion pydicom refused.

- **A store whose pixel geometry #186 already corrupted is now detected at open, and a session holding it does not grade PASS (#214).** The #186 fix stopped the write -- `get_pixel_data()` no longer persists a guessed `SamplesPerPixel=3, PhotometricInterpretation=RGB` with swapped axes for a multi-frame grayscale instance -- but it could not undo the descriptors already in stores, and nothing detected them. Measured on the fixed branch against a simulated post-#186 index of a 3-frame 8x3 source: the export writes 1 of 1, the file decodes to a 1x72 strip, `ExportSummary.failed` is 0, and the report grades `PASS`. Every step behaves correctly on input that is already wrong: the corrupted descriptors make `SidecarPixelLoader` build a 216-element target for 72 samples, it falls through to its 1-D return, and the resolver honestly reports a rank-1 array as `rows=1, cols=72`.

  The detector is arithmetic and runs twice through one method (`SqliteStore.check_pixel_geometry`): at session open, where each flagged instance gets a logged warning naming it, the disagreement (both byte counts), and the remedy; and in `generate_report`, where the same result files a `COMPLIANCE_CHECK` exception per instance -- the channel a `BurnedInAnnotation` hit already rides, because it is the same claim one attribute over: the store holds an instance whose export cannot be trusted, and a report over such a store must not say `PASS`. Re-derived from the store at report time rather than remembered from open, so any session over the store carries it. The check: Rows x Columns x SamplesPerPixel x NumberOfFrames x bytes-per-sample must equal the stored sidecar frame length, with bytes-per-sample mirroring the loader's dtype bucketing (`2 if bits > 8 else 1`, because the sidecar holds `pixel_array.tobytes()` and a 1-bit mask is stored expanded to uint8 -- `BitsAllocated/8` would flag every healthy Segmentation). **Scope: frames stored uncompressed.** A zlib frame's stored length is post-compression, so the equality binds no store either way, and deciding it by decompressing every frame would read the whole sidecar on every open; damage behind a compressed frame is caught where the bytes are decoded -- `export(verify_readback=True)` (#209), which is named in the warning. There is deliberately no repair: the sidecar's bytes are shape-free, the original file is the only source of truth for an instance that still has one, and a best-effort migration that silently half-works is worse than a detector. Same family as #168 and #172: the code is fixed, the data written by the old code is not. (#214)

- **A store written before the #160 fix no longer exports a hollow multiplex item: hydration prunes what ingest would have (#168).** #160 fixed the truncation at ingest -- `ingest_worker` drops the Waveform Sequence `(5400,0100)` items whose samples it discards -- which covers new ingests only. A store indexed between 0.8.2 and that fix holds one item per multiplex group with samples behind item 0 alone, and `ingest_worker` never runs again: `persistence.py` hydrated whatever the store held, and `_export_instance_worker` wrote every item back, declaring a multiplex group with no Waveform Data `(5400,1010)` -- Type 1 (PS3.3 C.10.9), a file a conformant reader may reject. Nothing self-healed it; `session.compact()` rewires sidecar offsets and never re-reads sources.

  The heal runs in the hydration path (`_wire_waveform_loader`, shared by `load_all` and `load_patient`): a Waveform Sequence with more than one item is pruned to item 0, and the annotations referencing the pruned groups go through the same filter ingest uses (#177) -- pruning the item alone would have unmasked exactly the dangling ordinal that filter exists to prevent. Hydration rather than export, for #160's own reason: the graph is what every consumer reads, and a writer that quietly drops items is a second answer to "which multiplex groups does this record have" -- besides re-creating the silent truncation #36 closed. It does edit a graph the user did not ask to have edited, so it is **loud**: a logged warning per instance naming what was pruned, why (the store predates the fix; the samples were discarded by the session that ingested, whose `DATA_LOSS` entry is in this same store's audit log), and the remedy (the samples are unrecoverable from the store; re-ingest the originals into a fresh index). It is a warning and not a new audit row because the loss is already in the trail -- the heal makes the graph agree with what the log has said since 0.8.2. The prune is not an edit in the bookkeeping sense: direct container mutation, no `set_attr`, so `_revision` stays put, the stored `phi_status` survives, and the graph reads as clean; the store is unchanged until the user saves, and the warning repeats on every open until then. `DicomExporter.write_tree()` on a hand-built graph is deliberately untouched and pinned so by test -- the serializer applies no gates by design, and there is no store, and no earlier session's audit trail, anywhere in that picture. (#168)

- **A DICOM export no longer carries a waveform annotation whose reference names a multiplex group that is not in the file (#177).** #160 removes the Waveform Sequence `(5400,0100)` items whose samples ingest discards, which fixed a Type 1 violation -- and created this: Waveform Annotation Sequence `(0040,B020)` lives at instance level, and its Referenced Waveform Channels `(0040,A0B0)` names a group by the ordinal of its Waveform Sequence item (PS3.3 C.10.10.1.1). After the del, an annotation on group 2 referenced an item the exported file did not carry -- a miss rather than a wrong answer, which is the better failure direction, but exactly the kind of dangling reference a strict downstream reader rejects. #159 fixed the same reference for the `annotations.json` path only; the DICOM writer consults nothing about which groups survived.

  The filter runs in `ingest_worker`, beside #160's del, and it works on `(group, channel)` PAIRS before it drops items: an annotation naming all of group 1 plus a channel of group 3 keeps its surviving pairs, and an item is dropped only when every pair it named is gone. Surviving ordinals are **never renumbered** -- the ordinal is positional, so renumbering after a discard would make the file internally consistent and *wrong* relative to the source, with no way to tell afterwards; kept references name kept items, whose positions did not move. Two shapes are deliberately untouched: an annotation with no `(0040,A0B0)` at all (Type 1C -- it applies to the whole waveform and cannot dangle) and one whose value yields no parseable pair (there is no reference to know is dangling). The drop is not silent -- that would re-create #36 one element over: one `DATA_LOSS` row per instance, scoped `STANDARD` (the acquired-samples loss those marks described already costs the run its `PASS` via the `SIGNAL` row; grading the bookkeeping too would double-charge one loss under two entries), naming how many annotations were dropped and which groups they referenced. The `(0040,A0B0)` reading -- list coercion, 1-based ordinal, pair iteration -- moved from `murmur.py` into `waveform.py` so the graph-side filter and the Murmur bridge parse the pairs identically; a second parser is a second answer to "which group does this mark name", which is how #159 happened. One consequence for the WFDB path: the bridge's own export-time drop now fires only for a graph that never passed through ingest, because the dangling marks are gone from the graph before it looks. (#177)

- **A private value containing a literal backslash no longer exports with the wrong multiplicity (#195, #190).** `\` is DICOM's value delimiter (PS3.5 6.2), and the exporter's fallback coerced backslash-bearing private values to `LO` -- a 1-n VR, under which pydicom writes the backslash as a separator and every reader re-splits on it. A conformant source file carrying `(0009,1025) LT 'se\rial'` -- `LT` is VM 1, backslash is ordinary text there -- came back from the documented ingest -> export path as `LO ['se', 'rial']`: one value in, two out, zero `DATA_LOSS` rows, no API call anywhere. The value survived ingest intact; the export's VR coercion is what changed what the bytes mean. The VM = 1 `str` arm had done this since #118 and shipped in every release; the VM > 1 arm arrived with #165, which for this one population converted a loud loss (the old "no VR fits" `ValueError`, filed as `DATA_LOSS`/`PRIVATE`, graded `REVIEW_REQUIRED`) into a silent wrong value.

  **The two arities take different answers, because only one of them has a lossless one.** A single value goes to `UT`: VM 1, so pydicom neither separates on write nor splits on read, and the value round-trips byte-faithfully -- measured in #195, so no loss row is filed and none is owed. The `str` arm is now "`LO` if 64 characters or fewer *and* no backslash, else `UT`". A multi-valued element with a backslash-bearing atom has no answer: no text VR carries both the content and the arity (`LO` splits the atom, and a `UT` join is ambiguous in exactly the same way -- the join's separators and the atom's own backslash are indistinguishable on read). `_fallback_multivalue` returns `None`, the existing "nothing fits" signal, which routes to the `DATA_LOSS`/`PRIVATE`/`REVIEW_REQUIRED` channel -- the same call #165's entry already makes for a partial element: a loud loss beats a silently wrong element. That shape is reachable only by injection (`set_attr` with a list whose atom carries `\`): pydicom splits source elements at ingest, so file-sourced atoms are separator-free, verified by #190's three-generation round trip. The check runs **before** the over-long collapse, deliberately -- once collapsed to a `UT` join, a backslash-bearing atom is unrecoverable, so the combination of an over-long sibling and a backslash-bearing atom takes the loud path, not the join.

  **The over-long collapse stays, and stops being silent.** `['x' * 80, 'y']` still becomes one `UT` string with a joined value -- the values are all present and recoverable by splitting, so a `DATA_LOSS` row would overstate it (#190's words) -- but `_merge` now logs a warning naming the tag and the arity change (VM n -> 1), because #146/#148 built the loss channel precisely so a reviewer does not discover a shape change by reading the file. No new audit action type: the row vocabulary is reserved for elements absent from the output, and this one is present.

  **This corrects #154's severity claim in passing.** That issue records the `LO` coercion and says "the values survive and are byte-faithful... a gap in what is promised, not a broken promise". For `LT`/`ST`/`UT` values containing a backslash that was not true: the coercion was silently lossy, and #154's own severity was understated by exactly this case. #154 itself -- carrying the source VR through so a private `UT` keeps its VR rather than being re-derived -- stays open; this fix only makes the re-derivation stop changing what the value *is*. Pinned at the encoder (`tests/test_private_tag_export.py`, both arities plus the precedence of the backslash check over the collapse) and end to end (`tests/test_export_loss_audit.py`: the injected list produces one `PRIVATE`-scoped `DATA_LOSS` row, a `REVIEW_REQUIRED` grade, and no element in the file). (#195, #190)

- **Two rules targeting one instance now both land under threads, every run (#257).** `execute_redaction_task` captured `original_sop_uid` from the live instance at the top of the worker. Under threads -- 3.14t's default, or `ISOCENTER_FORCE_THREADS` -- every task's worker shares that instance, and when two rules matched one instance (wildcard + explicit, or duplicate serial spellings out of `load_config`, which de-duplicates nothing) roughly 1 run in 8 had the second worker read `inst.sop_instance_uid` *after* the first worker's `regenerate_uid()`. Its mutation came home keyed on a post-redaction UID; the parent's `instances` map is keyed on **pre**-redaction UIDs, deliberately (#228); so the lookup missed, the error log said "does not match any targeted instance", and the redaction was **discarded by a run that reported no error**: `redact()` returned 1 where every other run of the same input returned 2, and -- the surface #255 added -- the losing pass's `REDACTION` audit row read `Applied 0 of 1` where the same input usually wrote `1 of 1`. Identical input, intermittently different compliance report. The pixels were actually redacted either way (the worker *is* the parent's object under threads); what raced was the accounting and the identity/loader bookkeeping the parent applies per mutation. Under processes it could never happen -- tasks are pickled at dispatch, each worker redacts its own pre-redaction copy -- which is why `test_two_rules_sharing_a_serial_write_two_rows` ran under the processes lever only, with the race named in its docstring rather than absorbed as flakiness.

  **The capture moves to task-preparation time.** `prepare_redaction_tasks` now records each instance's UID on its task dict as `"original_sop_uid"` -- parent-side, before any worker is dispatched, so no sibling's `regenerate_uid()` can have moved it -- and the worker trusts the task rather than re-reading the shared object. The task dict already carries per-task state the worker must not re-derive (`config_hash`, `force`); this is that pattern applied to identity. The alternatives in #257 were weighed: serializing tasks that share an instance gives up parallelism to protect a read that doesn't need protecting, and validating multi-rule configs away under threads declares an ordinary config undefined on one executor -- both treat the symptom of a read that simply belongs on the other side of dispatch. The parent's `instances` map keys on the same task field now, so one capture answers "what was this instance called before redaction" on both sides of the round-trip -- two independent reads of the live attribute at almost the same moment is exactly the shape that raced. Under processes nothing changes: the capture crosses the pickle boundary inside the task instead of being taken from the worker's copy, and the two are byte-identical.

  The race is probabilistic (unreproduced in 40 threads-lever runs on one machine, ~1 in 8 on the machine that found it), so `tests/test_redaction_uid_capture.py` pins its **certain form** instead: a serial in-process map runs the workers on the parent's own objects -- the threads path's sharing -- back-to-back, so the second task's capture always follows the first task's `regenerate_uid()`. Measured pre-fix: `applied=1` and `('SN_RELOAD', 'Applied 0 of 1 ...')` on **every** run, plus the discard error naming a UID no rule ever targeted. Alongside it, two structural pins: the task carries the pre-dispatch UID, and a `regenerate_uid()` landed between preparation and execution leaves the worker's mutation keyed on the task's capture. `test_two_rules_sharing_a_serial_write_two_rows` is parametrised over both executor levers now that the threads leg is deterministic -- coverage, not the pin, since a single threads run only ever hit the race ~1 in 8. (#257)

- **`validation_status` now demands evidence for what this session did, not for anything at all (#254).** The grade's summary arm asked only whether `audit_summary` was non-empty, so a session that redacted, and whose `REDACTION` rows were then lost to a second defect (a dropped batch of the #219 shape), still graded `PASS` on the strength of whatever unrelated rows survived -- the exact structure of #247's second reading, one defect further away. `generate_report` now compares the audit summary against the verbs the session performed: a run that redacted must find `REDACTION` in the summary, and a run that anonymized must find at least one of `RemediationService`'s emitted types (`REMEDIATION_REPLACE` / `REMEDIATION_SHIFT_DATE` / `REMEDIATION_REMOVE` -- spelled once, as `remediation.REMEDIATION_ACTION_TYPES`); missing expected evidence grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED`. No previously-honest report changes grade: a clean redacting or anonymizing run carries its own rows and keeps its `PASS`, and a session that performed neither verb owes the summary nothing. The report whose grade changes is precisely the one whose section 2 already contradicted it.

  **The session's memory of its own verbs is transient, in-memory and session-scoped, on purpose.** #254's open call warned that this half of the design is a second answer to "what happened", the kind this codebase deletes (the retired `text_index`, #84) -- and persisted, it would be exactly that: a durable record that can disagree with the audit log across reopens. Held only for the life of the `Session` object, it is instead the cross-check's other input: the grade is a comparison between what this session did and what the log heard, and a comparison needs both sides. A reopened session grades on the log alone, as before -- the memory answers for the run that holds it, never for a predecessor.

  **"Performed" means "would have emitted", not "was called".** The redaction verb is recorded beside `record_redaction_pass` (#255's single emitter), after the early returns for no rules loaded and no matching images -- so a `redact()` that emits no row demands no row. The anonymize verb is recorded on a nonzero applied count, the point where remediation queues its rows. A run that performs work and *fails* still expects its evidence, correctly: both emitters write their rows before their raise (#255, #213), so the rows exist unless something lost them. `tests/test_report_action_evidence.py` pins the two loss cases red-first (rows deleted after the barrier, one unrelated `EXPORT` row keeping the summary non-empty) and holds still the clean `PASS` on both verbs plus the three no-work boundaries. (#254)

- **A failed write no longer leaves its partial file under the real name (#199).** `_export_instance_worker` wrote through `save_as` directly onto the output path, and `save_as` creates the file before streaming elements into it in ascending tag order. A write that raised part-way returned `ExportOutcome(ok=False)` -- audited, counted, graded -- and left the file it had created exactly where a recipient would find it. What a recipient of a released cohort was exposed to: a `.dcm` that `pydicom.dcmread` accepts without complaint, short by whatever had not been streamed when the exception fired -- most often Pixel Data, since `(7FE0,0010)` is written last and is the largest element. Not a truncation error at read time; a dataset that parses, missing elements. The mechanism is the commonest real export failure there is: an `ENOSPC` or `EIO` late in a large cohort export leaves the last few files truncated after their identity block. It also made the report contradict the folder -- `| Instances Written | 2 of 3 requested |` beside three readable files -- and it is why #198 existed: with partials on disk, `ok=False` could not mean "nothing was delivered", so the recoverable-identity disclosure had to go and `stat` the output tree to avoid under-counting re-identifiable files.

  The worker now writes to a temporary name in the destination directory (`<real>.{pid}.tmp` -- same filesystem, so the finishing `os.replace` is one atomic directory-entry swap) and renames into place only when the write finished. The cleanup on failure lives in the worker because it is the only frame that knows a write started and did not finish, and it is always a subprocess (`maxtasksperchild=25`), so it cannot lean on parent state. A worker killed outright can still orphan one `.tmp`; that residue is what atomicity costs, and what can no longer exist is a partial under a name a recipient trusts. `DicomExporter.write_tree()` funnels through the same worker, so the serializer path -- which fails by raising and previously left the same partials in fixture-generator output with no audit trail at all -- is fixed by the same change. **#198's delivered-set widening in `_report_recoverable_identities` is deliberately not reverted**, although #199 removes the case it was filed for: the `os.path.exists` union still covers a worker that renamed its file and then died before answering (`run_parallel` hands back an exception with no outcome, #232) and a re-identifiable file left by an earlier export into the folder being released -- both under-claims, and an under-claim is what gets a re-identifiable file treated as safe. Its docstring now says which directions it still guards. One observable disclosure changes: a late write failure used to leave a token-carrying partial and be disclosed as "3 of 3"; the same failure now delivers two files and disclosed "2 of 2", which is the count of files that exist. Pinned in `tests/test_export_atomic_write.py` (both paths: no file under the real name, no orphaned temp, clean exports byte-identical in layout) and re-pinned in `tests/test_check_reversibility.py`, whose late-failure arm previously asserted the partial file it existed to document. (#199)

- **`generate_report()` comes after `export()`, both documents now say so, and a report generated before any export names its own boundary instead of silently grading (#153).** Export-side `DATA_LOSS` rows are written *during* `export()` -- `_merge`'s failure arm (the only export-side site deriving a `PRIVATE` scope from a real tag), the empty-waveform-samples loss, `_report_export_losses` -- and `generate_report()` reads the audit log at the moment it is called. CLAUDE.md documented the call order as `… → verify → generate_report → export`, in which every export-time loss misses the report; README put `export()` first, which works. Measured on one session, varying **only** the call order, with an unencodable value planted on private tag `(0009,1003)`: `report_then_export -> PASS`, `export_then_report -> REVIEW_REQUIRED`. Same session, same loss, same final audit log -- and since #146 the cost is the grade, which is the part people quote. CLAUDE.md was the wrong document; its pipeline line now reads `… → verify → export → generate_report` and says why, and `docs/analytics.md`'s warning box closes the question it had left open.

  **The order is a real constraint, so the report states it rather than relying on the reader having picked the right document.** When the audit log holds no `EXPORT` row (#166's row -- keyed on the log rather than a session flag because the log survives a session reopened on an existing store, where a per-process `_has_exported` cannot), the Executive Summary carries a boundary note directly under the Validation Status -- *"generated before any export -- the audit trail records no EXPORT action. Losses recorded at export time cannot appear in this report or its Validation Status; if this session exports, regenerate the report afterwards."* -- and `generate_report()` logs a warning. The note sits under the grade, not in section 3, because a pre-export PASS with the caveat three sections away is how this read in the first place. It never moves the grade: an audit-only session that legitimately never exports is stating a boundary, not failing -- the same absent-row logic as `Instances Written` (#181), said out loud. A log line only, never a `WARNING` audit row, which would flip the very grade the note deliberately leaves alone.

  **The coverage gap that let this survive a green suite is closed.** No test exercised emitter → grade: `tests/test_data_loss_reporting.py` hand-injects rows via `log_audit(..., loss_scope=...)` and `tests/test_export_loss_audit.py` stops at the row. `tests/test_report_export_boundary.py` now runs the pipeline end-to-end -- plants a real unencodable value on `(0009,1003)`, exports, generates, and asserts `REVIEW_REQUIRED` with the tag named in section 3.1 -- plus a replay of the old documented order asserting the pre-export report grades PASS *and* carries the note, the post-export one drops the note and grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, and the boundary survives a session reopened on the same store. (#153)

- **`export()` writes an unconditional `EXPORT` audit row, so the report's Audit Trail counts the third action README always promised (#166).** README.md:369 describes the Audit Trail as "Aggregated counts of every action (Anonymize, Redact, Export)", and two of the three existed: `anonymize()` writes `REMEDIATION_*`, `redact()` writes `REDACTION`, and `export()` wrote no row of its own -- measured on ingest → `audit()` → `anonymize()` → `export()`, the summary gained `{}` by exporting, so the reader of a report on a run that exported 5,000 images saw no line saying so. The row was always intended: `log_audit`'s docstring lists `'EXPORT'` as its **first** example action type and nothing in the tree wrote it, and the export path already wrote a conditional row (`REVERSIBLE_EXPORT`). One row per run rather than per instance -- the per-instance record is the output tree itself -- with `entity_uid` the destination folder and details carrying the delivery arithmetic the run already computes: *"DICOM export to /out: wrote 5 of 5 planned instances from 2 patients."* The empty-plan early return writes one too (*"wrote 0 of 0 planned instances; nothing matched the export plan"*): still an export run, and a subset that matched nothing is a fact the audit trail has to carry. The WFDB exporter writes its own (*"WFDB export to /out: wrote 3 records."*), because #153's boundary note keys on the row's absence, and a format that skipped it would tell its users their finished export never happened. `docs/analytics.md`'s Audit Trail line now names export runs alongside the other two counts. (#166)

- **An export that returns early or raises no longer inherits the previous export's `Instances Written` row (#196).** `_last_export_written`/`_last_export_requested` are session-scoped and were assigned only at the successful end of `_export_dicom`, and no path ever cleared them -- so after a second export whose plan was empty (a subset matching nothing) or whose batch raised at the pool, `generate_report()` still rendered the *first* export's numbers as a description of the second: "Instances Written | 3 of 3 requested" under a `PASS`, beside an output folder holding zero files. The raising case is the sharp one -- `export()` does raise there, so the caller is told, but the compliance report generated afterwards is the document a site reads, and it was silent. Both attributes are now reset to `None` at the top of `_export_dicom`, before anything can return or raise, so an export that does not complete leaves the row *absent* rather than wrong. That is the option consistent with the reasoning already recorded on `ComplianceReport`: an absent row says "not answered here", a zero would say "nothing was written", and a stale pair answers for the wrong export. Callers who read the row after a failed export see it disappear where it previously showed a confident stale number -- there is no previously-*true* sentence this removes. Pinned in `tests/test_export_delivery_counters.py` through both incomplete paths, each after a successful export that plants the stale numbers. (#196)

- **Two instances sharing a SOP Instance UID no longer export as "2 of 2 requested" beside one file, and the overwrite is audited (#197).** Filenames are the SOP Instance UID, so both instances -- an ordinary duplicated file in a real archive reaches the in-memory graph as exactly this, since the sqlite `UNIQUE(sop_instance_uid)` constraint protects the index but `export()` works off the graph -- are written to the same path and the second write silently replaces the first. The collision is older than the delivery counters, but #181/#187's counters turned it into false compliance sentences: `Instances Written` counted write operations (`len(written_uids)`, "2 of 2 requested" for one delivered file), and the recoverable-identity disclosure counted its numerator over the tasks and its denominator over `set(written_uids)`, rendering **"2 of 1 exported instances carry encrypted original identities"** -- arithmetic that cannot be true under any reading, in the row whose entire job is telling a recipient how many re-identifiable files they hold. Both counts now derive from the de-duplicated set -- `ExportSummary.written` is `len(set(written_uids))`, and the disclosure's `affected` is a set matching its `delivered` denominator -- so the report reads "Instances Written | 1 of 2 requested" and the disclosure "1 of 1": one UID is one file, whatever wrote it.

  **The overwrite itself is reported, not refused.** `export()` still writes -- deciding whether a duplicate UID should abort an export is a bigger call than these counters and is left open -- but `_report_export_collisions` now files an `ERROR` audit row per colliding output path, naming the UID, how many instances mapped to it, and that each successful write overwrote the one before. `ERROR` rather than `DATA_LOSS`, and not a new vocabulary: the end state is an instance that was requested and is not in the folder, which is exactly what `_report_export_failures` files `ERROR` for (#181), so the row reaches `get_audit_errors()`, the Exceptions & Errors section names it, and the run grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED` like any other undelivered instance -- where a `DATA_LOSS` row graded `STANDARD` by `loss_scope` would leave the run at `PASS` over a silent overwrite nothing can certify as a harmless duplicate rather than two different images wrongly sharing a UID. Grouped by output path rather than by UID (the same UID under two different series lands in two directories and collides with nothing), and keyed on the outcome like #187's disclosure: a path every write to failed has no file, no overwrite, and its failures already carry their own `ERROR` rows. A previously-`PASS` run over a duplicate-UID archive now grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, which is the point: the folder disagrees with the plan, and now the report says so. Pinned in `tests/test_export_delivery_counters.py`, including a unique-UID control filing no row. (#197)

- **A `DATA_LOSS` row on an instance whose write then failed now says the file was never written (#240).** `DicomExporter._report_export_losses` filed a row for every entry in `r.losses` without asking `r.ok`, so a worker that dropped an element and *then* failed -- the float16 arm followed by an IOD-validation raise, or #223's Pixel Data Provider URL deletion ahead of any later failure -- produced a `DATA_LOSS` row reading "was not exported" about a file that does not exist, two lines above the `ERROR` row saying so. A reader of section 3 of the compliance report was told the export dropped one element from an instance whose entire contents were dropped. Same shape as #187, one channel over: a compliance statement written from what was attempted rather than what was delivered.

  **The row is kept, not suppressed.** The observation is true -- the exporter did delete the element from the in-memory copy before the write failed -- and `if r.ok:` would silently discard it for instances that failed for an unrelated reason later, which is #181's principle in reverse: a compliance record that drops an observation is its own small lie. What was false was the reader's inference, so the record now removes the inference instead of the observation: every `DATA_LOSS` row (and its warning log line) belonging to a failed outcome carries the suffix *"The file itself was not written: this instance's export failed after the element was dropped."* No new action type -- the row is still a data loss, still scoped by the same parity rule, still rendered in section 3.1; a distinct type would have moved these rows out of the one table a reader checks for losses.

  **This changes what section 3 contains for every failed export.** A failed instance's losses used to render indistinguishably from a written instance's; they now name the missing file in the Element column. The *grade* never moved and still does not: the `ERROR` row from `_report_export_failures` already takes the run to `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, which is why this was a wrong statement rather than a wrong grade. Whether `STANDARD` losses should move `validation_status` at all stays open on #150, and #199's partial file left in the output tree is a different mechanism, untouched here. Pinned in `tests/test_export_loss_audit.py`, at the parent contract (both outcomes side by side: a loss on a written file reads exactly as the worker wrote it) and end to end through `session.export()` on the float16-then-validation-failure shape: zero files, one `ERROR` row, one annotated `DATA_LOSS` row. (#240)
- **The leak classifier reads config zones the way redaction applies them (#264).** `verification.py` was the last consumer of `redaction_zones` still speaking the old convention: `verify_instance`'s coverage loop unpacked each zone as `(zx, zy, zw, zh)` and `is_covered`'s documented contract said `zone_box: (x, y, w, h)`, while the established convention (#258) is zone space `(y1, y2, x1, x2)` -- `apply_redaction_to_array`, both redact paths, and the export worker all read it that way, and #263 made automation write it that way. So the PARTIAL_LEAK/NEW_LEAK classification misread every zone, in both directions. Text a correct zone provably redacts could classify as a leak -- and those false findings feed `suggest_config_updates`, which #263's now-correct automation faithfully converts into real, unnecessary zones, so the pipeline's response to its own misreading was to grow the config. And text no zone touches could classify as covered and be silently dropped from the findings: a `verify()` pass attesting coverage the pixels don't have, which is the half that reports success while wrong -- the user reading "no leaks" was told the burned-in text is inside a zone when redaction, reading the same numbers correctly, never goes near it.

  The zone unpacking now has exactly one spelling: a `_coverage` helper holds the intersection math and the conversion comment, `is_covered` is a threshold over it, and `verify_instance`'s loop calls it instead of inlining its own copy -- the inline copy's own comment asked "reuse is_covered logic but return float?", and two copies of the unpacking is precisely how a convention fix to one reader would miss the other. `text_box` deliberately stays OCR box space `(x, y, w, h)`: the boxes come from `analyze_pixels` and the finding metadata's `text_box` is documented as box space, which #263's EXPAND_ZONE arm depends on (it converts `text_box` and does *not* convert `best_zone`, because `best_zone` is the rule's own entry stored verbatim -- unchanged here, and now actually in zone space on arrival, which that arm already assumed).

  Fixtures encoding the old convention are re-derived, not preserved, the same way #263 did for `tests/test_automation.py`: `tests/test_ocr_formal.py` and `tests/test_verification_logic.py` both pinned an `[0, 0, 100, 100]` zone -- a square that only means anything read as `(x, y, w, h)`; read as zone space it is rows 0..0, zero pixels -- and now carry `[0, 100, 0, 100]` with the geometry restated in each comment. Two new tests (`tests/test_verification_logic.py::TestVerifierReadsZoneSpace`) close the loop against the real consumer rather than against the classifier's own arithmetic: each first proves what the zone does to pixels by running it through `apply_redaction_to_array` (text pixels zeroed in one, untouched in the other), then asserts the classification agrees -- covered text yields no finding, uncovered text yields `NEW_LEAK`. Both are red on the commit before this one: the covered text classified `NEW_LEAK`, the uncovered text classified safe. (#264)

- **An audit row that fails to write is now an exception in the compliance report, not a log line (#219).** Since #218 the audit worker does not retry a failed batch write: rows that left the queue and hit an exception in `log_audit_batch` are gone. The rows stay dropped -- that is deliberate, and restoring the old retry is refused twice over. The pre-#218 retry held the failed rows in a worker-local `batch` across iterations, which is precisely the "owned by a local variable no reader can see" state #218's barrier exists to make impossible; and a retry rebuilt on top of the barrier would have to re-enqueue under the lock, which loops forever on a permanently failing write and reorders the log besides. What was not acceptable is what the drop *sounded* like: a `logger.error` line, in the worker's `except` for a non-sqlite failure and inside `log_audit_batch` itself for a `sqlite3.Error` (the spec's §9 item 1) -- two layers, each quietly writing off rows whose entire purpose is to be read later by someone deciding whether the run was clean. Both were the same defect #146, #181 and #218 were fixed for: a compliance reader silently under-informed.

  One mechanism answers both layers. `log_audit_batch` no longer lets any failure out or swallows any silently: whatever the exception, it counts the batch's rows into a dropped-rows counter on the store (its own lock -- the worker calls the method while holding the non-reentrant `_audit_write_lock`, and `RemediationService` calls it holding nothing) and `SqliteStore.get_audit_drops()` reads the count through the flush barrier. `generate_report()` files a non-zero count under Exceptions & Errors as an `AUDIT_DROP` entry, which takes `validation_status` to `REVIEW_REQUIRED` -- the same channel a `BurnedInAnnotation` hit rides, chosen because the failure means the same thing: every count, loss and error in the report was read from a table the dropped rows never reached, so the report cannot vouch for its own inputs and must not say `PASS`. A previously-working sequence changes shape once: `flush_audit_queue()` (and so `stop()` and every audit reader) no longer propagates a non-sqlite exception raised mid-write -- the batch is counted and dropped instead of half-raised and dropped. `tests/test_audit_drop_accounting.py` pins the count, the not-retrying, the surviving worker, the pickle round trip, and the grade flip against a PASS control; #218's barrier tests are untouched and green. (#219)

- **`Study.date_shifted` survives a save/reload, so a reloaded WFDB export keeps its "de-identified" qualifier (#182).** SHIFT_DATE writes the jittered date onto `study.study_date` and sets `study.date_shifted = True`; `WfdbExporter._start_datetime` reads that flag to decide whether the header's date comment may say `de-identified start date:` rather than `start date:` -- the branch exists because the comment used to claim de-identification unconditionally, a false provenance claim on any un-anonymized export. The `studies` table had no column for the flag, so every save dropped it and a reloaded study reported `False` whether or not `anonymize()` ever ran. #171 fixed the value half (the shifted date itself now round-trips as a `date`); this closes the provenance half it deliberately left: the same store exported `de-identified start date: 11/08/2022` fresh and `start date: 11/08/2022` after a reopen. Understating was the safer direction of the two -- the header never *claimed* a de-identification that had not happened -- but a consumer reading provenance out of the one place they would check got a different answer depending on whether the exporting session happened to be reopened.

  The issue's open call -- derive the answer from `phi_status` or the audit log instead of adding a second "was this remediated" record -- resolves to the column, because the flag is not that question. `phi_status` says *whether* a study was remediated; `date_shifted` says *what happened to one field*, and the two come apart for a study whose findings were all non-date -- deriving the qualifier from `phi_status` would stamp "de-identified" on a date that was never shifted, which is exactly the false claim the exporter's branch exists to prevent. Deriving it from the audit log is authoritative but makes an export path query the audit log. So: `date_shifted INTEGER` on `studies`, written by the study upsert (`1`/`0`, never NULL on a current write), read back as `bool(...)` in both `Study` hydrations (`load_all`, `load_patient` -- the same two sites #171 touched), and `tests/test_date_shifted_roundtrip.py` pins the flag and the header qualifier through a real anonymize → save → reopen → export round trip so the two records cannot silently diverge.

  **A store created before the column opens cleanly and reads `False`.** The schema is `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, so an old `.db` keeps its old column set with no error; the guarded ALTER in `_add_missing_columns` adds the column exactly as `phi_status` and `instances.source_path` were added. Rows predating it read NULL, which hydrates as `False` -- correct rather than lossy: the old schema never recorded whether a date was shifted, and a provenance claim the store cannot back must not be fabricated. The information is genuinely gone for such stores: a date shifted under an older release exports without the qualifier, correctly labelled as unverifiable rather than incorrectly labelled as original -- the comment reads `start date:`, which asserts nothing about provenance either way. `Study.study_time` and `Instance.date_shifted` have the same no-column absence (#182's sweep comment) and are deliberately **not** persisted here; whether those ride the same pattern or a different one is still #182's open design call, and folding them in silently would decide it. (#182)

- **Pixels replaced through `Instance.set_pixel_data()` can be read back after the save that persisted them (#212).** `SqliteStore._persist_pixels` wrote the new frame to the sidecar, built the replacement `SidecarPixelLoader`, and only then updated `inst._pixel_hash` -- and the loader's constructor falls back to `pixel_hash or getattr(instance, "_pixel_hash", None)`, so it was built carrying the digest of the frame its bytes had just replaced. The frame on disk was correct; only the loader's expectation of it was stale. A previously-working sequence -- `set_pixel_data()`, `save()`, `unload_pixel_data()`, `get_pixel_data()` -- raised `RuntimeError: Pixel Loader failed for <uid>: Integrity Error: Pixel data hash mismatch ... Expected <pre-replacement digest>, got <digest of the frame just written>`, making correctly-saved pixel data unreadable for the rest of the session; `release_memory()` runs unconditionally in `_export_dicom`, so the next export of that instance failed too. Not silent -- it raised rather than returning wrong bytes -- but loud about the wrong party.

  The fix passes `pixel_hash=digest` into `_create_pixel_loader`, the way `persist_pixel_data` -- the other write path -- always has, rather than reordering the two assignments: passing it removes the ordering dependency instead of relying on the next reader noticing it. The production redaction path was never exposed, by two separate accidents (`RedactionService.redact()` nulls `_pixel_hash` before modifying, and `persist_pixel_data` passes the hash explicitly); the exposed path was the public one. Nothing in the suite ran set-save-unload-read before, which is how 906 tests passed over it; `tests/test_persistence_incremental.py::test_replaced_pixels_read_back_after_save` now does, and is red on the commit before this one. (#212)

- **An automation-applied zone now lands on the pixels the OCR box described (#258).** `ConfigAutomator` wrote OCR box-space `(x, y, w, h)` into `redaction_zones` in both arms -- ADD_ZONE appended the finding's `text_box` verbatim, EXPAND_ZONE computed its union in box space and stored `[x, y, w, h]` -- while every consumer of `redaction_zones` reads `(y1, y2, x1, x2)` (`apply_redaction_to_array`, both redact paths, the export worker). So an applied suggestion redacted rows `x:y` and columns `w:h`: a valid but *different* rectangle, or -- whenever the read produced an empty slice -- nothing at all, and until #244 the nothing-at-all case still earned the count, the new SOP Instance UID, `BurnedInAnnotation = NO` and the attestation hash while the leak the suggestion was created for stayed in the image. `discovery.py` has always converted at exactly this boundary (`"zone": [y, y + h, x, x + w]`); automation is the other producer and now does the same. Suggestion dicts carry `zone`/`new_zone` in `(y1, y2, x1, x2)`; the docstring that promised `[x, y, w, h]` is corrected rather than kept as a second spelling.

  **EXPAND_ZONE needed a provenance ruling before it could be fixed, and the issue asked for it explicitly.** `best_zone` is written in one place -- `verification.py` stores the matched rule's own `redaction_zones` entry verbatim into the finding's metadata -- so it arrives in zone space already and is *not* converted: the union unpacks it as `(y1, y2, x1, x2)`, converts only the box-space `text_box`, and takes the union in zone space. The old code unpacked `best_zone` as `(zx, zy, zw, zh)`, so even its union's inputs mixed conventions. `original_zone` still carries `best_zone` untouched, which is what lets `apply_suggestions` find the entry it replaces.

  `tests/test_automation.py`'s assertions are re-derived, not preserved: the old fixture pinned the wrong convention end to end -- a rule carrying `[[0, 0, 50, 50]]` (zero pixels as read), `best_zone` to match, and `new_zone == [0, 0, 125, 125]` required verbatim. The rewritten test uses a fixture that means something in zone space, and two new tests close the loop the old one never had: each pushes a suggestion through `suggest_config_updates` and `apply_suggestions` and then hands the resulting `redaction_zones` to the real consumer, `apply_redaction_to_array`, asserting the pixels the OCR box described actually went to zero. Before the fix one fails silently-wrong (a plausible rectangle, wrong place) and two fail loud through #244's guard -- the two halves of the defect, both pinned. **Not fixed here**: `verification.py`'s own coverage math reads config zones as `(x, y, w, h)` when classifying leaks, so the PARTIAL/NEW split feeding automation misreads zone space by the same convention; that is the classifier, not the boundary this issue names, and it is left for its own decision. *(Decided and fixed as #264 -- see that entry above; this sentence is annotated in place rather than left describing a defect that no longer exists.)*

- **A worker `run_parallel` loses is a reported failure now, not a vanished pass (#232).** Two consumers branch on a parallel result *being* an exception -- `Session._apply_redaction_outcomes`, whose arm #213 added under the comment "`run_parallel` handing back a worker that died", and the export accounting behind `DicomExporter.write_tree` and `export_batch` -- and no `run_parallel` strategy ever produced that shape: all three were a plain `yield from` over `executor.map` / `imap` / `imap_unordered`, every one of which re-raises a worker's exception at the point of iteration. Both arms were unreachable, and the loss they exist to report happened anyway, unreported: the raise terminated the result generator mid-pass, so every outcome still queued behind the failure was discarded -- instances that *were* redacted never reached the graph -- no `ERROR` row was written for any of them, no `RedactionError` was raised, and the caller got a bare `BrokenProcessPool` (or, for a worker function that raised, the bare exception). Not silent -- the pass was loudly lost and can be re-run -- but the accounting the comments promised never ran, on a PHI-safety path.

  **`run_parallel(..., yield_exceptions=True)` is the mode that makes the documented contract true, and the three dispatch sites that carry an Exception arm now pass it.** Two halves, because a lost worker has two shapes. A task whose function **raises** comes back as its exception, as that task's value, in that task's slot -- caught by a picklable wrapper inside the worker, so one bad item costs that item alone on every strategy (and, on the chunked process paths, no longer costs its innocent chunk-mates either). A worker **killed outright** has no interpreter left to catch anything, so the pool's own failure -- `BrokenProcessPool`, or a result that will not unpickle -- is caught at the iteration and yielded once, as the final value: the results already produced survive, and the tasks queued behind the death, which the pool cannot name, are represented by that one trailing exception. The default is unchanged, deliberately: scan, verify and ingest have no Exception arm, and handing them an exception as data would let it flow into the graph as a result.

  **What a previously-propagating loss looks like now.** `session.redact()` over a lost worker raises `RedactionError` after the pass -- `.attempted` and `.failures` populated, a `("UNKNOWN", "Redaction worker failed: ...")` row (there is no outcome to name the instance with, and the row still has to exist), an `ERROR` audit row under `UNKNOWN`, and every mutation the other workers returned applied to the graph first. A caller that had learned to catch `BrokenProcessPool` around `redact()` should catch `RedactionError`. `write_tree` raises its own documented `RuntimeError` (`Export incomplete. N failed. ...`) with the survivors on disk, instead of the worker's bare exception with no counts; `export_batch` keeps its no-raise contract -- the loss lands in `ExportSummary.failures` and an `ERROR` audit row rather than taking the whole batch's accounting with it.

  **One promise the recycling pool cannot make, written on the parameter rather than left to be found.** `multiprocessing.Pool` answers a *killed* worker by respawning it and waiting forever for the lost task, so under `maxtasksperchild` that shape hangs rather than yields -- ordinary task exceptions still come back as values there. The kill shape is pinned on the `ProcessPoolExecutor` path (`tests/test_parallel_contract.py`, `os._exit` in a module-scope worker); the consumer-visible half is `tests/test_worker_loss_is_reported.py`, under the threads lever because a monkeypatched worker cannot reach a separate process. One residue of the issue is narrower now but not closed: a worker that persisted its redaction to the shared sidecar in its `finally` and died before its result was delivered still leaves bytes the parent never points at -- `compact()` reclaims them -- because the result, not the write, is what crosses back. (#232)

- **A successful `session.redact()` writes the `REDACTION` audit row the compliance report attests with (#247).** It wrote none: the serial path (`redact_machine_instances`) logged one row per machine and the public parallel path logged only failures and `RISK` findings, so a session that redacted an image produced a report whose section 2 read "*No audit logs found*" -- and graded `REVIEW_REQUIRED` only through the `not audit_summary` arm, flipping to `PASS` the moment anything else (one `ANONYMIZE`, say) wrote a row. Whether the report mentioned redaction at all depended on which executor path ran; the grade depended on what else the session happened to do. Measured on `65dcb2e`, both interpreters: one instance, one landing zone, `applied = 1`, `audit summary: {}`.

  **The accounting unit, decided rather than inherited (#247 asked for exactly that).** One row per rule-pass, keyed on the serial spelling the rule was configured with (`"*"` included) -- bounded like the old per-machine row, where per-instance rows would put 10k lines in a 10k-instance report. Written **after** the pass with the outcome -- `Applied X of N candidate images with M zones` -- and **in the parent**, because a worker's audit thread is torn down at pool shutdown without `stop()` and its rows can vanish (#126). Both paths call one emitter, `RedactionService.record_redaction_pass`, and nothing else writes `REDACTION` rows; `tests/test_redaction_audit_accounting.py` pins the two paths to byte-identical accounting for identical work, the same coherence `test_api_coherence.py` demands of the two export paths. "Per rule-pass" is keyed on the rule's *index*, not its serial: `load_config` takes rules verbatim from user YAML with no serial de-duplication, and the first cut of this change keyed on the serial -- review measured two same-serial rules collapsing into one row that kept the first rule's zone count and double-counted the instance, while the serial path wrote two correct rows. The row-before-`RedactionError` ordering is likewise pinned by a test that review demonstrated nothing else held (moving the emitter after the raise left the whole suite green). One inherited limit, filed rather than absorbed: under threads, two rules matching one *instance* race on the shared object (#228's territory), so identical input can intermittently apply once instead of twice -- and now say so in its row; that is #257, and the duplicate-serial test ran under the processes lever, where task pickling makes it deterministic, with the race named in its docstring. *Fixed in this same release: the worker now keys its mutation on a UID captured at task-preparation time, and that test runs under both levers -- see the #257 entry above.*

  **Two visible changes ride on that.** The serial path's row used to be written *before* its loop, as intent -- `Redacting N images with M zones` -- which attested passes whose every instance was then skipped or failed; anything reading `details` text sees the new spelling only, deleted rather than aliased, pre-1.0. And a session that only redacts, cleanly, now grades `PASS` where it graded `REVIEW_REQUIRED` -- the old grade was the right answer for the wrong reason, produced by the empty summary rather than by any judgement about the run. A pass that targeted instances and applied nothing (every zone missed, or everything already attested) writes `Applied 0 of N`, so a silent pass and an absent pass stop looking identical. The grade still requires only *some* audit row, not a `REDACTION` row specifically -- that residue of #247's second reading is filed separately rather than decided here.

- **Every process pool starts its workers by spawn; on Linux 3.12 they forked (#220, #250).** `_run_on_recycling_pool` has pinned spawn since it existed, with the reason beside it: *a forked worker inherits the parent's open SQLite handles and its sidecar file position*. The other two pools -- `_run_on_new_executor`, the one the redaction path uses and the one that pickles the store, and `Session._executor`, including its OOM-recovery restart -- took the platform default, which macOS resolves to spawn and Linux 3.12 resolves to fork. Every local run therefore agreed with the comment while the CI floor quietly did the thing the comment forbids.

  The bill for that arrived as #250: `test (3.12)` on ubuntu intermittently stalled for exactly 900 seconds -- `persistence.py`'s connection busy-timeout -- inside a forked redaction worker's `persist_pixel_data`, then failed with `sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked`. Five occurrences, all Linux/fork, all in redact-after-recent-write shapes (a retry after a failing pass; a `force=` re-redaction of a just-saved store), zero in hundreds of spawn-platform runs, and three of them burned as 'cancelled' jobs before #252's instrumentation could name the error. The faulthandler dump from occurrence four shows every parent thread idle -- the holder is in a child, which is precisely the state a forked child can inherit mid-write and a spawned one cannot. The mechanism inside the child is not pinned down to a line; the population is, and it is the population this closes. #250 stays open until the fix demonstrates itself against CI's hit rate.

  Spawn costs a fresh interpreter per worker (~100-300ms) where fork copied the parent for free. Redaction pools are created per `redact()` call with a handful of workers, and the imaging path already paid it -- the recycling pool has always spawned. Worker functions were already spawn-clean by construction (module scope, picklable state; CLAUDE.md's rule), which is why the whole suite passes on Linux under the pin with no production change beyond the three constructor calls. One test shim adapts: `test_export_error.py` substitutes `ThreadPoolExecutor` for the process pool and now swallows the `mp_context` kwarg the production call passes. `test_parallel_contract.py` pins the constructor argument itself on all three pools -- asserted on the argument, not the effect, because macOS resolves the default to spawn and an effect-shaped assertion would be vacuous on every machine the suite runs on locally.

- **A redaction zone that can never select a pixel now raises instead of earning a full attestation (#244).** `[[0, 0, 20, 20]]` is `(y1, y2, x1, x2)` with `y2 <= y1` -- `arr[0:0, 20:20]`, empty on any image -- and a pass over it used to count the instance, regenerate its SOP Instance UID, and write `BurnedInAnnotation = NO`, `ImageType = DERIVED/SECONDARY` and the attestation hash onto pixels nothing touched: an exported first-class claim that identifiers were removed from an image the pass never looked at. A previously-working `session.redact()` over such a zone now raises `RedactionError` after the pass (per-instance `ValueError: redaction zone (...) selects no pixels ...` rows land as `ERROR` in the audit log first), `redact_machine_instances` raises identically, and an export whose compression path re-applies such a zone fails that instance's write. Nothing is attested, renamed, or persisted for the failed instances -- a corrected configuration retries them (#213's contract).

  **Why validation rather than the gate.** #235 pinned this shape deliberately: the gate is "a zone was applied", and redefining it as "the bytes changed" would cost the attestation-hash skip and turn an idempotent re-redaction into "not applied" -- that refusal stands. But a zone whose *shape* is empty is judgeable with no image at all: it is a configuration error, not a miss. The check sits in `apply_redaction_to_array`, before #235's off-image `continue` (a real zone that landed elsewhere is still a legitimate skip), because that function is the one choke point all three write paths cross -- both redact paths and the export worker -- and a parse-time check would have left `redact_machine_instances`' public `rois` argument and stored-config exports unguarded: two answers to one question.

  **Where these zones come from is its own defect, filed as #258.** The automation path (`automation.py`) writes OCR-space `(x, y, w, h)` boxes verbatim into `redaction_zones` -- `discovery.py` converts to `(y1, y2, x1, x2)`, automation does not -- so an automation-suggested zone redacts the wrong rectangle, or (near the origin) nothing, which is the empty slice this change makes loud. `tests/test_redact_reports_outcome.py`'s own fixture carried `[[0, 0, 20, 20]]` in exactly that belief, so every count it asserted was earned by applications that touched nothing; it is re-derived with a landing zone (same counts, now meaning it), and the pinned zero-area test in `tests/test_redaction_attestation.py` is re-derived into the rejection tests, serial path asserted alongside as ever. The wrong-rectangle half of #258 is *not* closed here -- a plausible-but-different rectangle raises nothing -- which is why that issue stays open at bug severity.

- **Exporting no longer emits a pydicom `UserWarning` per instance from a membership test that could never pass (#248).** `_export_instance_worker` asked `if "_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH" in ds:` before saving, and pydicom's `Dataset.__contains__` rejects a string that is neither a tag nor an element keyword: `UserWarning: Invalid value '_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH' used with the 'in' operator: must be an element tag as a 2-tuple or int, or an element keyword` -- once per exported instance, on the host application's warning stream, because this package deliberately installs no global warning filter (#144). A 10k-instance export produced 10k identical warnings. The suite never saw one because `pytest.ini` ignores everything pydicom raises; the new tests override that filter for themselves.

  The guard and the `del` under it are deleted, and #248 asked for the no-op to be confirmed by measurement rather than by the reading, so it was: on a saved-and-reopened instance redacted under a landing zone -- so `inst.attributes` verifiably carried the attestation the guard named -- the dataset was captured at `_finalize_dataset`, the first call after the deleted site, and `"_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH" in ds` evaluated **False**. `_merge` skips every `_`-prefixed key before the `Dataset` sees it, and the string cannot parse as a tag or keyword, so the `del` was unreachable from both directions. No exported byte changes; the only observable difference is the silence. `tests/test_export_redaction_hash_warning.py` is new: the warning-stream test is red before the deletion and green after, and the membership measurement is green on both sides -- it is the tripwire that fires if `_merge` ever stops skipping underscore keys, which is the one change that could have made the deleted guard matter. Both tests drive `_export_instance_worker` in-process, because `session.export()` always spawns subprocesses (`maxtasksperchild=25` rules threads out) and a worker's warning cannot reach the parent's `warnings` machinery across that boundary. (#248)

- **Re-ingesting a folder after `redact()` no longer re-adds the un-redacted source (#238).** `Instance.regenerate_uid()` clears `file_path` -- it must, or `get_pixel_data()` would reload the un-redacted frame -- and `DicomStore.get_known_files()` built the incremental-ingest de-duplication set out of that same field. A redacted instance therefore stopped contributing its source path, and a second `session.ingest(folder)` re-added the original as a distinct instance: it keeps its SOP Instance UID while the redacted copy carries a generated one, so no key in the store relates them. Measured on 3.12.13 and 3.14.7t, both exporting two files, the source's carrying its burned-in identifier intact, under a compliance report naming nothing. De-duplication now keys on `Instance.source_path`, a new field recording the file an instance was read from, which redaction does not clear and which is persisted in a new `instances.source_path` column (added to existing databases by `ALTER TABLE` on open).

  - `DicomStore.get_known_files()` is **renamed** `get_ingested_paths()`. `store.get_known_files()` now raises `AttributeError: 'DicomStore' object has no attribute 'get_known_files'`. The rename is the behaviour change: a path in the returned set no longer implies the file matches the instance -- for a redacted instance it means the opposite.
  - A second `ingest()` that offers the **pre-redaction original of an image the session already holds** -- recognised by SOP Instance UID, so a copy at another path or through a symlinked mount is caught too -- no longer adds it. The file is skipped, a `WARNING` is logged, and a `WARNING` audit row per file names it in section 4 of the compliance report, taking the run to `REVIEW_REQUIRED`. `ingest()` still returns normally; nothing raises. `WARNING` is not a new channel and needed no renderer change: `get_audit_errors()` already selects `action_type IN ('ERROR','WARNING')` into section 4, and section 2 already counts it.
  - Instances redacted by 0.9.0 or earlier and saved have no recorded origin -- their `file_path` was cleared before any release recorded it -- so a store reopened from that state still re-imports its source folder once, **and does so silently**: neither gate can fire on an instance that recorded nothing, so there is no `WARNING`, no audit row, and the run stays eligible for `PASS`.
