The new machinery does not cover this case and cannot -- read the paragraph above as describing files this release recorded, not files it inherited. There is nothing in the database to recover it from. Re-running `redact()` on such a store does not repair it either; the redacted pixels are already the store's own. Measured: a store built on `65dcb2e` with one redacted and one untouched instance, reopened under this commit, gains the column and hydrates on both load paths; the untouched instance's origin is re-derived from its stored `file_path` and the redacted one's stays `None`; re-ingesting the source folder then leaves three instances -- the redacted copy, the untouched original, and the re-added un-redacted one -- with `get_audit_errors() == []`.
  - This fix does not clean up a store that was already polluted. An un-redacted original that a previous run re-added is a live instance, and removing it is a data decision this release does not make for you.

  `tests/test_reingest_after_redact.py` is new: 13 test functions, 16 cases. 9 functions / 12 cases are detection and every one is red on `65dcb2e` on **both** gate interpreters -- 13 failed, 3 passed on 3.12.13 and on 3.14.7t -- five of them behaviourally, without naming any new API: the folder re-ingest (`['<generated>', '1.2.3.phi']` under both executor levers), the same after a save-and-reload, a copy of the source in a second directory, the sidecar growing for a file that was never imported, and section 4 of the report naming nothing. The other four are red by `AttributeError`/`KeyError`/`sqlite3.OperationalError: no such column: source_path`, which is weaker evidence and is labelled as such in T2's docstring, the one place a reader meets it first; they pin clauses the behavioural five cannot distinguish -- that `file_path` was not resurrected, that the record is written under both executors, and that a `redact(force=True)` does not overwrite it with a generated UID no file carries. That last one runs under **both executor levers**, like the two beside it, because write-once is enforced at two sites and each answers on one executor: making `regenerate_uid()`'s guard unconditional is recorded by the threads leg and invisible to the processes leg, so un-parametrised it passed 15/15 on 3.12 with that guard deleted and was caught only on 3.14t. It is also the only guard on `RedactionService.redact_machine_instances(force=True)`, the serial path, which has no `_apply_redaction_outcomes` behind it on any interpreter -- measured directly, two forced passes through that method: the record stays `1.2.3.phi` and `get_superseded_uids()` still names it. That path is pinned transitively, through `session.redact()`'s threads leg, and not by a test that calls it. The remaining 4 cases are selectivity guards: a genuinely new file still ingesting after a redaction, an ordinary second ingest of an unredacted folder staying silent, and the record not reaching the exported bytes are green on both sides; the fourth -- an instance no zone touched recording nothing -- is vacuous in the two clauses `65dcb2e` can evaluate and errors there on the third, which its docstring says rather than claiming a green.

- **PHI inside a private sequence was invisible to `audit()` under Implicit VR Little Endian, and exported unremediated.** Implicit VR carries no VR on the wire and the standard dictionary has no entry for an odd-group tag, so a private sequence written with a *defined* element length reaches `populate_attrs` as `UN` with a `bytes` value. `UN` is deliberately not in `BINARY_VRS`, so the bytes fell through to `set_attr`, nothing was ever written to `sequences`, and `PhiInspector._scan_instance` walks `sequences`. Measured on one instance carrying private creator `(0009,0010) = ACME_HEADER` and a private `SQ` at `(0009,1003)` whose single item holds `PatientName = SECRET^PHI` and `PatientID = MRN-999`, written twice and run through `ingest -> audit -> anonymize -> export -> generate_report` with `remove_private_tags=False` and `phi_tags = {"0010,0010": REPLACE, "0010,0020": REPLACE}` -- the counts move with the tag configuration, so it is part of the measurement: Explicit VR gave **7 findings**, the two nested ones among them, and an export containing neither identifier; Implicit VR gave **5**, missing exactly those two, and an export containing both. Both graded `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`. One file, two transfer syntaxes, two answers, and the wrong one is the silent one. **Only a defined-length element is affected** -- an undefined-length private `SQ` is already recovered as a `Sequence` by pydicom, whatever the item framing inside it.

  **The parse is not a guess, and the byte-exact re-encode is the whole reason.** `_sequence_from_un_bytes` accepts a candidate only when all four hold: the value starts with the item tag `FE FF 00 E0`; `pydicom.filereader.read_sequence` returns without raising; it consumed exactly `len(value)` bytes; and re-encoding the parsed sequence with `write_sequence` reproduces the original bytes **exactly**. Rules 2 and 3 alone are not enough, measured: a garbage item length, an item followed by eight bytes that decode as one nonsense element, and an empty item followed by eight bytes of vendor payload all get past `read_sequence` without raising and without leaving a byte unread -- those three are the parametrized cases in `tests/test_private_sequence_implicit_vr.py::test_an_unverifiable_candidate_keeps_its_bytes_exactly` that only rule 4 refuses; the fourth, a truncated item header, is refused at rule 2. The empty item is the case that matters -- it decodes to one empty item, and accepting it would silently destroy the eight payload bytes of an ordinary vendor blob that happens to begin with those four. With rule 4 all three fall back untouched. (An undefined-length item with no delimiter behaves the same way and is refused at rule 4 too; it is not among the parametrized four.) The equality is what makes the substitution safe: the caller replaces an attribute with a structure, and byte-identity proves the two are the same bytes, so nothing is lost and nothing is inferred. The gate is conservative in one known way -- a sequence whose item elements are not in ascending tag order re-encodes differently, because pydicom's writer sorts, and so falls back. Non-conformant input gets a row rather than a guess, which is the right side to err on.

  **What a caller sees change.** A previously-clean `audit()` on such an instance now returns the identifiers inside the sequence, each carrying an `entity_path` down to its item, and `anonymize()` remediates them where they live; the exported file carries `ANONYMIZED` where it carried the identifier. `inst.sequences` gains the tag and `inst.attributes` loses it -- deliberately not both, because one tag in both dicts goes through `_merge` and `_merge_sequences` and their call order, not any stated rule, would decide whether the export carried the remediated sequence or the original bytes. The structure is persisted as `__sequences__` and rebuilt on load, so this version's stores round-trip; **a store written before this fix keeps its blob**, because re-deriving structure on load would put a second answer to "what is this element" into the codebase, which is what #84 removed. Callers with an old store re-ingest -- and until they do, that store is unfixed. Measured by writing a store on `65dcb2e` and reopening it with this release, `remove_private_tags=False`: the blob is still an attribute, `inst.sequences` is empty, `audit()` returns **5** findings rather than 7, the exported file carries `SECRET^PHI`, section 3.2 is empty and the report grades `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`. Re-ingest is not an optimisation; it is what makes the fix reach data already indexed.

  **A candidate that does not verify is reported rather than narrowed into silence.** It keeps its bytes byte for byte, is held in the object graph exactly as before, and files a `SCAN_GAP` audit row. It is **not** a `DATA_LOSS` row: section 3.1 of the compliance report is headed "present in the source and **not** in the exported data", and these bytes were ingested whole -- a row that makes its own section header false is worse than no row. Section 3 is retitled **"Data Loss & Unscanned Content"** with `### 3.1 Data Loss` and `### 3.2 Unscanned Content` beneath it; sections 4 and 5 keep their numbers, which three test files assert verbatim. An empty 3.2 renders the sentence *"No unscanned content was recorded."* rather than an empty table.

  **Section 3.2 says what became of each element, because the row cannot.** The `SCAN_GAP` row is written by `DicomImporter` at ingest, where `remove_private_tags` has not been applied and no export has run. It used to end *"It was retained verbatim and exported"*, and under the shipped default `remove_private_tags=True` that was false: the sweep deletes the element, section 2 of the same report carries a `REMEDIATION_REMOVE` for it, the exported file has no `(0009,1003)` and none of the payload bytes, and section 3.2 went on saying it shipped. Two sections of one compliance report, describing the same element, disagreeing about whether it reached the output -- under the default configuration. The row now states ingest knowledge only (*"It was ingested verbatim; the PHI scan could not open it."*) and `generate_report` resolves the rest against the object graph into a **Disposition** column: `removed before export`, `retained for export`, or `unresolved`. The graph settles the question because `remove_private_tags` has exactly one consumer, `PhiInspector`, and the exporter applies no private filtering of its own -- an element still in the graph is one the next `export()` writes. It is a presence test at every depth, never a second run of the parse gate: which elements the gate refused was settled once, at ingest, and is read back off the row rather than decided again (#84). The wording is tenseless because the documented call order puts `generate_report` *before* `export()`.

  **The grade turns on the disposition, not on the row's existence.** A gap the sweep removed is content the export does not carry, which is the same test #146 applies to `DATA_LOSS`. Grading the row's presence made the report disagree with itself: measured on one adversarial 16-byte blob and one real private sequence, same `remove_private_tags=True`, both absent from the exported file, the parseable one graded `PASS` and the unparseable one `REVIEW_REQUIRED` -- an asymmetry with no argument behind it, since "we removed bytes we could not read" and "we removed a sequence we read" are the same attestation about the *export*. `retained for export` still costs the run its `PASS`, because a run that cannot vouch for what it exported does not get to call itself clean; so does `unresolved`, because a disposition nothing could establish is not a clean one.

  `SqliteStore.get_audit_scan_gaps()` returns `(timestamp, entity_uid, details, element_tag)` and `ComplianceReport.scan_gaps` holds `(timestamp, entity_uid, details, disposition)`; both are separate from the loss pair rather than a widening of it, for the same reason the sections are. `audit_log` gains an **`element_tag`** column -- set only on `SCAN_GAP` rows, by the emitter, and added to an existing store by `ALTER TABLE` -- for the reason `loss_scope` was added in #146: only the emitter still holds the tag, and reading it back out of `details` is a second answer to "which element is this". `SqliteStore.log_audit()` gains a matching `element_tag=` keyword and `log_audit_batch` entries are five-tuples. The reachable route to **`unresolved`** is an instance whose SOP Instance UID changed after the row was written -- `redact()` regenerates it -- so the row can no longer be matched back to a live instance; it keeps its `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, the safe side, even when the element was in fact swept and the export is clean. (A row with no tag at all also reads `unresolved`, defensively; no released store can hold one, since the action type itself is new in this release.) The `ALTER TABLE`'s load-bearing case is therefore not a row predating the column -- none can exist -- but the *store* that does: a 0.9.0-era database reopened under this release and re-ingested fails outright on the new INSERT naming `element_tag` unless the column has been added first.

  **The private-tag sweep had to learn about sequences in the same change, and doing so closes a second defect that was never about Implicit VR.** `_scan_instance` built its sweep targets from `item.attributes.keys()` alone, so a private *sequence* was never a candidate: measured on unmodified `main`, `remove_private_tags=True` -- the default -- swept the creator `(0009,0010)` and exported `(0009,1003)` anyway, an orphaned private sequence whose creator had been stripped off it. Under Explicit VR that has always been the behaviour. Under Implicit VR it was masked, and only by the bug above: the blob was an *attribute*, so the attribute sweep caught it. Restoring the structure removes the mask, so shipping the parse without the sweep would have been a regression in the default configuration -- which is why the two are one change. The sweep now asks the question directly, at every depth, and `RemediationService`'s `REMOVE_TAG` arm deletes from `entity.sequences` as well as `entity.attributes`. Its audit rows stay `REMEDIATION_REMOVE`; `audit_summary` counts action types, and a second spelling would split one behaviour across two rows of the report's section 2. The removals are appended **after** the configured-tag findings, not before: a finding raised inside the sequence holds a live reference to an item within it, and remediating the contents first means every audit row describes an item that was still in the graph when the row was written. Among themselves the removals run **deepest first**, for the same reason: a private sequence can hold another private sequence, `iter_item_tree` yields the container before its contents, and in that order remediation deletes the outer sequence and then files a `REMEDIATION_REMOVE` row for an inner one it deleted from a dict nothing could reach any more. The exported file is identical either way; the audit trail is not.

  **Two grade changes to expect on data that has not changed.** An instance whose private `UN` blob does not verify, *and whose blob the export still carries*, now grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED` where it graded `PASS`; swept away by the default `remove_private_tags=True`, it stays `PASS`. And an instance whose private sequence carries a **binary-VR child** -- a vendor `OB` inside the item -- now grades `REVIEW_REQUIRED` too: with the structure restored, that child goes through the ordinary rules, binary VRs do not reach the object graph, and it is reported as a private `DATA_LOSS`. The loss was always happening; before this it had nothing saying so, which is #137 one level down.

  `tests/test_private_sequence_implicit_vr.py` is new: 24 cases, of which 17 are red on `65dcb2e` -- including one of the two `remove_private_tags=True` legs, the Explicit VR one, which is the second defect above. Its Implicit VR fixtures are assembled by hand with `struct.pack` -- element header, value and item framing -- and handed to pydicom as `OB` so the write puts exactly those bytes on the wire; no helper supplies the value under test, and every assertion routes through `session.audit()` or `session.export()` rather than a direct `PhiInspector` call, which is #57's lesson. `tests/test_pydicom_deprecations.py` gains two: the gate's exact call shape must keep working and must announce no 4.0 removal, and `Dataset._character_set` must keep existing. `tests/test_data_loss_reporting.py` gains three, because section 3 now holds two tables: its `_loss_table` helper is bounded by the subheadings rather than by "Data Loss" and "Exceptions"; one of the new cases emits both sections at once, which no test in that file had ever done; and one reopens a hand-written pre-migration `audit_log` carrying a `SCAN_GAP` row, so disabling the `element_tag` `ALTER TABLE` is caught rather than trusted. That case asserts on the row *count* and the row *contents* rather than on the column width, deliberately: both tables became four columns wide the moment 3.2 gained Disposition, and a width test would have gone green on that change alone and stopped pinning the bounds it exists to pin. Both `DicomIO.is_little_endian` and `is_implicit_VR` are on pydicom's removal list and both are *required* here -- without them `read_sequence` raises `AttributeError: 'DicomBytesIO' object has no attribute '_tag_packer'` -- so a pydicom bump that removed them would make the gate return None for every sequence and #167 would come back reported as "this vendor block is unparseable" when the truth would be that our parser broke. (#167)

- **An instance whose declared pixels would not decode exported as a file carrying no pixel element at all, and the run graded `PASS`.** `Instance.get_pixel_data()` ended its `dcmread` arm with `except (AttributeError, TypeError): return None`, under a comment reading "No pixel data element". `Dataset.pixel_array` raises `AttributeError` for a family of reasons that are not that -- a missing Type 1 descriptor pydicom needs in order to reshape, most of all -- and every one of them was answered with "this instance has no pixels", silently, three modules away from anything that could report it. Measured on an ordinary `ingest -> examine -> anonymize -> export -> generate_report` of a Parametric Map declaring `SamplesPerPixel 3` with no `(0028,0006)` Planar Configuration, whose `.pixel_array` raises `AttributeError: Missing required element: (0028,0006) 'Planar Configuration'`: **one file written**, `Rows 4  Columns 4  SamplesPerPixel 3  BitsAllocated 32`, and `(7fe0,0010)`, `(7fe0,0008)` and `(7fe0,0009)` **all absent** -- a 4x4 three-sample 32-bit image with no pixel element of any kind. Float Pixel Data is Type 1 in the Floating Point Image Pixel Module (PS3.3 C.7.6.24), so that is a missing Type 1: the same "declares something it does not carry" shape #193 removed from this path and #160 removed from the waveform path, arriving through a different door. The report read `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`, `| Instances Written | 1 of 1 requested |` and *"No exceptions or errors were recorded."*

  **What now raises, exactly.** `session.export()` still does not raise -- the DICOM exporter returns `None`, and #181's `ExportSummary` is internal to the export path -- and the failure surfaces where a compliance run reads it: **zero files written**, one `ERROR` audit row reading `Export failed for <path>: Lazy load failed for <source>: Missing required element: (0028,0006) 'Planar Configuration'`, `| Instances Written | 0 of 1 requested |`, and `| **Validation Status** | **REVIEW_REQUIRED** |` with that row rendered into section 4, Exceptions & Errors. `DicomExporter.write_tree()` -- the serializer path with no session behind it, which the `scripts/` fixture generators use -- **does** raise, through its own wrapper: `RuntimeError("Export incomplete. 1 failed. First error: Lazy load failed for <source>: Missing required element: (0028,0006) 'Planar Configuration'")`. And `session.redact()` and `session.redact_by_machine()` now raise `RedactionError` for such an instance where they previously returned a count, with an `ERROR` row beside it -- #213's machinery, reached by one more failure shape. Calling `Instance.get_pixel_data()` directly raises `RuntimeError("Lazy load failed for <path>: <pydicom's message>")`.

  **The redaction half is the larger change and the reason it is not merely cosmetic.** Before, an instance whose declared pixels could not be decoded was *reported as successfully redacted while nothing had touched its pixels*: `get_pixel_data()` returned `None`, the worker took the "nothing to do" arm, no row was written and `redact()` returned. That is the same "reports success while wrong" defect as the export half, on the path that carries the PHI. Measured on the fixture above with `redact_by_machine`: before, no exception and no audit row; after, `RedactionError` plus one `ERROR` row naming Planar Configuration. Genuinely pixel-less instances are still a silent skip and always were -- the two cases had one answer between them and now have two.

  **The narrowing, stated exactly, because the class of exception is not ours.** Only one condition moves: *the dataset holds one of `(7fe0,0010)`, `(7fe0,0008)`, `(7fe0,0009)` and `.pixel_array` still raised `AttributeError` or `TypeError`*. Three behaviours are deliberately unchanged -- `dcmread` itself raising `AttributeError`/`TypeError` (still `None`, guarded by `ds is not None`), a file holding no pixel element (still `None`), and the `"no pixel data" in str(e)` arm below it (untouched). The check asks the **dataset**, not the exception message: matching on the text would be a fourth spelling of "is there pixel data" in that file, and it is not a reliable discriminator. `tests/test_io_no_pixels.py::test_reproduce_no_pixel_data_crash` is the anchor for the unchanged half and stays green.

  **Of the three tags, only the two float ones are reachable here from an ordinary ingest**, and the reason is worth writing down because the sentence above reads as though all three were. `ingest_worker` decodes `.pixel_array` for every instance carrying `(7fe0,0010)` in order to fill the sidecar, and `import_files` turns a failure into `logger.error("Import Failed: ...")` and a `continue` -- so an *integer* file whose pixels will not decode never becomes an instance at all, and its pixels afterwards come from `SidecarPixelLoader`, a different arm of `get_pixel_data()` that already raised. Measured, an 8-bit `(7fe0,0010)` source with `SamplesPerPixel 3` and no `(0028,0006)`: zero instances after `ingest()`, no audit row, and identical before and after this change. The float pair is never sidecar'd (`ingest_worker` routes on `if "PixelData" in ds`), which is why the float arms reach the narrowed `except` from a plain `session.ingest()` while `(7fe0,0010)` reaches it only from a graph carrying a `file_path` and no loader. The tag stays in the check -- that shape is real, `tests/test_redaction_failure_is_reported.py` builds one -- but the ingest-side silent drop is a separate gap and is not this entry's subject.

  **The strongest objection, and why it is priced rather than answered away.** The condition is "a pixel element is present", not "the error means what we think" -- so the class of `AttributeError` now propagating is defined by pydicom and not by us, and a future pydicom that raises `AttributeError` somewhere new on a pixel-bearing dataset turns working exports into failed ones with no warning. That is the intended contract rather than a side effect: "a pixel element is present and this library could not decode it" is the condition that must never be reported as success, whatever the reason, and the alternative -- failing only *some* undecodable instances -- requires enumerating pydicom's error taxonomy, which #226 explicitly declines. The `pydicom<4.0` cap in `setup.py` bounds the surface in the meantime.

  **Widening the export-side modality guard instead was ruled out on measurement, not taste**, and the issue's own diagnosis is what recommends it. #226 reads "`Modality` is `OT` for a Parametric Map, which is not in `_IMAGE_MODALITIES`, so the export proceeds". `"OT"` **is** in `_IMAGE_MODALITIES` and has been since #208. The export proceeds because the guard sits inside `except FileNotFoundError:` and `get_pixel_data()` *returns* `None` rather than raising, so the guard is never consulted at all. Hoisting it to cover every `arr is None` turns `tests/test_io_no_pixels.py` red: its fixture is a CT Defined Procedure Protocol Storage instance -- genuinely non-image -- that declares no Modality, so the `"OT"` default puts it in the set and a legitimately pixel-less instance is refused. The guard is also in the wrong place to answer the question: by the export site, `arr is None` has already lost the distinction between "no pixels declared" and "pixels declared, undecodable". Only `get_pixel_data()` can still see it, so that is where the distinction now lives. `_IMAGE_MODALITIES` and the `except FileNotFoundError` arm are untouched.

  **Migration.** An export that newly fails was writing a file that declared an image it did not carry; the remedy is to fix the source instance -- here, supply `(0028,0006)`, or correct `SamplesPerPixel` -- not to suppress the error. A caller who genuinely wants the old behaviour has to decide, per instance, whether an undecodable pixel element is acceptable output, which is the decision the silent `None` was making on their behalf.

  Verified red on the base and green after, on 3.12.13 and 3.14.7t (`sys._is_gil_enabled()` asserted `False` inside the process after imports), pydicom 3.0.2: the export failure and its audit row, the report's grade and its Exceptions & Errors section, and the `RedactionError`. The two selectivity guards -- a file with no pixel element still returning `None`, and `tests/test_io_no_pixels.py` -- are green on both sides and are not evidence; the first goes red under an unconditional `raise` and the second goes red under the rejected modality-guard fix, which is what each is for. `tests/test_float_pixel_data_export.py`'s `_write_float_src` keeps writing `PlanarConfiguration` at `samples > 1` behind a new `planar=True` switch, because exactly one test (the #222 three-samples case) needs a *decodable* source to reach its subject; its comment is corrected, since it claimed that test "passes for the wrong reason" without the element when in fact it fails one assertion later, at `(0x7FE0, 0x0008) in exported`. (#226)
- **An ordinary `ingest -> export` of an ordinary Parametric Map wrote three elements PS3.5 says shall not be present, and deleted nobody's copy of the fourth element in the same sentence.** PS3.5 **Section 8.2** -- not A.1, which is the Implicit VR Little Endian Transfer Syntax and says nothing about pixel elements -- verbatim: "Float Pixel Data (7FE0,0008) is sent in Native Format; the Value Representation shall be OF, Bits Allocated (0028,0100) shall be 32, Bits Stored (0028,0101), High Bit (0028,0102) and Pixel Representation (0028,0103) shall not be present", and the same sentence again for Double Float Pixel Data at 64 bits. PS3.3 C.7.6.24 agrees structurally: none of the three appears in Table C.7.6.24-1 at all. Measured on a Parametric Map pydicom itself writes, run through `session.ingest()` then `session.export(..., use_compression=False)`: `BitsAllocated 32 BitsStored 32 HighBit 31 PixelRepresentation 0` beside `(7fe0,0008)`, and `64 / 64 / 63 / 0` beside `(7fe0,0009)`. No `DATA_LOSS` row, no `ERROR` row, `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`. Unlike most of #216's blast radius this needs no hand-built graph -- it is what a real Parametric Map does on the documented path.

  **They came through `_merge`, and the float branch never wrote one of them, so "stop writing them" was never available.** `ds.BitsStored`/`ds.HighBit`/`ds.PixelRepresentation` are assigned only in the *integer* block, which the float branch cannot reach -- it ends `arr = None` and the integer block is guarded by `if arr is not None:`. `populate_attrs` skips group `7fe0` at ingest and nothing in group `0028`, so all three arrive from the source file and `_merge` puts them on the outgoing dataset. Proved by mutation rather than by reading: a source declaring `BitsStored 16, HighBit 15` beside a `float32` payload exports `BitsAllocated 32 BitsStored 16 HighBit 15` -- the *source's* values, not a `default_bits`-shaped 32/31 -- and a source declaring none of the three exports none of them and still decodes, `(4, 4) float32 [0.5 1.5 2.5]`. They are now deleted from the outgoing dataset inside the `itemsize in (4, 8)` arm, the one that actually wrote a pixel element; the float16 arm writes none, so nothing there forbids them and it keeps all three.

  **Deleted from `ds`, never from `inst.attributes`, and with no loss row.** The graph is re-exportable and `SidecarPixelLoader` reads `"0028,0103"` to reconstruct dtype, so a graph-side deletion would be a read-path write and a real loss on the integer path. No `DATA_LOSS` row because deleting these three removes nothing a recipient could want: C.7.6.24's own words are that Bits Stored and High Bit "are not used because the stored pixel values always occupy the entire word" and Pixel Representation "is not used because the stored pixel values are always signed", so their content is fixed by the standard rather than by the source file. `BitsAllocated` is in the same sentence, is fixed the same way, and this branch has silently overwritten *it* since #170 -- one sentence, one class of element, one silent action. The argument that does **not** hold, and is not being made: "the values were wrong anyway". The measured case above is internally consistent -- 32/31/0 beside `BitsAllocated 32` -- and forbidden regardless.

  **Pixel Data Provider URL (0028,7FE0) is deleted on both branches, and that one files a `DATA_LOSS` row.** Section 8.2's third sentence: "It is not permitted to have more than one of Pixel Data Provider URL (0028,7FE0), Pixel Data (7FE0,0010), Float Pixel Data (7FE0,0008) or Double Float Pixel Data (7FE0,0009) in the top level Data Set." #216 closed three directions of that exclusion and left this one open, on the stated ground that removing a caller's element owes them a loss row; #224 declined it for the same reason. This is that decision landing rather than the ground being abandoned. Measured before: `float32` source plus a declared `(0028,7fe0)` exported `(7fe0,0008)` **and** the URL, `uint8` exported `(7fe0,0010)` **and** the URL, `audit_log` empty in both. The row is scoped `STANDARD` -- group `0028` is even, the parity rule every other loss row uses -- so per #146 it is visible in section 3 of the compliance report and does not by itself move `validation_status`. One consequence is named rather than left to be discovered: `_report_export_losses` files loss rows without checking the outcome's `ok`, so a loss recorded before a *later* per-instance failure describes a file that was never written -- pre-existing (#187's era), but this URL row is the first such loss reachable from an ordinary ingest, which is why it is now tracked as #240.

  **The asymmetry between the two halves is reachability, not "derived vs. declared".** `populate_attrs` skips the whole `7fe0` group, so a second *pixel* element can only arrive from a hand-built graph, and a file carrying two of them is one pydicom refuses to read back at all. `(0028,7FE0)` has VR `UR`, is not binary, and survives `populate_attrs` -- it comes straight through an ordinary ingest, and deleting it takes a URL the exported file does not otherwise carry. Refusing the export instead was rejected on #193's precedent: the exporter has the real bytes in hand and the URL is a stale pointer to pixels held elsewhere, so a refusal would fail an ordinary Parametric Map over an element the file no longer needs. The existing silent `del ds.PixelData` and `del ds[other]` deletions owe a row by that same test and are deliberately left alone here; that is a decision of its own.

  **The gate on the URL deletion is structural, and must stay that way.** With `use_compression=True` the worker never assigns `ds.PixelData` at all -- `_finalize_dataset` compresses from the array -- so `if "PixelData" in ds` would be false on every compressed integer export and the URL would survive there. The deletions are gated on *being inside* the integer `if arr is not None:` block and the float `itemsize in (4, 8)` arm instead. Measured on both: `uint8 + URL` compressed exports `TS 1.2.840.10008.1.2.4.90`, no URL, a row filed, decoding `(4, 4) uint8 [0 1 2]`.

  **An instance with no pixel element keeps its URL**, because Section 8.2 forbids more than one of the four rather than forbidding the URL: a `Modality SR` instance with no array exports the URL and files no row. Round-trip is stable -- source -> export -> re-ingest -> export leaves generation 1 and generation 2 identical, with the three absent both times and the geometry intact, since the float pair is never sidecar'd and `get_pixel_data()` re-reads the source file. Nothing that used to succeed now fails: the full suite is green on 3.12.13 and 3.14.7t, and the strip was run against Parametric Map, Secondary Capture, MR Image and CT Image at both float widths with byte-identical `IODValidator` output before and after. (#223)

- **A rule with N redaction zones applied only its Nth zone to any instance loaded from a saved store, reported every image updated, and graded `PASS`.** `SidecarPixelLoader` builds its array with `np.frombuffer` over an immutable `bytes` buffer, so an instance whose pixels come back from the sidecar hands out a **read-only** array -- the ordinary shape for the documented `ingest -> save -> reopen -> redact` workflow. Both redaction paths fetched that array once and looped `for roi in rois:`, and the per-zone wrapper opened with `if not arr.flags.writeable: arr = arr.copy(); inst.set_pixel_data(arr)`. **That rebind is local to the callee.** The next iteration was handed the caller's `arr` again -- still the pristine read-only original -- so zone 2 copied the untouched source afresh and `set_pixel_data` replaced the instance's array with it, discarding everything zone 1 had written. Nothing raised. `modified` was `True`, `_apply_redaction_flags` ran, the `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` was written, and `redact()` returned the full count.

  **"Only the Nth zone" is the optimistic reading; the measured rule was "only the last *applicable* zone".** Each zone's fresh copy came from the pristine original whether or not that zone changed anything, and `apply_redaction_to_array` `continue`s past a zone starting past the image edge without raising. So a rule pairing a valid zone with a trailing off-image one reverted the valid zone and applied nothing at all. Measured on `4507d48`, same 32x32 fixture: `[[0,8,0,8],[100,200,100,200]]` left the store at `total = 204800` -- every pixel still 200, **not one zone applied** -- with `applied = 1`, a real `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` and `(0028,0301) BurnedInAnnotation = NO`. Reversing those two zones produced a correct image, so whether a rule redacted anything at all depended on the order its zones happened to be written in. The same pair now reads `total = 192000`. (A rule whose zones are *all* off-image still reports `applied: 1` with a `None` hash; that is #235, unchanged here and byte-identical before and after.)

  **It reached disk, and it graded `PASS`.** Measured on 3.12.13 with two disjoint zones `[[0,8,0,8],[16,24,16,24]]` on a 32x32 image filled with 200: `redact returned 1`, `zone1 sum 12800` (untouched), `zone2 sum 0`. Anonymized, redacted, saved, exported and reported, the exported DICOM read `zone1 = 12800` on disk under `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`. The grade followed from the mechanism rather than from luck -- nothing raised, so no `ERROR` row; nothing was dropped, so no `DATA_LOSS` row. Identical figures on 3.14.7t (`sys._is_gil_enabled()` asserted `False` inside the process): the defect is arithmetic on names inside one stack frame, so the threads and processes executors reproduce it the same way.

  **What was hiding it is `session.export()` repairing the damage on the way out.** `_export_instance_worker` re-applies `ctx.redaction_zones` in a **single** `apply_redaction_to_array` call, so an export running under the same configuration silently rewrites the file correctly. That repair is unavailable on every other ordinary route: a second session that loaded the store without the config, `DicomExporter.write_tree()` (#78, which applies no zones at all), a serial that no longer matches at export time, or simply *reading the saved store* -- `save()` persists the half-redacted array to the sidecar, so the stored dataset carries the identifier whatever a later export does.

  **Upgrading does not repair a store that was already redacted, and re-running `redact()` will not either.** Both callers open with `if current_hash == config_hash: skip`, and `config_hash` is md5 over `{serial, sorted(rois)}` -- the *configuration*, not the pixels. So the hash a damaged instance is carrying is exactly the hash the fixed code computes for the same rule. Measured across the two versions on one database: redacted under `4507d48` the store holds `zone1 = 12800` and `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH = b3889c5b...`; reopened under this version with the identical rule, `redact()` returns **0**, the instance is skipped, and `zone1` is still `12800`. Remediating an affected store meant deleting `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` from its instances (or changing the rule, or re-ingesting from source) before redacting again; that recipe is superseded in this same unreleased section: the attestation's pixel-independence was filed as #237, and its entry under `### Changed` above -- `session.redact(force=True)` -- now does the repair without the user knowing an internal private-tag key name.

  **`_apply_roi_to_instance` is deleted rather than patched, because the loop was the defect.** `RedactionService._redact_instance_pixels(inst, arr, rois)` replaces it: it owns the writeability copy and makes exactly **one** `apply_redaction_to_array(arr, rois, geometry=...)` call with the whole zone list, which is the shape `_export_instance_worker` already used. Both callers lose their `for roi in rois:` loop, so there is no longer a loop for the aliasing to live in and no per-zone entry point to call in one. Hoisting the copy into the two callers and keeping a per-ROI wrapper was rejected: it fixes the loss but leaves a method named for managing an instance that no longer does, keeps N-1 redundant full-array copies, and leaves the loop in place for the next person to add a rebind to. Pre-1.0, so the old name is gone rather than aliased. It was private; the three in-tree test call sites pass `[roi]`.

  **The whole suite missed this because no redaction test anywhere exercised a reloaded instance.** Every redaction fixture in `tests/` was either built in memory (`inst.set_pixel_data(np.full(...))`) or read from a source file through pydicom, and **both give a writeable array**, where the code takes its other arm, mutates in place, and is correct. Closing that gap is half of this change: `tests/conftest.py` gains `reloaded_redaction_session`, a factory that builds, saves, closes and reopens a session and **asserts `get_pixel_data().flags.writeable is False`** before handing the instance back. That assertion is the fixture's guard on itself -- give the instance a `file_path` and pydicom returns a writeable array, at which point every test built on it goes vacuous rather than red, which is exactly how this survived. The fixture's instance is deliberately exportable (SC Image Storage plus `0008,0016`, `0008,0030`, `0008,0060`, `0028,0004`), because `export()` does not raise on a module-validation failure: a non-exportable fixture leaves an empty output tree and any test walking it iterates an empty list and passes.

  **A behaviour change on the failure path, which the issue does not mention.** `apply_redaction_to_array` raises mid-loop, and with the copy now made once, zones 1..k-1 **are** zeroed in the instance's array when zone k raises -- where before, zone k's fresh copy of the pristine original meant no partial mutation could form on a reloaded instance at all. The end state is unchanged, by a three-step chain: the `finally` block's persist gate does not run on a failure (#213), so nothing partial reaches the sidecar; `inst.unload_pixel_data()` then runs unconditionally and **succeeds**, because `set_pixel_data` does not clear `_pixel_loader`; and the next `get_pixel_data()` reloads the original through the loader. Measured on the fixed code: at the unload the instance's array reads `zone1 = 0`, after it the reload reads `zone1 = 12800`, and after `save()`/`close()`/reopen the store holds `12800`. The one shape this cannot reach is the one #213 already names -- a graph built in memory with neither loader nor `file_path`, where `unload_pixel_data()` correctly refuses -- and there the window widens from "the last zone only" to "every zone before the failure", in the direction of removing *more* PHI. That is the accepted reasoning unchanged: zeroing is monotone, the instance carries no hash, and the next run retries it.

  **This corrects an inference in the entry below.** That entry read the same measurement and concluded "there is no partial mutation to persist -- the gate is a no-op there rather than a fix", and filed the multi-zone loss as #229 in the same breath. The measurement was right both times; the inference that a fresh copy per zone was merely an inconvenience for that test's shape was not. `tests/test_redaction_failure_is_reported.py`'s `_write_source` and `_hydrated` docstrings said the same thing and are rewritten, because a reloaded instance can now hold a partial mutation and so detects the persist gate too. Test 18 in that file is the reloaded case, parametrised over both executor levers; it builds on `reloaded_redaction_session` rather than on `_hydrated`, whose own callers all use one-zone rules and so still cannot ask the question.

  **The method's *return value* is guarded too, because it was the one thing the suite did not check.** `_redact_instance_pixels`'s bool gates `_apply_redaction_flags`, `regenerate_uid()` and the `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` in both callers, so a wrong `True` is an attestation that a redaction happened when none did. Measured: replacing the body's `return self.apply_redaction_to_array(...)` with a call plus an unconditional `return True` left the **whole suite green (964 passed, 1 skipped)** while a rule whose zones are all off-image gained a real hash and `BurnedInAnnotation NO` on pixels nothing had touched. `test_a_rule_that_applied_nothing_returns_False_and_attests_nothing` closes that, and is labelled a selectivity guard: it needs the new method to exist, so it cannot be run against `4507d48` and is not evidence the multi-zone loss was fixed. It asserts the return value and the absence of the attestation only -- that run's *reporting* (`applied: 1`, `(0028,0301)` written as `None`) is #235 and is deliberately left alone.

  **`reloaded_redaction_session`'s `name` defaults per call, not to a constant.** It names the database file, and two `make()` calls in one test shared `tmp_path/reload.db` *and* `uid` -- so the second reopened the first's store and the lookup could hand back the first call's instance. That is the vacuous-fixture failure the fixture exists to prevent, reintroduced by its own defaults.

  Verified red on `4507d48` and green after, on 3.12.13 and 3.14.7t: both zone sums after `redact()` (parallel path), the same through `redact_machine_instances`, both zones in the exported file, and a `set_pixel_data` counter reading `1` rather than `3` for a three-zone rule. That last one is the clause a fix that repairs the aliasing but keeps the loop would fail. **Zone order is pinned in both directions**, because the measurement above is the claim: `[valid, off-image]` is red on `4507d48` like the others, and `[off-image, valid]` was green there and is covered anyway -- it is the only shape in the suite with an applicable zone *after* a skipped one, and turning `apply_redaction_to_array`'s `continue` past an off-image zone into a `break` left the whole suite green while reinstating the order dependence this entry says was removed. (#229)
- **A redaction zone that could not be applied left the burned-in identifier in the pixels, and every reporting surface said the run was clean.** `RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array` re-raises `(ValueError, IndexError, TypeError)` deliberately, under a comment saying that a silent skip ships the unredacted image. One frame up, `execute_redaction_task` and `redact_machine_instances` each caught bare `Exception` and only logged -- so the raise travelled exactly one stack frame. `modified` stayed `False`, `_apply_redaction_flags` never ran, `_ISOCENTER_REDACTION_HASH` was never set, **no audit row was written**, `session.redact()` returned a number, and the instance exported with its identifier intact and graded `PASS`. Measured on one instance with the zone `[0, "abc", 0, 4]`: `redact returned 0`, `pixel[0,0] = 200`, `hash = None`, `audit errors = 0`.

  **The exception this raises is `isocenter.RedactionError`**, from `session.redact()`, `RedactionService.redact_machine_instances` and `RedactionService.process_machine_rules`, at the end of a pass in which any instance's zone could not be applied -- where the call previously returned the count of the instances that *did* work, or `None`. It carries `.failures` (`[(sop_uid, detail)]`) and `.attempted`. `session.redact()`'s return type is unchanged: it is still the `int` count of instances updated, and it is now returned only on a run where nothing that was tried failed. **This honours a contract the docstring already stated** rather than inventing one -- `redact()` has documented `Raises: ... a failure here must reach the caller` since #150, and only *setup* failures honoured it.

  **The raise comes last, and the ordering is the design.** Every task is dispatched, every successful mutation is applied to the graph, every failure is audited, `scan_burned_in_annotations()` runs and writes its `RISK` rows, and the console summary prints -- and only then does the exception leave. A caller that catches it still has a correct object graph and a compliance report it can generate. That is also why the worker returns an outcome rather than raising: `_apply_redaction_rules` consumes `run_parallel(..., return_generator=True)` incrementally, so an exception escaping a worker terminates that generator mid-iteration and every mutation queued behind it is lost -- the images that *were* redacted would silently never reach the graph.

  **`ERROR`, not `DATA_LOSS`, for two independent reasons.** Nothing was dropped: the burned-in identifier is *present*, and being present is the problem, which is a failed operation and exactly what `_report_export_failures` writes `ERROR` for. And a `DATA_LOSS` row is graded by `loss_scope` -- `STANDARD` leaves the run at `PASS`, the opposite of what is needed, and `PRIVATE` would be a lie about a tag that does not exist. An `ERROR` row lands in `get_audit_errors()`, populates `report.exceptions`, and takes `validation_status` to `REVIEW_REQUIRED`. The write happens in the **parent**, not the worker: `SqliteStore.__getstate__` drops the queue, the stop event and the audit thread, and `__setstate__` starts a new thread in the child that is torn down at pool shutdown without `stop()` -- so a queued row can be lost, and for a `:memory:` database the child writes nowhere at all. Same reason `_report_export_failures` runs in the parent (#126).

  **The same failure used to leave two different stores, decided by the interpreter, and now leaves one.** `apply_redaction_to_array` applies zones in a loop and raises *mid-loop*, so zones 1..k-1 are already zeroed when zone k fails, and `execute_redaction_task`'s `finally` then persisted unconditionally. Measured with zones `[[0,8,0,8],[1,"abc",0,8]]` on a file-backed instance -- the ordinary ingested shape, and the one whose array pydicom hands back writeable: on the threads path -- which is 3.14t's default -- zone 1 came back **zeroed and persisted**; on the processes path -- 3.12's default -- the instance was untouched and the worker's mutated copy went into the shared sidecar as orphan bytes the parent never pointed at. Both CI gate versions ran and they disagreed. The persist is now gated on the task not having failed; `unload_pixel_data()` stays unconditional, so the mutated array is dropped and the next `get_pixel_data()` re-reads the original from the source file. Two shapes this gate does not decide, both measured. An instance whose pixels come back through a `SidecarPixelLoader` gets a **read-only** array, so every zone is applied to a fresh copy and there is no partial mutation to persist -- the gate is a no-op there rather than a fix. *A fresh copy per zone is copied from the pristine original*, which is a separate, live defect and not a consequence of this change: a rule carrying N zones applies only the Nth to such an instance, silently. Filed as #229; measured identically on `258331c`. And an instance with neither a loader nor a `file_path` -- one built in memory and redacted in the same session, which is what `session.save()` leaves behind, because saving does *not* attach a loader -- keeps the zones applied before the failure, because `unload_pixel_data()` refuses when clearing would be a silent discard. That last case is accepted: zeroing is monotone, so a partial redaction has removed *more* PHI than none, and it carries no hash, so the next run retries it. Fixing it with a pre-image copy of every array is exactly the resident-memory cost the lazy-pixel design exists to avoid, and is refused.

  **`execute_redaction_task` returned `None` for three different situations** -- already redacted under this configuration, no pixel data to redact, and it blew up -- and the parent read all three as "nothing to apply". `RedactionOutcome` splits them, and the parent treats an unrecognised result, **including a bare `None`**, as a failure rather than a skip: tolerating one would re-create the same conflation and would let a stub go on passing against a contract it no longer implements. The first two remain silent skips, as does a rule with no zones or no matching instances.

  **This closes the item the #186/#205 entry below already filed** as "a second path into #213 -- that method's `except Exception` swallows the re-raise #66 added and documents as never to be swallowed -- which is filed, not fixed here". A `(5,8,4)` array on an instance declaring `SamplesPerPixel=3` now produces the same `ERROR` row and the same raise as a malformed zone does.

  **The two public ways to write DICOM are both closed, by different mechanisms.** `session.export()` refuses the file: `_export_instance_worker` re-applies `ctx.redaction_zones` to the array it is about to write, so a zone that failed in `redact()` raises again in the worker and the write becomes `ExportOutcome(ok=False)` -- an `ERROR` row, `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, and nothing on disk. What was untested is the *failure* half of that: two tests already covered the re-application on the working path -- `test_export_contract.py::test_an_export_plan_carries_the_configured_redaction_zones` asserts the plan carries the zones, and `test_redaction_export.py::test_export_compressed_redaction` exports a successfully redacted instance and reads the zeroed pixels back -- and neither says anything about a zone that raises. `test_a_zone_that_could_not_be_applied_never_reaches_disk` does, and is measured against the mutation: with `redaction_zones=[]` at the single population site (`DicomSession._build_export_tasks`) the failed instance exports a file reading 200 across the region the rule was meant to zero, on both gate interpreters.

  **`DicomExporter.write_tree()` is closed by the raise instead, and cannot be closed any other way.** It is the serializer, not the pipeline: it takes no configuration, sets no `redaction_zones`, and writes the graph exactly as it stands -- which after a failed redaction is the pristine original, because the persist gate above leaves it that way. Before this change `redact()` returned normally and a `write_tree()` behind it put the identifier on disk and graded `PASS`, the same leak as the export route and a second instance of it rather than a general serializer gap: `write_tree()` carries a *successful* redaction correctly. What closes it is that `redact()` now raises, upstream of any call to it. A caller that swallows `RedactionError` and serializes anyway still gets the `ERROR` row and the `REVIEW_REQUIRED` grade, and that is the whole of what this entry can offer a path with no pipeline behind it.

  `apply_redaction_to_array`'s `#66` raise is unchanged. Its *signature* is not -- see the next entry. And the comment above that raise was half wrong and is corrected in place: the **export worker's** call (`io_handlers._export_instance_worker`) always did propagate, becoming `ExportOutcome(ok=False)` with no file written; the two callers the comment did not name are the ones that swallowed. (#213)
- **`RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array`'s `geometry` argument is required, and the heuristic behind its default is deleted rather than kept as a fallback.** A call that omitted it worked until now and raises `TypeError: RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array() missing 1 required positional argument: 'geometry'` from here on. The annotation narrows from `Optional[PixelGeometry]` to `PixelGeometry` as well as losing the default: leaving `Optional` while removing the default is a self-contradicting signature, telling a reader that `None` is a legal value while the interpreter says the argument is mandatory.

  **Why the old behaviour was wrong.** The default arm was `ndim >= 3 and arr.shape[-1] in [3, 4]`, which cannot tell `(frames, rows, cols)` from `(rows, cols, samples)` -- the same guess the #186/#205 entry below removed from four other sites. On a 4-frame 8x4 volume with an identifier burned into rows 6-7 of every frame, the zone `(6, 8, 0, 4)` addressed frames `6:8` of a 4-frame array, an empty slice, so **all 32 identifier cells reached the exported file while `redact()` reported "1 of 1 images updated"**. Reproduced during #215's review: 32/32 surviving on `692218c`, 0/32 on `cf9dcd6`. There was no way for a third-party caller to get the *correct* behaviour out of the default, so nothing usable is taken away -- and the direction of the failure is the whole trade: the heuristic failed **open**, zeroing the wrong region and reporting success, where a missing argument fails **closed**, with nothing zeroed, nothing persisted, no hash written, an `ERROR` row filed, and the pass raising.

  **The migration is one line**, and it is what both in-tree callers already do: `from isocenter.pixel_geometry import resolve_pixel_geometry`, then pass `geometry=resolve_pixel_geometry(arr.shape, instance.attributes)` -- or `{}` where there are no declared descriptors. Resolve it rather than constructing a `PixelGeometry` literal; the resolver is the invariant the axis selection depends on.

  **This supersedes the sentence in the `#186, #205` entry below** reading "A caller of the public static `RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array` outside this tree keeps the old behaviour: the resolved geometry arrives as a defaulted keyword argument". That sentence is corrected in place rather than left to disagree, and it is corrected rather than "withdrawn one release later" because the promise never shipped: #215 landed after v0.9.0 was tagged, so both entries are unreleased and no published version ever made it.

  **The missing-argument `TypeError` is caught and audited by the redaction paths like any other failure, deliberately, and the catch stays broad.** A malformed zone in a JSON config raises `TypeError` from inside the loop too -- measured, `(0, None, 0, 8)` and `(0, [1], 0, 8)` both do, where `(0, "abc", 0, 8)` raises `ValueError` -- so narrowing the catch to make room for the programming error would drop real failed redactions and re-open #213. The two are separable by walking `exc.__traceback__` (a binding error has no frame for the callee) and that is **refused**: a frame-name string comparison in the middle of a PHI-safety path breaks under any rename or decorator. The presentation is acceptable because a missing argument fails *every* instance in the pass, loudly -- an `ERROR` row each, `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, and `RedactionError` out of the public entry point -- and the row reads `TypeError: RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array() missing 1 required positional argument: 'geometry'`, which names the caller's bug rather than implying a finding about the data. An explicit `geometry=None` remains a legal call and dies at `geometry.samples` with `AttributeError`, before the loop, and is caught by the same broad handler; that is the residue, and it fails closed. (#217)
- **The compliance report could grade `PASS` a run that dropped a private tag, because the row was still in flight when it looked.** `SqliteStore` writes its audit log asynchronously: `log_audit()` puts a tuple on `audit_queue` and a background `AuditWorker` writes it. The worker took rows off the queue into a **local `batch` list** and called `log_audit_batch` afterwards, so between those two points a row was in neither the queue nor the database -- it was owned by a local variable no other thread could see. `get_audit_errors()` and `get_audit_losses()` settled the log by calling `flush_audit_queue()`, which was a queue drain and nothing else: it found the queue empty, wrote nothing, returned in 0.00 s, and the `SELECT` that followed ran without the row.

  **`get_audit_losses()` is the one that carries the grade, which is why this is a compliance defect and not a missing log line.** `generate_report` grades on `row[3] == LOSS_SCOPE_PRIVATE`: a `DATA_LOSS` scoped `PRIVATE` takes `validation_status` to `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, one scoped `STANDARD` leaves it at `PASS` (#146). A row missed in flight does not omit a line, it awards a passing grade. Measured on `258331c` with one `DATA_LOSS`/`PRIVATE` row and one `ERROR` row provably mid-write: `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`, with neither the loss detail nor the error detail anywhere in the report. That is the outcome #146 and #181 were fixed to prevent.

  **`flush_audit_queue()` is a barrier now, rather than a second method being added beside it.** The name already promised the guarantee -- "flush the audit queue" is what a reader believes it does -- and every existing caller (`stop()`, both racy readers, four test sites) was relying on a guarantee it did not give, so strengthening the primitive fixes all of them at once and adds no second spelling. The invariant is that **no audit row is ever owned by anything other than the queue or the database, except by a thread holding `_audit_write_lock`**. Rows leave the queue only under that lock and are in the database before it is released, so a thread that takes the lock and finds the queue empty knows every row enqueued before it is readable. The worker no longer dequeues on its own: it waits on `_audit_wakeup` owning nothing and calls the same `_drain_and_write()` the barrier does. Blocking on `audit_queue.get(timeout=1.0)` could not be kept, and shrinking that window would not have been a fix -- `get()` returns a row into a local name *before* any lock can be taken, so the gap narrows and never closes.

  **Producers take neither lock**, so `log_audit()` is still never blocked by a database write; that is what the async architecture buys and the fix does not spend it. The barrier has **no timeout**, deliberately: a compliance read that quietly gives up cannot be told apart from one that found nothing, which is precisely the failure described below. What that costs is bounded per **acquisition**, not per **call**: one acquisition is one `log_audit_batch` -- at most 100 rows plus a commit -- and producers never hold the lock, so no volume of logging can extend it. But `_drain_and_write` releases between batches and re-acquires, and exits only on a short batch, so under sustained logging a reader loops past many batches and the total call is unbounded. That unboundedness is inherited rather than introduced -- the old `flush_audit_queue` drained until the queue was empty too -- but it is stated here per-acquisition because the reassuring version of the sentence is what would justify never revisiting it. One residual follows on a `:memory:` store, where `log_audit_batch` waits on `_memory_lock`: an unbounded barrier can park a reader -- or `close()`, which flushes -- behind the worker blocked behind a `PersistenceManager` save. #164 was a `:memory:` deadlock on this codebase, so the shape is worth naming rather than leaving in the spec; this is a wait, not a cycle, and the lock order below is what keeps it one. The lock order is `_audit_write_lock` -> `_memory_lock`, always -- `log_audit_batch` reaches `_get_connection`, which takes `_memory_lock` on a `:memory:` store -- so all three readers keep the flush **above** the `with self._get_connection()` block; inverting it would deadlock. For the same reason `log_audit_batch` must never acquire `_audit_write_lock`: the worker calls it while holding one, `threading.Lock` is not reentrant, and the obvious defensive hardening would self-deadlock on the first row.

  **`get_audit_summary()` was not the safe reader the survey took it for.** It settled the log by calling `stop()`, which joins the worker with `join(timeout=2.0)` -- a race with a two-second head start, not a barrier, and the head start is a property of the machine. Amplify the batch write past the join and the measured behaviour is `{}` returned for a store with an `ERROR` row recorded, silently, plus a leaked thread: the `finally` cleared `_stop_event` and started a *new* worker, the old one re-checked a stop event that had just been cleared and never exited, and the `AuditWorker` count went 1 -> 2 and stayed. One leaked thread per timed-out read. The `stop()`/restart block is deleted, not repaired; the leak is fixed by removing the restart, not by improving the join. `stop()` keeps its join as a shutdown concern, and a timed-out join no longer loses rows because the `flush_audit_queue()` that follows waits out the in-flight write.

  Three behaviour changes follow, and they are named here rather than left to be discovered. Two are improvements. **`generate_report()` no longer stops and restarts the audit worker** -- it used to leave a different thread running than the one it found. And **`get_audit_summary()` no longer resurrects a stopped worker**: calling it after `close()` used to bring the subsystem quietly back up.

  **The third is not an improvement: the audit worker no longer retries a failed batch write, and those rows are dropped where they used to be retried** ([#219](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/219)). The old worker owned `batch` across loop iterations and cleared it *only after a successful write*, so a raising `log_audit_batch` left the rows in place and the next iteration wrote them. `batch` is now created fresh inside every lock acquisition, so an exception escaping the write propagates out of `_drain_and_write` to the worker's `except`, which logs and continues -- and the rows have already left the queue. **It cannot simply be reverted**, which is why it ships named rather than fixed: rows held in a worker-local `batch` across iterations are exactly the "owned by a local variable no reader can see" state the barrier exists to make impossible, so restoring the old retry reopens the defect above. A retry that preserved the invariant would have to put the rows *back on the queue under the lock*. The net loss surface still **narrows**: `log_audit_batch` already swallowed `sqlite3.Error` itself before this change, so the dominant failure path was lossy already and the retry only ever covered the non-sqlite tail -- an `OSError`/`MemoryError` from the connection, or a malformed tuple, which nothing in the package enqueues today.

  **It also removes an accident that no test could have caught.** `generate_report()` read the summary at `session.py:1497` and the two racy readers at `:1498` and `:1499`. Line 1497 joined and restarted the worker, so by 1498 the queue was drained and the restarted worker's batch was empty: the two racy reads were protected by a side effect of the line above them, which nothing stated and nothing enforced. Swapping those three lines, or dropping the summary call, re-opened the window on the grade with the whole suite still green. Deleting the `stop()` removes the dependence **structurally** -- there is no longer a line with a settling side effect to depend on -- rather than documenting it, and no comment is added at `session.py:1497-1499`, because a comment there would be a second home for an invariant that now lives in one place.

  `tests/test_audit_read_barrier.py` pins it, with the polarities kept apart. The deterministic test holds `_audit_write_lock` itself and asserts a reader thread is still blocked, then releases and asserts it returns the row -- delete the barrier and there is nothing to block on, so it fails without depending on timing in the failure direction. The amplified probe is margin-based (a 1.0 s in-flight window against a drain-and-`SELECT` measured at 0.00 s) and is parametrized over all three readers with a **fresh store per case and no other reader called in the case**, which is the mechanical enforcement of "each read is safe on its own" -- a shared-store version would rebuild the accident above inside the test file. The pickle round-trip guards `__getstate__`: both new primitives raise `TypeError: cannot pickle '_thread.lock' object`, and `__getstate__`/`__setstate__` exist so a store can cross a process boundary. **Whether anything else catches a missing entry depends on the build, and the gate runs both kinds, so both halves belong here.** On a build with a GIL, `Session.redact()` reaches `_run_on_new_executor` -- it passes no `executor=` and no `maxtasksperchild`, so it gets a `ProcessPoolExecutor` of its own rather than `Session._executor` -- and the callable is the bound `service.execute_redaction_task`, so `RedactionService.store_backend` is pickled into every worker. Deleting the two `keys_to_remove` entries there turns nine tests red: eight redaction tests (`test_redaction_parallel.py`, `test_redact_reports_outcome.py`, `test_redaction_wildcard.py`, `test_session.py::test_execute_config_integration`) with the `TypeError` raised from `_ForkingPickler.dumps`, plus the round-trip itself. On the **free-threaded** build `_use_threads` returns `True`, `redact()` runs in threads, nothing is pickled, and those eight pass with the entries missing -- **the round-trip is then the only test that fails.** Measured on 3.12.13 (9 red) and 3.14.7t (1 red). So it is the sole pin on one of the two gate legs and one of nine on the other, and a reader who finds the redaction tests should not conclude it is redundant. (#218)
- **The float pixel path wrote no geometry at all, so an exported float instance carried either no `Rows` or the wrong one.** `_export_instance_worker` resolves one `PixelGeometry` and then used it on the **integer** branch only. The float branch (#170) sits above that branch and ends with `arr = None`, so a float array reached neither the descriptor writes nor #215's refusal to write a geometry that had to be guessed.

  **The defect has two shapes, and the one #216 filed is the milder.** Measured on `258331c`. *Absent*: a `(5,6,3) float32` array on an instance declaring no `SamplesPerPixel`, no `NumberOfFrames` and no `Rows`/`Columns` exported a file carrying `(7fe0,0008) Float Pixel Data` with **no Rows, no Columns and no Samples per Pixel** -- while the byte-for-byte identical `uint8` graph was correctly refused. That is loud: all three are **Type 1** in the Floating Point Image Pixel Module (PS3.3 C.7.6.24) and in the Double Floating Point Image Pixel Module (C.7.6.25), so nothing decodes it. *Present and stale*: a `(4,4) float32` array on an instance declaring `Rows=10 Columns=10` exported `Rows=10 Columns=10` beside **16 floats** -- a file announcing 100 pixels and carrying 16 -- and a `(2,4,8)` array declaring `Rows=99 Columns=99` exported those, beside 64. Those files are not undecodable. They are decodable files describing a different image, which is the relabelling this changelog has argued twice now is worse than a drop, because nothing disagrees loudly and so nothing invites the reader to go back. **A fix that only hoists the refusal -- which is what the issue suggests -- leaves the second shape standing**, and the second shape is the one that ships quietly.

  So the rule is stated as *both pixel branches write their descriptors from the one resolved geometry, and neither writes a geometry that had to be guessed*, and the hoist is one consequence of it rather than the fix. `Rows`, `Columns`, `SamplesPerPixel` and `NumberOfFrames` now come from `_write_pixel_geometry`, called from both branches -- the integer branch's own block, moved, so its behaviour is unchanged. The comment that used to stand where the float call now goes justified writing nothing with "the descriptors were merged from `attributes`, which is the same source file this array was read back from, so they already agree". That is true of the ingest path and false of every `set_attr` and every hand-built graph, which is exactly the reachability class the float16 arm already existed to serve.

  **`PhotometricInterpretation` is written on the float path now, and still not forced.** It is Type 1 in both float modules with Enumerated Value `MONOCHROME2`, and it was simply absent from every exported float instance that had not declared one. It goes through the same `resolve_photometric_interpretation` the integer path uses, whose `None` arm means "the declared value is coherent, leave it alone" -- so a declared `MONOCHROME1` survives the round trip rather than being overwritten with the value the module enumerates. One arm of that resolver is now taken differently on the two branches, and the costs paragraph below says which and why.

  **Four costs, stated rather than discovered.** *One.* `session.export()` and `DicomExporter.write_tree()` can now **fail** a float instance whose geometry is a guess, with the refusal naming the tag that would settle it: `RuntimeError: Refusing to write <path>: the pixel array's shape (5, 6, 3) is ambiguous -- it is equally a multi-frame grayscale image and a single-frame image with 3 samples per pixel -- and the instance declares no SamplesPerPixel (0028,0002), NumberOfFrames (0028,0008) or Rows/Columns to resolve it. Writing it would guess the image's geometry, and a recipient cannot tell a guess apart from a correct answer.` Through the session that becomes `ExportOutcome(ok=False)`, audited `ERROR`, counted in `ExportSummary.failed` and grading `REVIEW_REQUIRED`; through `write_tree` it propagates inside the existing `RuntimeError: Export incomplete. 1 failed. First error: ...` wrapper, which interpolates the message above. No `scripts/` generator produces one -- verified by grep, there is not a single float pixel array anywhere under `scripts/`, only two waveform `--fs`/`--baseline` parameters.

  *Two.* A float instance's `Rows`/`Columns`/`SamplesPerPixel` can change between `258331c` and this release, from the declared value to the resolved one, wherever the two disagreed. That change is the fix.

  *Three, and it is a case that previously produced a file.* The geometry refusal sits **above** the branch that picks a float element by `itemsize`, so it also catches the one width that has no DICOM element at all. A rank-3 `float16` array with no declared descriptors -- `(5,6,3)`, modality `SR` -- exported on `258331c` as `ok=True` with a file on disk and one `STANDARD` `DATA_LOSS` row saying the pixel data was not written; it now returns `ok=False` with no file and no loss row, because the instance is refused before the float16 arm runs. The report changes from "exported, minus its pixels" to "not exported", which is the more accurate of the two, but it is a behaviour change and not merely a stricter descriptor. Rank-2 `float16` is unaffected: it resolves `STRUCTURAL`, never `GUESSED`, and still exports with its loss row.

  *Four, and it is the one this branch got wrong first.* A rank-3 float array with a **declared** `SamplesPerPixel = 3` resolves `DECLARED`, so the shared `resolve_photometric_interpretation` reaches its `RGB` arm on a float element. `RGB` is a value neither float module permits: both enumerate `MONOCHROME2`, and PS3.3 C.7.6.3.1.2 says that value "may be used only when Samples per Pixel (0028,0002) has a Value of 1", so a conformant float instance is single-sample. Three columns, because "nonconformant whatever this code does" is true of the *instance* and not of every element on it. Declared `RGB`: unchanged, `_merge` had already written it. Declared **nothing**: `258331c` wrote no Photometric Interpretation at all -- a missing Type 1, the defect this entry is about -- and this release writes `RGB`. That column is the accepted residual and is still open as #222. Declared **`MONOCHROME2`**: the first four commits of this branch overwrote it with `RGB`, and a later commit puts it back, because that column is a regression and not a cost.

  **The reachability argument that let it through was wrong, and the correction matters more than the fix.** It was defended here and in #222 as "reachable only from a hand-built graph or a `set_attr` call, since `populate_attrs` skips group `7fe0`". Group `7fe0` is skipped, and it is irrelevant: the float array reaches the export worker from `get_pixel_data()` re-reading the source file with pydicom -- the whole #170 mechanism -- while `SamplesPerPixel` (0028,0002), `PhotometricInterpretation` (0028,0004) and `PlanarConfiguration` (0028,0006) are ordinary group-0028 elements that pass straight through ingest. Measured on a pydicom-written Parametric Map carrying `(7fe0,0008)`, `SamplesPerPixel = 3`, `PlanarConfiguration = 0`, `PhotometricInterpretation = MONOCHROME2`, 4x4, `BitsAllocated = 32`, through plain `session.ingest()` -> `session.export()` with nothing hand-built: `258331c` exported `MONOCHROME2`, this branch before the fix exported **`RGB`**. So it was never a hand-built-graph curiosity; it was a relabelling of ordinary ingested data, of exactly the kind #186 removed from the integer path.

  The fix is #222's option 1, the narrowest of the three it offers: a float-only guard around the single `resolve_photometric_interpretation` call, passing a **declared monochrome** value through instead of the resolver's `RGB`. The resolver itself is unchanged, because on the integer path the `RGB` arm is right -- three samples beside a monochrome value is nonconformant whichever half is wrong, and `RGB` is the neutral reading of three interleaved samples. On a float element there is no such neutral reading, so the correction is not narrowed, it is declined. `MONOCHROME1` and `PALETTE COLOR` are covered too, not just `MONOCHROME2`. `PlanarConfiguration` is deliberately **not** addressed: it is still written on the float path and appears in neither C.7.6.24 nor C.7.6.25, which is #222's option 2 and still open -- and it is left unpinned by any test, because a test fixing current behaviour would cement an element the IOD does not define.

  A **conformant** float instance -- single-sample, `MONOCHROME2`, `Rows`/`Columns` agreeing with the array -- exports byte-identically to `258331c`; measured.

  **The pixel-element exclusion now runs in all three reachable directions.** PS3.5 **Section 8.2**, verbatim: "It is not permitted to have more than one of Pixel Data Provider URL (0028,7FE0), Pixel Data (7FE0,0010), Float Pixel Data (7FE0,0008) or Double Float Pixel Data (7FE0,0009) in the top level Data Set." The rule was cited as PS3.5 A.1 here and in #170; A.1 is the Implicit VR Little Endian Transfer Syntax and says nothing about which pixel elements may coexist, so every citation of it for this rule is corrected. The float branch has deleted `(7fe0,0010)` since #170; the integer branch never deleted its counterparts, and a `(4,4) uint8` instance carrying a `"7fe0,0008"` of its own in `attributes` exported a file with **both** -- measured, not inferred, and the same file pydicom itself refuses to read back ("One and only one of 'Pixel Data', 'Float Pixel Data' or 'Double Float Pixel Data' may be present"). The third direction is the one both halves of that pair missed: a `float32` array beside a declared `"7fe0,0009"` left with `(7fe0,0008)` *and* `(7fe0,0009)`, and `float64` beside a declared `"7fe0,0008"` the same way, both producing exactly the file pydicom rejects. The float branch now deletes the other float element as well as the integer one. Not writing an element was never the same as removing it: `_merge` writes whatever `attributes` holds. `populate_attrs` skips the whole `7fe0` group at ingest, so all three arrive only from a hand-built graph or a `set_attr` call. Pixel Data Provider URL is in the same sentence and is *not* deleted -- removing a caller's element is a data-loss action that owes them a loss row, which is filed rather than added here.

  Nothing in `verify()` or `generate_report()` could see any of this. Nothing was dropped, so there is no `DATA_LOSS` row; the worker returned `ok=True`, so there is no `ERROR` row; the run graded `PASS`. (#216)

- **Four places decided what an image looked like by guessing which axis of the pixel array was which, and every one of them graded `PASS`.** `(frames, rows, cols)` and `(rows, cols, samples)` are both rank 3, and all four broke the tie with the same line -- `if shape[-1] in [3, 4]` -- while `SamplesPerPixel` (0028,0002) and `NumberOfFrames` (0028,0008), which settle it, sat unread in `Instance.attributes` at every one of the four call sites. Nothing in `verify()` or `generate_report` can see a geometry error: the verifier is an OCR pass comparing detected text against redaction zones, and no element was dropped, so there is no `DATA_LOSS` row and the run grades `PASS`.

  **What a user was getting.** A 3-frame 4x4 `MONOCHROME2` Secondary Capture, ingested and exported with `use_compression=False`, produced a file declaring `NumberOfFrames=3 Rows=3 Columns=4 SamplesPerPixel=4 PhotometricInterpretation=RGB`, on which `pydicom.dcmread(...).pixel_array` raises `ValueError: 'Samples per Pixel' value of '4' is invalid`. `session.export()` returned `None`, the worker returned `ok=True`, and the compliance report graded the run `PASS`. The same source through `DicomExporter.write_tree()` -- the serializer the `scripts/` fixture generators use -- produced the *worse* failure: `Rows=2 Columns=8 SamplesPerPixel=1` for a 2-frame 8x4 image, a file that is internally coherent, decodes cleanly, and simply describes a different picture. Nothing disagrees loudly, so nothing invites anyone to look. (#186 and #205 measured `SPP=4` and `SPP=1` for the same defect because `_export_dicom` calls `release_memory()` unconditionally before building the export plan, so through a session the pixels are never resident and the load path fires first.)

  **The corruption was durable, and a *read* caused it.** `get_pixel_data()` called `set_pixel_data(arr)` "to ensure attributes (rows, cols) are synced". `SidecarPixelLoader` had just reshaped that array *from* those attributes, so the sync could only ever disagree with its own input -- and `set_pixel_data` ends in `mark_modified()`, so the next `save()` wrote the guess into SQLite. Reopening the database showed `SamplesPerPixel=4, PhotometricInterpretation=RGB, PlanarConfiguration=0, Rows=3, Columns=4` for a file whose bytes on disk said none of that. The source file is never touched; the index the rest of the pipeline reads was.
