  **The colour half is far broader than the filed trigger, and needs no unusual dimensions at all.** The same line ran `if samples >= 3: set_attr("0028,0004", "RGB")`, so a plain single-frame 8x8 `YBR_FULL` instance came back from `get_pixel_data()` as `RGB` and exported that way. Every non-RGB colour space was relabelled -- `YBR_FULL`, `YBR_FULL_422`, `YBR_ICT`, `YBR_RCT` -- not only the 3-or-4-column images the issues describe.

  **Two of the four sites are in neither issue, and one of them is the worst of the four.** `RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array` used the same expression to choose which axes a redaction zone's rows and columns address, so on a 2-frame 8x4 grayscale image the zone landed on the wrong region: **the burned-in identifier stayed in the exported pixels while the pipeline reported a successful redaction**. Fixing the descriptors without fixing this would have been actively worse than the bug -- correct geometry wrapped around pixels zeroed in the wrong place. `analyze_pixels` used it to split frames for OCR, so a 3-frame 8x3 image was handed to the scanner as one RGB frame and text burned into frames 1 and 2 was never looked at. Two further readers of `shape[-1]` are deliberately untouched, because neither guesses: `_compress_j2k` squeezes singleton axes using `ds.NumberOfFrames`/`ds.SamplesPerPixel`, and `SidecarPixelLoader.__call__` reshapes purely from stored metadata -- it is the model the rest of this follows.

  **One rule, one module.** `isocenter/pixel_geometry.py` holds it: *attributes are authoritative for **layout** -- which axis means what; the array is authoritative for **magnitude** -- how large each axis is.* Handing the setter a differently-sized array stays legitimate and always was (`test_compaction.py` replaces a `(100,1000)` array); guessing which axis is which is the defect. `resolve_pixel_geometry(shape, attributes)` eliminates arms by declared `SamplesPerPixel`, then `NumberOfFrames`, then `Rows`/`Columns`, and reports **how** it decided -- `DECLARED`, `STRUCTURAL`, `MATCHED` or `GUESSED` -- rather than how confident it is, because the two sides apply different policies to a guess. The module imports only `enum` and `typing`: no numpy (the input is a shape tuple, not an array), no `entities` or `io_handlers` (both import it, so the other direction would be a cycle), and no third-party package, so `_export_instance_worker` can import it at module scope in a child interpreter and `test_packaging_contract.py` is unaffected.

  **The exceptions this newly raises.** `Instance.set_pixel_data` raises `ValueError` when the instance declares a `SamplesPerPixel` that no axis of the array can carry -- `set_pixel_data(np.zeros((5,8,4)))` on an instance declaring `SamplesPerPixel=3`, where previously it silently wrote `SamplesPerPixel=4` over the declaration. The message names the shape and all four declared descriptors. Nothing was dropped and nothing is missing, so a `DATA_LOSS` row would misname it: two statements disagree, and trusting either one is what produced this bug or its mirror image. In `_export_instance_worker` that same `ValueError` is caught by the existing handler and becomes `ExportOutcome(ok=False)` -- audited, counted in `ExportSummary.failed`, and surfaced by the compliance report through the machinery #181 added. So `session.export()` can now fail an instance that previously "succeeded" wrongly, which is the point. A *magnitude* disagreement never raises; only a layout one does. Through `session.redact()` this new `ValueError` is a second path into #213 -- that method's `except Exception` swallows the re-raise #66 added and documents as never to be swallowed -- which is filed, not fixed here.

  **A guess is accepted by the setter and refused by the export, deliberately.** An instance with no `SamplesPerPixel`, no `NumberOfFrames` and no `Rows`/`Columns` given a `(100,200,3)` array is genuinely ambiguous. `set_pixel_data` takes it and logs a `WARNING` naming the tag that would settle it, because a hand-built graph has to be able to take pixels before its attributes -- that is what `write_tree` exists to serve, and what the `scripts/` generators do. `_export_instance_worker` raises `RuntimeError` on the same geometry, because that is the boundary at which a guess becomes a file on disk that a recipient cannot tell apart from a correct one; through `export_batch` it is audited and the run continues, through `write_tree` it propagates and fails the call. All three `scripts/` generators were run with the resolver instrumented rather than read: their RGB fixtures do produce a `GUESSED` and its warning at `set_pixel_data`, and **every** resolution reaching the export worker is `DECLARED` or `STRUCTURAL`, because the setter writes back the sample count it chose. No generator output changes.

  **`PhotometricInterpretation` is corrected only where it contradicts itself.** It is not derivable from an array -- three samples are equally `RGB`, `YBR_FULL` or `YBR_RCT` -- so a monochrome interpretation beside three samples becomes `RGB`, a colour one beside a single sample becomes `MONOCHROME2`, an absent one takes the neutral default for the sample count, and everything coherent is left exactly as declared. `PALETTE COLOR` counts as monochrome, because it is `SamplesPerPixel = 1`. `PlanarConfiguration` is written only when the instance is colour *and* has not declared one; `ingest_worker` already normalises it from 1 to 0 at ingest, so forcing it had nothing left to correct on any live path.

  **`BitsAllocated` stays derived from the array, and that is a decision rather than an oversight.** It is not the same defect. The frames-versus-samples question has no attribute-free answer, whereas `array.itemsize * 8` is exact; and the export writes `arr.tobytes()`, so a declared width disagreeing with the array produces a file that cannot be decoded at all -- "the attributes win" is not available when the attribute cannot be honoured. Handing the setter an array of a different dtype is as legitimate as handing it one of a different shape, which `test_pixel_bits_allocated` does three times on one instance. It is now written only when it differs, and logs at `DEBUG` when it overrides a declared value. `BitsStored`, `HighBit` and `PixelRepresentation` are still not derived, so a corrected `BitsAllocated` can sit beside a stale `BitsStored`; that is pre-existing and deliberately untouched here.

  **`get_pixel_data()` no longer writes anything.** All three load branches assign `pixel_array` directly. That alone removes the entire durable-corruption class for the geometry, because the loader had already reshaped from the attributes and the sync could only echo them back. It had exactly one thing to add that the loader did not already know, and that is `BitsAllocated`: the sync ended in an unconditional `set_attr("0028,0100", array.itemsize * 8)`, and it was the only place a declared width was ever reconciled with the dtype the loader produces. `SidecarPixelLoader` buckets dtype as `uint16 if bits > 8 else uint8`, so every declared value outside `{8, 16}` -- a binary Segmentation's `BitsAllocated=1` being the ordinary one -- yields a `uint8` array. That reconciliation now happens at the *write* instead, three lines below `ds.PixelData = arr.tobytes()`, where the bytes whose width is in question are actually produced. Without it the export wrote `BitsAllocated=1` beside 8-bit bytes and pydicom read a 2-frame 4x8 mask back as 16 frames: decodable, internally coherent, and a different image. The descriptor writes in `set_pixel_data` are now conditional on the value actually changing, so an idempotent call does not rewrite the graph -- but `mark_modified()` is **unconditional**, because the array's *contents* are part of what the store holds and are invisible to every descriptor comparison: a redacted frame has exactly the Rows, Columns and BitsAllocated of the frame it replaced. Making the dirty flag follow the descriptors leaves an incremental `save_all` skipping the instance and the redacted pixels never reaching the sidecar, which `test_blob_storage.py`'s compaction and save-path tests catch and `test_persistence_incremental.py::test_unsaved_tracking_pixel_change` does not -- it sets pixels on a *fresh* instance, where the descriptors change either way. Object identity is not a safe proxy either, since `RedactionService._apply_roi_to_instance` mutates the instance's own array in place, so the rule is the one-liner: setting pixel data is a mutation, full stop.

  **One behavioural change here is not a bug fix, and it is the rank-1 branch.** `set_pixel_data` given a flat 1-D buffer that the declared descriptors already describe reshapes it and returns without writing any attribute -- and now marks the instance modified on the way out, where before it returned without doing so. The branch returns *above* the descriptor writes, which makes it the conditional-dirtying defect in its purest form: nothing changes because nothing is written at all, while `pixel_array` has been replaced and the store's copy is stale, so an incremental `save_all` skips the instance. The declared descriptors are the *input* to that reshape, so no comparison in the method can notice. Callers relying on a flat `set_pixel_data` leaving an instance clean -- `has_unsaved_changes` False on both sides of the call -- will now see it report unsaved changes and be written on the next save. The geometry it derives is unchanged.

  A caller of the public static `RedactionService.apply_redaction_to_array` outside this tree passes the resolved geometry too: it is a required argument, not a defaulted one. This paragraph promised the opposite until #217 deleted the fallback in the same unreleased cycle -- see that entry above for the exception a call omitting it now raises. (#186, #205)
- **Float pixel data came out under the integer tag, and the rest of group 7fe0 came out nowhere at all -- both in silence.** `populate_attrs` opened with `if elem.tag.group == 0x7fe0: continue  # Skip pixels`, one line above the VR check that feeds the `DATA_LOSS` list. The comment described the intent and the code described the group, so nothing in `7fe0` could ever reach `dropped`: Float Pixel Data (7fe0,0008), Double Float Pixel Data (7fe0,0009), and the (7fe0,0010) inside an Icon Image Sequence item were all skipped as though routed while being routed nowhere. Only the top-level (7fe0,0010) was actually routed -- `ingest_worker` finds it with `if "PixelData" in ds`, a top-level keyword lookup -- so the skip was correct for one member of a six-member group. This is the group-parity blind spot #137 closed for standard `OW` elements, at the one group #137's fix could not see.

  **What a consumer was getting.** A Parametric Map whose payload is `FloatPixelData = float32 [0.5 1.5 2.5 3.5]` exported as a file carrying `(7fe0,0010) Pixel Data` with byte-identical content, `BitsAllocated=32` and `PixelRepresentation=0` beside it, and read back `uint32 [1056964608 1069547520 1075838976 1080033280]` -- `1056964608` is `0x3F000000`, the IEEE-754 encoding of `0.5`. Zero `DATA_LOSS` rows, and the compliance report graded `PASS`. A CT carrying an Icon Image Sequence exported the icon item with all eight of its `0028,xxxx` descriptors, no Pixel Data, zero loss rows, and `PASS` -- an item declaring a 2x2 8-bit image and carrying nothing, with Pixel Data Type 1 in the Icon Image Macro (PS3.3 C.7.6.1.1.6). `reporting.py`'s section 3 promises that the elements below it "were present in the source and are **not** in the exported data"; for this whole family the section was empty. A relabelling is worse than a drop, and that is why it is fixed on the same commit as the silence: a dropped element is recoverable from the source, and a relabelled one invites nobody to go back. The output was internally coherent -- 4x4, 32 bits allocated, 64 bytes of Pixel Data -- so nothing downstream errored and there was nothing to distrust, which is exactly the state #146 exists to prevent.

  **One routing decision, and everything else derives from it.** Skipping is not the same as routing, and the group has six assigned members that answer differently. It takes two questions, not one, and they are two names in the code rather than one overloaded test:

  | Element | Verdict | Where it goes |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | (7fe0,0001) Extended Offset Table (`OV`) | not data | an index into a fragment layout the export does not produce -- not a loss |
  | (7fe0,0002) Extended Offset Table Lengths (`OV`) | not data | same |
  | (7fe0,0003) Encapsulated Pixel Data Value Total Length (`UV`) | not data | same |
  | (7fe0,0008) Float Pixel Data, top level | routed | re-read from the source at export, written back under (7fe0,0008) |
  | (7fe0,0009) Double Float Pixel Data, top level | routed | same, under (7fe0,0009) |
  | (7fe0,0010) Pixel Data, top level | routed | sidecar via `ingest_worker` → `SidecarPixelLoader` -- unchanged |
  | any of those three inside a sequence item | lost | nowhere; reported `STANDARD` |

  **The float pair is carried, and not by the sidecar.** `SidecarPixelLoader` reconstructs dtype from BitsAllocated and PixelRepresentation alone (`uint16 if bits > 8`), so it has no channel to say "these bytes are float32" and cannot hold them; giving it one is #183. But the export does not need it to. `_export_instance_worker` receives the array from `get_pixel_data()`, which re-reads the source file, and pydicom surfaces (7fe0,0008)/(7fe0,0009) through `.pixel_array` -- so at the writeback the dtype is *known* and the correct tag is a lookup rather than a guess. `itemsize` picks it, because 32- and 64-bit IEEE-754 is what the two elements are defined by; `BitsAllocated` is set to match, and (7fe0,0010) is deleted rather than merely not written, since `_merge` writes whatever `attributes` holds and PS3.5 Section 8.2 makes the two mutually exclusive. Both round-trip exactly under implicit and explicit VR.

  Writing nothing was the first cut of this fix and it was wrong: it swapped a silent corruption for a quiet nonconformance. Float Pixel Data is Type 1 in the Floating Point Image Pixel Module (PS3.3 C.7.6.24), so a Parametric Map exported with no pixel element is invalid -- the same "declares something it does not carry" shape #160 had just finished removing from the waveform path, reintroduced two commits later at a different site. The guard also sits **below** the redaction block rather than above it: zeroing a zone works on a float array, and writing the bytes before the zones were applied would export the burned-in identifiers this pipeline exists to remove.

  `float16` is the one arm that still loses the data, because no DICOM element carries a 16-bit float at any tag. It is unreachable from a source -- nothing decodes to it -- so only a caller handing `set_pixel_data` a float16 array gets there, and it takes **the same modality decision as a missing source file**: `_export_instance_worker` already raised `Pixels missing for Image Modality` rather than write a pixel-less file for an image, and the first cut set `arr = None` *after* that check had passed, producing exactly the file it exists to prevent. `_IMAGE_MODALITIES` is now one module-level constant consulted by both, so they agree by construction rather than by review.

  **Three of the six are not data, and reporting them was itself a false loss.** The Extended Offset Table is byte offsets and lengths into the encapsulated Pixel Data fragment stream, and (7fe0,0003) is that stream's total length. Ingest decodes the pixels and the export re-writes them uncompressed, so the layout those describe does not exist in the exported file: they cannot be carried, and their absence loses nothing recoverable -- the pixels they index round-trip byte for byte. Reporting them filed two `DATA_LOSS` rows and two warnings on **every** encapsulated instance stored with an EOT, which is the mechanism DICOM added for large multi-frame objects, so the noise landed exactly where it is least welcome. That is the failure the gate's own comment warns about, arrived at from the other side. They are also the group's three non-binary members, and `import_files` words a standard loss as "binary-VR elements are not held in the object graph" -- so exempting them is also what makes that clause true of everything still reported.

  **The exemptions are depth-sensitive, and one of them is conditional.** `_is_routed(tag, is_root, has_pixel_data)` is the single answer to "does something else carry these bytes"; `populate_attrs` gains an `is_root` argument that `process_sequence` passes as `False` at every depth. Top level only, for all three routed tags: `get_pixel_data()` reads the top level and `ingest_worker` looks for `PixelData` there, so the same tag one level down is carried by nothing. And the float pair is exempt only when the instance has no (7fe0,0010) of its own -- a file carrying both is nonconformant, but malformed input exists, and the export takes the sidecar array whenever there is a loader, so in that case the float half really does vanish and must still be reported. `_ROUTED_BINARY_TAGS` was decorative for (7fe0,0010) until now -- its own comment said the group check took that tag before the frozenset was ever consulted -- and it is load-bearing from here, which is why the depth split lives in a named function rather than in a second membership test.

  The group `continue` itself stays: it is not only about binary VRs, and deleting it would start landing (7fe0,0001) and (7fe0,0002), the `OV` pair, in `attributes` as a side effect of a change that is about reporting. What moved is that the skip is recorded first.

  **(5400,1010) is deliberately not given the same treatment**, against the original issue's own suggestion that the "same depth blind spot" be fixed in the same pass. Waveform Data is *never* at the top level -- it only exists inside Waveform Sequence items -- so an `is_root` rule would report the one multiplex group that *is* routed, on every waveform ever ingested. Which of several groups survived is an index question, not a depth question, and #160 already reports the discarded ones from the source's group count.

  Every new row is scoped `LOSS_SCOPE_STANDARD` by the parity rule -- group `7fe0` is even -- and deliberately not graded harder. Whether a loss of this weight should take `validation_status` to `REVIEW_REQUIRED` is open on #150, and deciding it here would decide it on the wrong ticket. The nested icon is the one member still dropped: carrying it needs the blob store to key a second pixel blob per sequence item, which is #183. (#169, #170, #193, #194)
- **`scripts/mutation_probe.py` could print a verdict about a mutation the tests were never shown.** The probe rewrites a module, runs pytest in a subprocess, and reads the exit code as a verdict. CPython validates a timestamp-based `.pyc` against the source's `(mtime, size)` pair, with the mtime truncated to whole seconds in the cache header, so when both halves of that key match the previous write the interpreter hands back the *previous mutant's* bytecode and never compiles the one being scored. `SURVIVED  line 401: NotIn -> In` then means "the suite did not notice a change that was not in the code", which looks exactly like a real coverage gap and sends a reader to write a test for a line that is already covered.

  **The size half of that key collides by construction, not by coincidence.** #174 reads the failure as two unlikely conditions landing together; only one of them is unlikely. The probe writes `ast.unparse` output, so consecutive mutants of the same file differ from each other by only the mutation delta -- and two `Eq -> NotEq` flips, two dropped `not`s, or two `True -> False`s differ by exactly zero bytes. Comparison flips dominate most modules, so equal-size neighbours are the norm. What makes it intermittent is the seconds half, and that stops being rare the moment a test set is quick: `crypto.py`'s two files run in 0.6s, so consecutive writes land in the same whole second about half the time.

  **Reproduced on the real module, with the mutated line instrumented so execution was observed rather than inferred.** The dropped `not` at line 390 of `remediation.py` (site 56, a genuine survivor) and the dropped `not` at line 338 (site 49, a genuine kill) unparse to the same byte count with nothing contrived about it -- that collision is what the operator set produces. Site 49's mutated branch was given a marker write and the pair run as the probe runs them, with no clock manipulation, only a retry until the second write landed in the same second. Compiled fresh: **killed, marker present**. Written in the same second as its neighbour: **SURVIVED, marker absent** -- the mutated line never executed. With the fix and the same second-collision: **killed, marker present**.

  **It does not bias toward survivors, which is the part of #174 worth correcting.** A stale cache repeats the *neighbour's* verdict, so it invents a coverage gap when the neighbour survived and hides one when the neighbour was killed. `-x` tilts it the second way: a killed mutant exits as soon as the first test fails, which shortens the gap to the next write. Survivor counts from this tool were never an upper bound; they were pulled toward the local base rate in whichever direction it ran.

  **The fix is one environment variable, and it works by breaking a chain rather than by stopping a read.** `run()` passes `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`. A `.pyc` is still *read* -- validation is unchanged -- but none is written, so no run plants the trap the next one falls into. It is the variable and not `-B` because `run_parallel()` spawns worker processes, and a grandchild that writes a `.pyc` plants the same trap the parent avoided; the variable is inherited unconditionally, while an interpreter flag reaches a spawned child only through `_args_from_interpreter_flags`. Verified rather than assumed: with every `__pycache__` removed, a run of `tests/test_multiprocessing.py` -- the `run_parallel()` path -- leaves **zero** `.pyc` files under `isocenter/` and `tests/`. The environment is copied rather than replaced, because a bare `env=` dict loses PATH and that failure would be scored as a kill.

  Two of the three mechanisms this might have needed do not apply, and saying which is part of the fix. The granularity that matters is **CPython truncating to whole seconds in the cache header**, not the filesystem's -- APFS records nanoseconds. A `__pycache__` directory already on disk when the probe starts is not a second hazard: its recorded mtime is in the past and every write the probe makes is later, so it can match on size and still never validate. Observed directly in the instrumented runs, where the frozen header `(1787951605, 11905)` sat next to a mutant of exactly 11905 bytes and was correctly rejected. And the parent process's own already-imported modules are irrelevant -- the probe imports only stdlib and every test runs in a fresh subprocess.

  **`assert_fresh()` is a guard, not a second fix.** Before every run, the control included, it applies CPython's own validation rule to the cache entry for the file just written and aborts the whole run if that entry would be reused. It should never fire now; if it does, the variable stopped reaching the subprocess and every verdict in that run is suspect, so it exits rather than printing one. It is deliberately outside the loop's `except Exception`, which reports a per-sample `skipped`. A **sentinel** -- injecting `__MUTANT__ = "<id>"` into the AST and asserting pytest imported it -- was considered and not built: once nothing writes a `.pyc`, the only remaining way for pytest to execute different bytes is a different *file*, which is the `.venv`-resolution confusion #174 files separately, and the sentinel costs either an extra interpreter start per mutant or a plugin injected into the run. The other suggestion, flagging a mutant whose output matches the baseline in suspiciously little time, was rejected outright: many mutants legitimately pass with identical output in identical time, so it has false positives and false negatives both, and it is a smell test standing in for a check that can be made exactly. The guard has one known hole and it is in the documented invocation: `cache_from_source` names the cache file from the *parent* interpreter's tag while `PYTEST` hardcodes `.venv/bin/python`, so `python -m scripts.mutation_probe` under any other interpreter inspects a `.pyc` the subprocess never reads and returns silently. It degrades in one direction only -- never a false abort, always a false pass -- and it does not touch the fix, since the environment variable is inherited whichever interpreter launched the probe. Written into `assert_fresh`'s own docstring rather than left to be rediscovered, and filed as [#201](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/201).

  **What it changes in the numbers, and what it does not.** Same budget (30), same `TARGETS`, same operators, so the sampled sites are identical either side and the runs compare sample for sample. Two full runs before the fix and two after are **identical**: `crypto.py` 0/6, `privacy.py` 5/33, `remediation.py` 11/35. Those counts stand, and the reason they stand is not that `TARGETS` scope is out of range of the collision -- it is not, and an earlier draft of this entry claimed it was. `privacy.py`'s quickest `TARGETS` run was 0.76s, and `remediation.py` had 17 of its 35 write-to-write gaps under a second, because `-x` exits on the first failing test. Inside one clean pre-fix run both halves of the key were separately observed matching: the mtime half twice, saved only by differing sizes, and `crypto.py`'s sites 2 and 3 at 1977 bytes apiece 0.93s apart -- 0.07s of clock phase from a phantom. Expected collisions per full run are about 0.07, not zero, and duration is not a stable property either (the same `crypto.py` arm measured 0.59s and 1.18s median on one machine), so **an idle machine is the adversarial case, not the safe one**. What rescues each count is a different argument and only one of them is a guarantee. `crypto.py` 0/6 is invariant under the clock: a collision repeats the *neighbour's* verdict rather than randomising it, and every sample in that arm was killed, so a repeat still prints killed. `remediation.py` 11/35 -- **#132's number** -- is rescued by measurement alone: its two equal-size pairs happened to land at 13.75s and 13.73s gaps while 17 other gaps in the same arm were sub-second, so it is safe by where the pairs fell, and that placement moves whenever the module or its tests change. **#140's number**, on `io_handlers.py`, is the solid one -- ~2 minutes per mutant is two orders of magnitude clear of the one-second boundary. So #174's "any survivor count from this tool is an upper bound" is still the wrong shape, but the counts are safe for three reasons rather than one. The hazard is scope-dependent and it bites hardest in the documented narrow invocation (`python -m scripts.mutation_probe 30 <mod> <tests>`), where a fast test set puts every consecutive write under a second apart. Measured there, on `remediation.py` against `tests/test_remediation_dates.py` (0.6s per run, 69 sites), with the same predicate `assert_fresh()` uses: **3, 0, 4, and 3 of 69 runs executed stale bytecode before the fix; 0, 0, 0 after.** Separately, `remediation.py` reads 11/35 today against #132's "14 of 35". That gap is *not* this defect -- the count is unchanged either side of the fix -- it is code and test changes since #132 was written. `tests/test_mutation_probe_freshness.py` pins both halves: that `run()` disables bytecode writing without clearing the environment, and that `assert_fresh()` aborts on a cache entry that would validate. (#174)
- **`store_backend.get_flattened_instances()` hung a `:memory:` store the moment anyone actually streamed from it.** The method yielded rows from inside `with self._get_connection()`, and on an in-memory store that context manager holds `_memory_lock` across its own yield. That lock is a plain, non-reentrant `threading.Lock`, so a generator parked between rows held the store's only database lock for as long as it stayed parked, and every other call on that store blocked forever -- including the audit-log writer thread, which reads through the same context manager and simply stopped draining its queue. `next(gen)` once, then `store.get_total_instances()`, and nothing returns.

  **The sting is that partial consumption is the advertised use.** The docstring offered the method for "streaming exports or analysis without loading the entire graph into RAM", and streaming *is* partial consumption. The two usages that ever worked -- the deleted `DicomSession.export_to_parquet` and the two tests standing in for it since #55 -- did `rows = list(generator)`, which runs the generator to completion, exits the `with`, releases the lock, and materialises the whole cohort first, defeating the one property the method exists for. So the suite was green because nothing in it had ever used the method as documented. `CHANGELOG.md` above points users here for the eight columns #55 removed, and `DicomSession(":memory:")` is an ordinary shape, so the path from the project's own migration note to a silent hang was three lines long.

  **Fixed by paging the walk, not by materialising it.** Rows come back `page_size` at a time (default 500, `SqliteStore.FLATTENED_PAGE_SIZE`), each page in its own `_get_connection` block, and nothing is yielded while a handle is held. Fetching everything under the lock and yielding afterwards -- the obvious one-line fix -- would have made the docstring a lie: a method whose whole claim is that it does not load the cohort into RAM cannot begin by loading the cohort into RAM. Paging keeps the claim and bounds the lock to one query.

  **`fetchmany` on a live cursor is not an option, which is why this is a keyset walk.** On a file-backed store `_get_connection` **closes** the connection when its block exits, so a cursor cannot survive to a second page at all -- the two backends could not share the code. Each page is therefore its own query, resuming from `WHERE i.id > ?` on `instances.id`. That column is an `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` and so the rowid, making the resume a seek rather than the O(n^2) rescan an `OFFSET` would cost, and the three joins are all primary-key lookups. `i.id` is selected as the first column purely as the walk cursor and stripped before yielding: the row dicts keep exactly the eighteen keys they had, with no `id` among them.

  **Two behaviours changed with it, both deliberate.** Iteration is no longer a single snapshot -- writes landing between pages are visible and rows deleted between pages are not returned -- because the only way to keep one snapshot is to hold a read open across the yield, which is the defect. And the order is now defined, by `instances.id`, rather than whatever the join happened to emit. `page_size` below 1 raises `ValueError` at the call rather than on the first `next()` (the method is a plain function wrapping the generator so that it can): `LIMIT 0` returns an empty page, and an empty page is exactly how the walk decides it has reached the end, so a zero would have reported an empty store instead of failing.

  **The file-backed path was not deadlocking but was not clean either, and that half is pinned too.** `_get_connection` opens a fresh connection per call there, so nothing blocked -- but a parked generator held that connection open with a stepping `SELECT` on it. File stores run in WAL mode, where a reader does not block writers; what it blocks is checkpointing, since SQLite cannot reset the `-wal` file past the oldest live read snapshot. A generator left parked therefore let the WAL grow without bound. `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` reports `busy=1` against the old code and `busy=0` against the new one.

  **The regression tests must not be able to hang CI**, since the failure mode under test is a hang. The mechanism is pinned deterministically with no threads at all -- park the generator, assert `_memory_lock.locked() is False` -- and the behavioural probe runs `get_total_instances` on a daemon thread with a two-second join, asserting the returned *value* rather than merely that the thread finished, since a probe that raised also finishes. Either way the test closes the generator in a `finally`, which throws `GeneratorExit` in at the yield point, unwinds the `with`, and releases the lock: a regression here fails one test in two seconds instead of wedging the suite. `signal.alarm` was rejected as main-thread-only. (#164)

  Whether the method keeps its place at all is [#142](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/142)'s question and stays open. This defect is independent of it: if the method survives it should not hang, and if it is deleted the method and its tests go together.
- **A failed export certified that re-identifiable files had been released.** `REVERSIBLE_EXPORT` is the only audit row `session.export()` writes on the DICOM path, and it was written from the export *plan*, before `_run_export_batch` ran, and never reconciled with what the batch delivered. With `lock_identities()` in force and every write failing -- zero files on disk -- the compliance report still carried `| REVERSIBLE_EXPORT | 1 |` and the row *"3 of 3 exported instances carry encrypted original identities (0400,0500). They are recoverable with the session key; treat the export as re-identifiable by any holder of it."* A site reading that starts a disclosure process for an export that never happened, or -- in the partial case -- for the wrong count of instances.

  **The docstring's reasoning was right about two filters and silent about the third.** It said the check runs against the plan "so it counts what will actually be written -- after the subset filter and the burned-in scan have removed whatever they remove". Both true; the write itself also removes instances, and nothing revisited the row once the outcomes were known. The row's own prose commits to the stronger claim -- "N of M **exported** instances", "treat **the export** as re-identifiable" -- which is a statement about files a recipient holds, not about an intention. `_report_recoverable_identities` now runs after the batch and takes the delivered set, so an export that wrote nothing writes no disclosure, and one that wrote two of three discloses "2 of 2".

  **Matched on SOP Instance UID rather than on counts, because the error directions are not symmetrical.** Subtracting a failure count from the affected count would be right only if failures fell on the affected instances, and getting it wrong in the other direction -- telling a recipient that fewer of their files are re-identifiable than actually are -- is worse than the over-claim being fixed. The plan names every output file after that UID, so the key always exists.

  **Delivered means a file is on disk, not that a worker said it wrote one.** The first version of this fix keyed the disclosure on `ExportOutcome.ok`, and `ok=False` reports that a write did not *complete* -- not that nothing reached disk. `save_as` creates the file and streams elements in ascending tag order, and the Encrypted Attributes Sequence is group `0400`: a failure past it leaves a short file that `pydicom.dcmread` accepts and that carries the encrypted originals in full. Measured on three locked instances with one failing at (6000,0010): three files in the output folder, all three re-identifiable, disclosure reading *"2 of 2 exported instances"*. The pre-#187 row said "3 of 3" and covered it, so that was a regression in the one direction this entry argues is the worse one -- an over-claim costs a site a disclosure process for an export that did not happen, an under-claim costs a recipient, who treats a re-identifiable file as safe. A planned path that exists on disk now counts as delivered whatever the worker concluded, and the union runs the safe way in both directions: an instance the workers wrote counts even if its file has since been removed. Only instances not already known to be written are stat-ed, so a clean export does no filesystem work and a failed one does one call per failure. The contrived part is the tag; the mechanism is not, since Pixel Data is written last and largest and an `ENOSPC` part-way through a cohort export produces exactly this. That the partial file is left in the released tree at all is older than this branch and is #199. (#198)

  This is deliberately not the same fix as #181's. That one adds the row that was missing for a failed instance; without this one a report would carry three `ERROR` rows *and* a disclosure asserting three exported instances, contradicting each other in the same table with nothing to say which described the delivery. And whatever shape a future `EXPORT` row takes (#166), it has to be written from `ExportOutcome`s for the same reason -- modelled on this row as it stood, it would inherit this defect. (#187)

- **An export that wrote none of its files certified the same document as a clean run.** `_export_instance_worker` catches every exception and hands the parent an `ExportOutcome(ok=False, error=exc)` -- and nothing read `error`. `export_batch` counted `ok` and returned the count; the failures went no further. So an instance that never reached disk produced no audit row of any kind, and `session.generate_report()` afterwards read `| **Validation Status** | **PASS** |`, `| Total Instances | 3 |` from the object graph, and *"No exceptions or errors were recorded."* Measured across four unrelated failure origins -- `EACCES` on the output root, `IODValidator` raising inside `_finalize_dataset` on a CT instance missing a Type 1 element, a zlib failure from `SidecarPixelLoader` on corrupted sidecar bytes, and a clean control -- every arm produced that same report. The `EACCES` arm wrote **0 of 3** files.

  **`ERROR` is not a new vocabulary term; it is the emitter that was missing.** `get_audit_errors()` has always selected `WHERE action_type IN ('ERROR', 'WARNING')`, `log_audit`'s docstring names `'ERROR'`, and `ComplianceReport.exceptions` is commented "List of 'ERROR' or 'WARNING' logs". Nothing in the package had ever written one: the only `ERROR` row the test suite has ever seen was hand-injected by `test_reporting.py`, so `get_audit_errors()` could only return rows a test put there. `DicomExporter._report_export_failures` now writes one row per instance that did not reach disk, naming the instance and the exception -- the mirror of `_report_export_losses` and in the same place, because the code that hits the exception is in a subprocess with no store handle and the outcome is what crosses back (#126). This does **not** add a general "an export happened" row: #166 is the live question of whether an `EXPORT` row should exist and what shape it takes, and a failure row decides none of it.

  With the rows written, the existing grade moves on its own -- `exceptions` is non-empty, so `validation_status` reads `REVIEW_REQUIRED` and the "No exceptions or errors were recorded" sentence is replaced by the table of failures. `REVIEW_REQUIRED` rather than a new terminal status: the DTO already forecloses the alternative, "Nothing emits `FAIL`: this report describes a run, and a run that fails raises rather than grading itself". Whether a 0-of-N delivery deserves a status distinct from one dropped private tag is a grading-scheme question and is deliberately not answered here.

  **`| Total Instances | 3 |` beside zero delivered files was its own false line, and it is answered rather than relabelled.** That row counts the object graph, which is not wrong for what it measures and is wrong for where it sits -- directly under the grade, in a document about an export. The Executive Summary gains `| Instances Written | 0 of 3 requested |`, from a count `export_batch` already computed and `_run_export_batch` already threw away. The row is **omitted** when no export has run in this session rather than rendered as a zero: "not answered here" and "nothing was written" are different statements, and the report cannot tell them apart for a session that never exported.

  The `ERROR` rows are cumulative in the store, exactly as `DATA_LOSS` rows are (#146): a report generated in a later session still sees a previous run's failures. That is a property of the audit log, not of this change, and it is stated here rather than discovered.

  **`export()` still returns `None` on this path, including when it writes nothing.** So the caller remains unable to detect the failure programmatically, while `DicomExporter.write_tree()` raises `RuntimeError` on the same graph and the same failing instance -- the two public DICOM write paths disagree about failure. Changing that is a user-facing API decision and is filed as #191, not taken here. (#181)

- **A private tag with more than one value was not written to the exported file at all.** #118 closed on "private tags reach the exported file"; they reached it at VM = 1. `_fallback_encoding` dispatched on the Python type of the value and had an arm for `bytes`, `bool`, `int`/`float` and `str` and none for a sequence of any of them, so it returned `None` -- its "nothing fits" signal -- and `_merge` turned that into `ValueError("no VR fits a MultiValue value")` and wrote no element. `US [1, 2, 3]`, `LO ['alpha', 'beta']`, `DS [1.5, 2.5]` and `IS [4, 5]` were each absent from the export, on all three export shapes measured. Multi-valued private elements are ordinary in real vendor blocks -- Siemens `US` triples, GE `IS` and `DS` lists, backslash-delimited `LO` -- so `remove_private_tags: false` did not keep the vendor block, it kept the scalar half of it. Standard multi-valued elements were never affected: `dictionary_VR` resolves `ImageOrientationPatient` and it never reaches the fallback.

  **The docstring's own reasoning is where the gap opened, and it is corrected rather than left standing.** It said these values "arrive from `value_text` already flattened to a single string", which is why multiplicity was never considered. That describes the EAV round-trip and only it. `populate_attrs` calls `set_attr(tag, elem.value)` with whatever pydicom produced, and for VM > 1 that is a `MultiValue` -- on the in-memory path, which is the one `session.export()` takes.

  **Both shapes are handled, because after #158 there are two.** In memory the value is a `MultiValue`, which is a `MutableSequence` and *not* a `list` subclass -- an `isinstance(value, list)` arm would have missed the path an export actually takes, the same trap `save_vertical_attributes` documents on the storage side. After a save, close and reopen, `load_vertical_attributes` reassembles the EAV rows as a plain list of strings, so the reloaded path arrives as a `list`. #158 made this gap *more* reachable, not less. The two converge exactly: the EAV writes `str(atom)` and the encoder stringifies each value by the same scalar rules, so an in-memory `US [1, 2, 3]` and its reloaded `['1', '2', '3']` produce the identical element. `test_a_reloaded_export_matches_an_in_memory_one` asserts that as an equality between the two exports rather than as two expected values, so there is nothing to keep in step; it fails on both arms without this fix.

  **No VR is restored and no type is inferred.** `[1, 2, 3]` is not worked back to `US`. The values are encoded elementwise by the existing scalar arms and written as a multi-valued *string* element, which is how DICOM expresses multiplicity natively -- one element, backslash-separated on the wire, and pydicom does the separating. What VR a private tag should be written under is [#154](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/154) and the repo owner's call; deciding it here would be deciding it on the wrong ticket. A multi-valued `AT` consequently stringifies to `(0010,0010)` per value exactly as the VM = 1 case has since #118 -- `Tag` is an `int` subclass whose `str` is the parenthesised form -- rather than being forked into a second answer for the multi-valued case. `test_a_multi_valued_AT_stringifies_exactly_as_a_single_one_does` pins the two against each other so they cannot drift while #154 is open.

  **The 64-character cap is checked per value, not against the join.** PS3.5 6.2 bounds each *value* of a multi-valued element, so two 50-character values under `LO` are conformant even though their encoding is 101 bytes; measured rather than assumed -- pydicom writes and reads that element back under both implicit and explicit VR with no warning. Escalating on the joined length would collapse a conformant two-valued element into one string carrying a literal backslash for no gain. When a single value *does* exceed 64 there is no text VR that holds both the length and the multiplicity -- `LO` is 1-n and capped, `UT` is unbounded and VM 1 -- so the element collapses to one `UT` string with literal backslashes, which is the trade the single-value `UT` branch already documents. Losing the arity is worse than losing nothing and better than losing the values.

  **Two edge shapes were decided here rather than discovered, and both go beyond what #165 reported.** Neither follows from "stop dropping multi-valued private tags", so a reader who disagrees should be able to see that a choice was made and what it rested on. **An empty sequence is now a zero-length `LO`** -- a legal element saying the tag was present and had no value, which is what the graph held -- where it previously reached the "nothing fits" arm and was reported as data loss. That is one fewer `DATA_LOSS` row for an input that was never really lossy, and it is a behaviour change to the loss channel, not only to the exporter. **And a partial element is refused on purpose**: one value the encoder cannot take (an `object`, or `bytes`, whose `UN` is an OB-family VR with no multiplicity to put a list into) takes the whole element to a loss, siblings included. Writing the values that *do* encode and dropping the rest was the alternative and was rejected -- there is no half-written element in DICOM, and two of three vendor values arriving silently under a plausible-looking tag is the disguised-loss shape this fix exists to avoid, not a smaller version of it. A loud loss of three values beats a silent loss of one.

  **This was data loss, not corruption, and it stops being reported because it stops happening.** The drop filed a `DATA_LOSS` entry scoped `PRIVATE` and graded the run `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, exactly as #146 and #148 intended -- loud, and recoverable in principle, unlike the silent VM = 1 stringification #154 records. The reporting was never the defect; the loss was. `test_a_multi_valued_private_tag_is_not_reported_as_data_loss` asserts the entry is gone as well, because a `PRIVATE`-scoped loss left behind for an element that is now in the file puts a reviewer in front of a loss that did not happen. (#165)

  **One shape is newly silent and it is not a case this fix handles.** A value that itself contains a backslash has no representation under a 1-n text VR: `LO` separates on `\`, so `["se\rial", "ok"]` is written as three values and re-reads as three. Before this fix that element returned `None` and filed a `PRIVATE`-scoped `DATA_LOSS` row; now it is written wrong and reported nowhere. The same is already true of the VM = 1 `str` arm on `main` and has been since #118, so the mechanism is inherited rather than introduced -- but the population is new, and "this was data loss, not corruption" holds for every shape except this one. Measured on a conformant source file, not an injection: a private tag declared `LT` (VM 1, where a backslash is ordinary text) carrying `se\rial` ingests intact and is exported as `LO ['se', 'rial']` with no `DATA_LOSS` row and no API call. Filed as #195 together with the single-valued half, because fixing only the arm added here would make the two arities disagree. (#165, #195)

- **`_export_instance_worker` merged the instance and patient attributes twice, on every instance of every export.** A copy-paste in `0efc25d` duplicated the `# 0. Base Attributes` and `# 1. Patient Level` blocks, comments and numbering included, and #135 later updated both copies -- which is how they stayed identical and invisible. The exported file is unchanged: `_merge` writes through `add_new`, so the second pass overwrote each element with the same value; `_merge` dedupes its loss entries, so no `DATA_LOSS` row was doubled; and `_merge_sequences` builds a fresh `Sequence()` per call rather than appending to the one already on the dataset. Confirmed by execution rather than by reading: a fixture carrying a private block, an over-long private value, a numeric private value, a multi-valued standard element and a one-item sequence exports to SHA256 `75b3833a...` before the deletion and `75b3833a...` after it, reproducibly across runs.

  **Byte-identical is the reason it survived, and the reason it is worth deleting anyway.** Six of `io_handlers.py`'s mutation sites were permanently unkillable -- deleting any one of the duplicated calls changes nothing observable, so `scripts/mutation_probe.py` reports SURVIVED for them forever. Those are not coverage gaps and no test should have been written for them; the fix is deletion (#140, and the same distinction as #132). It is also three merges per instance, one of them a full sequence rebuild that walks every item, on a library whose throughput claims are made about 100GB+ datasets. And it *reads* as intentional: the duplicated `# 0.` and `# 1.` comments sitting above a surviving `# 2.` / `# 3.` made the block look like a deliberate sequence rather than a paste artifact, so a future reader was more likely to preserve it than to question it.

  **`tests/test_export_merge_shape.py` pins the shape by counting the merging, not by comparing the output.** There is no observable difference in the file, so an output comparison would pass either way -- which is exactly why nothing caught this. The test records the identity of each mapping handed to `_merge`/`_merge_sequences` and asserts none arrives twice, with a companion asserting all four levels still arrive at all, so "merge nothing" does not satisfy it. It counts by identity rather than by a total, so adding a genuinely new level is a one-line change here rather than a magic number to bump. It calls `_export_instance_worker` **directly, in-process**: the worker normally runs in a `ProcessPoolExecutor` child, where a monkeypatched `_merge` in the parent is invisible and the assertion would pass vacuously against the unfixed code. Its fixture is `SR` rather than `OT` for the same care -- `OT` is in the worker's `IMAGE_MODALITIES` set, so a pixel-less instance raises there instead of reaching the merges.

  **`_merge`'s loss dedupe stays, with a narrower reason than it had.** Until now it was load-bearing bluntly: every loss arrived in duplicate because the instance mapping was merged twice, and the dedupe was the only thing keeping section 3 of the compliance report from double-counting. That reason is gone. What remains is that the four surviving merges overlap by tag -- `(0010,0010)`, `(0008,0020)`, `(0020,000d)`, `(0020,000e)` and `(0008,0060)` are all in `inst.attributes` *and* in the patient/study/series mapping stamped over it -- so one malformed value present at two levels still fails identically twice and would still be counted twice. Keys within a single mapping are unique, so a duplicate can only ever come from a second call. Reachable in principle rather than routine, which is a thinner reason than the one it replaces and therefore written down in the code and pinned by `test_one_tag_lost_at_two_levels_is_reported_once` rather than left to be inferred. (#179)
- **A session saved and reopened later exported Study Date as `2024-01-15`, which is not a DA value.** `ingest()` parses (0008,0020) into a `datetime.date` -- deliberately, since #60 -- and the store serialises that with `isoformat()`, also deliberately, since Python 3.12 deprecated sqlite3's default date adapter and `_as_stored_date` exists to stop it guessing. Hydration had no inverse: `load_all` and `load_patient` both handed `Study(...)` the ISO *string* straight back into the field a `date` came out of, and `grep -rn fromisoformat isocenter/` was empty. `_study_attributes` stamps `study.study_date` into (0008,0020) unconverted, and pydicom renders a `date` as `YYYYMMDD` but writes a `str` verbatim -- so one line of code emitted a legal element on a fresh session and an illegal one on a reloaded one. DA is fixed-length eight digits (PS3.5 Table 6.2-1). The export succeeded, nothing was audited, and `generate_report` said PASS; the only complaint anywhere was a pydicom `UserWarning` inside an export worker.

  **No PHI escaped on the DICOM path, and the reason that is worth stating is that the value was never wrong.** The reloaded study holds the same date -- shifted, if `anonymize()` ran -- rendered in the wrong format. This is a conformance defect, not a disclosure one. It needs no `anonymize()`, no config, and no non-default setting to reproduce: plain `ingest → export → save`, reopen, `export` again.

  **The WFDB path was the disclosure half, and only outside the shipped config.** `WfdbExporter._start_datetime` reads the study date through `format_study_date` and then `datetime.strptime(normalized, "%Y%m%d")` inside a `try`. An ISO string does not match, the `ValueError` is swallowed, `shifted_date` becomes `None`, and the function falls through to `_instance_only_datetime` -- the instance's own, never-shifted date tags. That fallback is precisely what `_start_datetime`'s docstring exists to prevent, and its guard did not fire: the guard was built against a *missing* study, and a reloaded session supplies a perfectly present one whose `study_date` is merely the wrong type. Measured on an ECG fixture whose real Study Date is `20260101`, anonymized, saved, reopened: the reloaded header's record line read `10:15:30 01/01/2026` where the fresh one read `10:15:30 19/04/2025`. **With the scaffold config `create_config()` writes, this did not happen** -- the instance's own date tags are shifted and (0008,002A) is removed, so both exports agreed on the value and only the header comment's "de-identified" qualifier was lost. The regression needed a session that performs a study-level SHIFT_DATE while its instance-level date tags were never remediated, which is what a session with no PHI tags loaded does, warning `No PHI tags defined` as it goes.

  **Fixed at hydration rather than at either export boundary, so `study_date` is one type everywhere.** `_as_loaded_date` sits next to `_as_stored_date` as its stated inverse and is applied at the two `Study(...)` constructions. Normalising in `_study_attributes` instead would have fixed the DICOM element and left the WFDB path taking the instance fallback regardless, because that path does not consult the normaliser's output -- it consults whether `strptime` succeeded; and it would have left `Study.study_date` genuinely polymorphic for the next consumer to trip over. An unparseable stored value is returned as it was stored and NULL stays `None`, which is #60's rule -- a date we cannot read is a date we do not have, not one we invent -- so `test_missing_study_date.py`'s round trip still holds.

  **It changes one exported directory name, knowingly.** `export_folder_names` builds the study folder from `str(study.study_date or "NoDate")` -- not `format_study_date`, which is the normaliser the two export paths use for the *element*. A study whose date was set by hand as a DA-spelled string (`DicomBuilder.add_study(uid, "20240101")`, the fixture generators in `scripts/`) therefore files under `Study_20240101_...` before a round trip and `Study_2024-01-01_...` after one, where before this fix both said `20240101`. **Ingested studies are unaffected on both revisions** -- ingest already produces a `date`, so `str()` yielded the ISO spelling on either side of the round trip already; the divergence needs a hand-built graph. Routing the folder name through `format_study_date` would close it and is deliberately not done: that renames the study directory of every study ever ingested, in every existing export tree, which is a far larger break than the one it repairs. Accepted and filed as [#189](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/189). `tests/test_api_coherence.py` structurally cannot catch this -- both write paths call `export_folder_names`, so they diverge together rather than from each other.

  **The provenance half is not fixed here and is still open.** `Study.date_shifted` has no column either, so a reloaded session reports `date_shifted=False` whether or not `anonymize()` ran, and the WFDB header comment reads `start date:` where the fresh export writes `de-identified start date:`. That is a schema change with a design question attached -- persisting it adds a second answer to "was this remediated" alongside `phi_status` -- rather than a two-call-site inverse, so it is filed as [#182](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/182) rather than folded in here. (#171)

- **A waveform annotation on a discarded multiplex group came out of `annotations.json` wearing another signal's lead name, at another signal's sample position.** `isocenter/murmur.py` resolved every annotation against multiplex group 1 without ever reading which group the annotation named. Referenced Waveform Channels (0040,A0B0) is a list of `(multiplex group, channel)` pairs -- `_lead_for`'s own docstring said so -- and the code read `values[1]` for the length check, took the channel, and dropped `values[0]` on the floor. `waveform.channels` is the ingested group's channel list, so an annotation on group 2 channel 2 was labelled with **group 1's** channel 2. The Referenced Time Offsets fallback had the same root: `fs = waveform.sampling_frequency` is the ingested group's rate, and groups in a multi-group record differ in sampling rate -- that is what makes them separate groups. A 1.0 s offset on a 1000 Hz median-beat group was converted at the 500 Hz rhythm strip's rate and landed at sample 500.

  What a consumer of `annotations.json` received was a well-formed, schema-valid document with a plausible lead name at a plausible sample position, both belonging to a signal that is not in the record. Nothing warned, and no `DATA_LOSS` entry was written, because from the bridge's point of view nothing had failed. A mislabelled clinical mark is worse than a missing one for the same reason a wrong grade is worse than a missing row: it is not visibly absent.

  **An annotation naming a group that was not ingested is now dropped, and said out loud.** Ingest keeps Waveform Sequence item 0 and discards the rest (#36), so item 0's sample axis is the only one the exported record has. `build_annotations` drops the annotation and appends its distinct multiplex group ordinals to a caller-supplied `dropped_groups` list -- all of them, because (0040,A0B0) is VM 2-2n and one annotation may name several discarded groups, so reporting only the first under-reports the loss while the message's plural promises otherwise; `WfdbExporter._write_instance` logs a warning and files a `DATA_LOSS` audit entry -- warn-plus-audit, the shape #36 established for the group discard this follows from. **One entry per instance, naming the annotation count and the distinct groups**, not one per mark: #36's emitter reports its discard once with a count, and a cart that marks forty beats on a discarded group would otherwise put forty near-identical rows into section 3 of the compliance report -- a section nobody can read reports nothing, which is the bare `DATA_LOSS: 3` defect #146 opened against, relocated. The out-parameter carries ordinals rather than prose for the same reason `populate_attrs` hands back `(tag, vr)` and lets its caller word the message (#125): only the caller can see the whole instance, and only the caller has the logger and the store handle. It nests one list per dropped annotation, because the count of lost marks and the set of groups they pointed at are different axes of the same data. A two-group annotated record therefore files two `DATA_LOSS` rows -- one at ingest for the groups, one at export for the marks that referenced them -- which is two different losses, not a double count. Clearing only the lead would not have been enough: Referenced Sample Positions index the named group's samples, so a lead-less finding would still place a clinical mark at the wrong sample in the exported signal. The drop is required for sample-position annotations, not just the time-offset fallback.

  **The behaviour is the one that is correct whichever way #150 goes.** #150 asks whether the multiplex discard should still grade `PASS`, and #160 asks what to do with the discarded groups' sequence items; neither is decided, and nothing here presumes an answer. While only item 0 survives, an annotation on any other group is unplaceable and dropping it is the honest result. If multi-rate support later lands and groups 1..n stop being discarded, the check becomes "resolve against the right group" -- the group index is read and converted to an item index in one place (`_item_index`) already -- and the drop path stops firing on its own. The audit entry is scoped `STANDARD`, because Waveform Annotation Sequence `(0040,B020)` is an even group and the scope states what the element *was*, not how bad the loss felt (#146). Grading this one harder than the discard it descends from would decide #150 here, on the wrong ticket.

  **Two smaller readings of (0040,A0B0) were wrong alongside the group and are fixed with it.** The attribute is VM 2-2n, so one annotation may name several groups; only the first pair was ever read, which would have dropped a finding that genuinely applies to the exported signal as well. And DICOM numbers multiplex groups from **1** -- PS3.3 C.10.10.1.1 "Referenced Channels" defines the first value of each pair as "the ordinal of the Item of Waveform Sequence (5400,0100)", and its worked example writes an annotation covering the entire *first* multiplex group plus channels 2 and 3 of the *third* as `0001 0000 0003 0002 0003 0003` -- while #159's prose, and Isocenter's own fixture generator, count from 0. Those two conventions differ by exactly one at exactly the place the defect lives: reading "group 1" as ordinal 1 and writing the check 0-based would drop every annotation in every conformant file. The same section is the normative source for two behaviours that had been implemented correctly and asserted without a citation: a channel number of 0 means every channel in that multiplex group (so the mark is emitted with no `lead` rather than dropped or assigned one), and an odd trailing value cannot be paired. `_item_index` converts ordinal to item index once, and reads a group ordinal of 0 as the first group rather than rejecting it -- 0 is not a valid 1-based ordinal, so it cannot be confused with a group that survived, and rejecting it would discard every annotation a 0-counting source carries. `scripts/generate_waveform_test_data.py`'s `add_annotation` now writes a conformant `group=1` by default, gains a `group` argument, and gains a `time_offsets` argument, which is the only way to reach the seconds-to-samples fallback from a fixture.

  `docs/waveforms.md` records both halves beside the existing #36 note. The `.hea` cannot drift from `annotations.json` as a result: the header's record and signal lines are built from Waveform Sequence item 0 and the sample array, and this guard reads only (0040,B020). A dropped finding removes a mark; it moves no channel count, gain, rate, or sample count. Asserted directly -- the same two-group record exported with its annotation on group 1 and on group 2 produces byte-identical `.hea` files. (#159)

- **Private tags survive a session reload. They did not, and nothing said so.** `_split_core_and_private` routes odd-group values to the `instance_attributes` EAV table, and `SqliteStore.load_all` -- which `DicomSession.__init__` calls unconditionally on every open of an existing database -- never SELECTed from it. `load_vertical_attributes` existed, worked, and was unit-tested by `tests/test_vertical_table.py`; it had no production caller. `git log --all -S load_vertical_attributes` returns the commit that created the tier (075b7b9, hybrid metadata storage, then still `gantry/persistence.py`) and nothing else but the rename, and that commit adds the method with `tests/test_vertical_table.py` as its only caller. The read path has been unreferenced since the day it was written; there has never been a release in which the tier round-tripped.

  **What that lost.** `remove_private_tags: false` is a site saying "keep the vendor block", and it was honoured for exactly as long as the session stayed in memory. Save, close, reopen: the private tags were gone from the graph and gone from any export taken afterwards. No warning and no `DATA_LOSS` entry, reasonably -- nothing was lost at ingest and the write half succeeded. The values were sitting in the database, correct, and nothing went and got them, so a site inspecting the store saw its private tags and the export that ran after a reload had none of them.

  **The surviving subset was decided by the source file's transfer syntax, not by anything a site chose.** `_split_core_and_private` keeps `bytes` values inline in `attributes_json` because the vertical table's column is TEXT, and `attributes_json` *is* read back. Under implicit VR the private elements pydicom resolves to `UN` arrive as `bytes` and take that path, so the tags that came back from a reload were precisely the ones that took the unintended tier at ingest (#151). That is what made the loss look partial rather than total.

  One exception to "private tags survive a reload", stated because the headline does not cover it: a private tag whose value is an **empty list** produces no rows -- there is no atom to write -- so it is absent from the graph after a reload rather than present and empty. That is not a regression (it did not survive before either, along with everything else on the tier) and it is the same missing-arity gap as the one-element list further down, but a reader checking whether their vendor block came back should not have to discover it.

  **The read is one query, not one per instance.** The whole reason for the standard/private split is that 10k instances load without 10k joins, and `load_vertical_attributes` takes a single SOP Instance UID -- calling it in a loop would have put an N+1 on the default session-open path. `load_vertical_attributes_bulk()` is the new entry point: `load_all` calls it with no filter for one `SELECT ... ORDER BY instance_uid, group_id, element_id, atom_index` over the table, the same move as the `wave_refs` pre-fetch a few lines above it; `load_patient` passes its instance UIDs and gets them chunked 500 at a time to stay under SQLite's bound-parameter limit. It is fixed alongside `load_all` because it hydrates the same rows and had the same hole: it is `scan_worker`'s DB-rehydration branch, the one that rebuilds a patient from a `(db_path, patient_id, ...)` tuple rather than being handed a lightweight copy. `session.py:1155` builds only the object form today, so nothing inside the library reaches it -- but it is public API, and a second hydration path that disagrees with the first about what an instance contains is the shape of #142. `load_vertical_attributes` now delegates to it, so the reassembly logic has one home. Both callers pass their open connection: `_memory_lock` is a plain non-reentrant lock, so a nested `_get_connection` inside a `:memory:` load would deadlock outright.

  **A read failure is no longer silent.** `load_vertical_attributes` returned `{}` on a `sqlite3.Error`, logging and carrying on; the bulk loader it now delegates to lets the error propagate. An empty result from this tier is indistinguishable from an instance that genuinely has no private tags, so swallowing a read failure reproduces #158 exactly -- tags absent from the graph, absent from the export, and nothing saying so. `load_all` and `load_patient` have their own handlers and turn a store-level failure into a logged empty load, which is loud. The write path's `raise` is now load-bearing for the same reason and says so in a comment: its `DELETE` is what strips a private tag the graph no longer has, and swallowing that would let the caller's transaction commit the instance row and mark it persisted -- a save that failed to de-identify, reporting success.

  **The write half had to change too, and it is the half that mattered for de-identification.** `save_vertical_attributes` deleted only the keys it was about to re-insert, and `_build_instance_writes` skipped the call entirely when an instance had no private tags left. `REMEDIATION_REMOVE` deletes the key from `inst.attributes`, so after `remove_private_tags: true` an instance's private set is empty and neither the per-key delete nor the skipped call touched the stripped rows. Inert while nothing read them; the moment hydration does, a reload puts a vendor block back onto a graph that was de-identified on purpose -- a worse bug than the one being fixed, and shipped by the read alone. The write now replaces an instance's whole vertical set: delete by `instance_uid`, then insert, with an empty mapping meaning "clear it" rather than "do nothing".

  **VM > 1 was being flattened into a string that looked like a list.** `atom_index` exists so a multi-valued element becomes one row per atom, but the gate was `isinstance(val, list)` and pydicom hands back a `MultiValue`, which is a `MutableSequence` and not a `list`. Every real multi-valued private tag went down the scalar arm and was stored as `"['alpha', 'beta', 'gamma']"` in a single row -- which would have reloaded as a string that survives a glance. The gate now reads `(list, MultiValue)`, matching what `IsocenterJSONEncoder` already does for the other tier.

  **Hydration does not look like an edit, and the reason is `phi_status`.** The loaded values are assigned into `attributes` directly, exactly as `_deserialize_into` does, and never through `set_attr`. A reloaded instance reports `has_unsaved_changes == False` and keeps its stored `phi_status` rather than reading `UNSCANNED`. The second half is the one that needs the rule: `set_attr` advances `_revision`, and a status recorded against a revision the entity has since left reads as `UNSCANNED` structurally -- so an instance rebuilt from a row that recorded a conclusion would report that nothing is known about it. `has_unsaved_changes` is not at risk either way, because `mark_subtree_persisted()` runs last in both callers and absorbs any bump.

  That absorption is why the rule needed a test of its own rather than a comment. Both callers also apply these values ahead of their `record_phi_status` loop, so replacing the assignment with `set_attr` changes nothing observable through a reload: it leaves every round-trip test in the new file green, and 23 more across `test_persistence`, `test_vertical_table`, `test_save_all_contract`, `test_analysis_persistence` and `test_phi_retention`. The ordering is kept -- it is what makes the mistake survivable -- but it is defence, not the invariant, and an earlier draft of this entry and of the comments in `persistence.py` had it the other way round. `test_applying_a_loaded_private_tag_is_not_an_edit` asserts on the helper, where the rule is written, and is the only test in the suite that fails when that line changes.

  **Types are not restored, and this does not pretend to.** `save_vertical_attributes` writes `str(val)`, so a private `5` reloads as `"5"`. The table records no VR to reconstruct one from -- `value_rep` is hardcoded to `"UN"` on write and is not read here -- and inferring a type from the text would be a storage-shape decision. That is #154's, and it is left untouched, `value_rep` included. For the same reason a saved one-element list reloads as a scalar: no arity is recorded either. Both are pinned by tests so a later fix has to change them deliberately. Nothing here touches #151 -- `bytes` still never enters the EAV, so there is no path that loads such a value twice -- and a multi-valued private tag still does not reach an exported file, because `_fallback_encoding` has no arm for a sequence; the reload makes that path *more* reachable and it is filed as #165 rather than fixed here.

  **Upgrading does not clean a store that was already written, and it can go the wrong way.** The write half being wrong means a database saved by 0.8.x or earlier may hold private tag rows the graph does not -- a session that ran `remove_private_tags: true`, anonymized, and saved left the whole stripped block in `instance_attributes` untouched. Those rows were inert while nothing read them. They are not now: the first open of such a store after upgrading puts them back on the graph, and an export taken from that session carries them. It is not fixed here, because the discriminator is not where a migration could just read it. A stale row and a legitimate one are byte-identical, and the store carries no schema version -- no `PRAGMA user_version`, no version table -- so *which release wrote this database* is genuinely unknowable. *Was this tag stripped?* is a different question and the store does have material on it: `remediation.py` writes a `REMEDIATION_REMOVE` audit row per removed tag. That material is prose in a `details` string rather than a structured column, and the audit writer is a background thread, so it is best-effort evidence and not a ledger -- but it is evidence, and #172 carries it as a third option rather than this entry claiming there is nothing to work with. What a migration cannot do is guess: dropping the tier discards the vendor block of every site that set `remove_private_tags: false`, undoing this fix, and keeping it resurrects every stripped one. Filed as #172 with the options; the behaviour is pinned by a test so the choice is visible rather than latent. A site that de-identified a store before upgrading should re-run the privacy pipeline over it, or clear the table.

  `tests/test_private_tag_reload.py` is the coverage the round trip never had. `tests/test_private_tag_export.py` uses one in-memory session and never reloads; `test_save_all_contract.py::test_private_tags_are_split_out_into_the_vertical_table` asserts the write half only, correctly for what it is named after. Every test in the new file crosses `load_all`, and one of them counts the SQL statements that touch `instance_attributes` during a 40-instance load and requires exactly one. (#158)

- **A multi-group waveform exported a file that declared a multiplex group it did not carry.** Ingest keeps group 0's samples and discards the rest (#36), but the samples and the metadata arrive by different paths: `ingest_worker` pulls `WaveformData` out of `ds.WaveformSequence[0]` alone, while `populate_attrs` walks the *whole* sequence and builds a `DicomItem` for every group. The graph therefore held one item per group and the sidecar held one group's bytes, and `_export_instance_worker` wrote every item back:

  ```
  # before, on a rhythm + median-beat ECG:
  item 0: WaveformData present=True,  fs=500.0,  nsamp=5000, nch=8
  item 1: WaveformData present=False, fs=1000.0, nsamp=1200, nch=8
  # after:
  item 0: WaveformData present=True,  fs=500.0,  nsamp=5000, nch=8
  ```

  Waveform Data `(5400,1010)` is **Type 1** under PS3.3 C.10.9 -- required, and with no permitted absent-or-empty state. The exported file stated it had 8 channels at 1000 Hz over 1200 samples and carried none of them: a conformant reader is entitled to reject it, and one that trusts Type 1 without checking reads a sample count with nothing behind it. `ingest_worker` now drops the sequence items whose samples it discarded, so what is exported is a conformant single-group record.

  **This is not the #150 question and does not answer it.** #150 asks whether discarding a group should flip the compliance grade; that is open, and the `DATA_LOSS` entry is untouched here -- same text, same `STANDARD` `loss_scope`, still reported and still ungraded. What is fixed is a different defect that #150's investigation surfaced. It also changes the *shape* of the loss rather than its size: a record that drops a group entirely is smaller and honest, indistinguishable from a single-group record, which is exactly the state #36's warn-plus-audit was designed to make visible. A record that advertises data it does not have is not. The audit entry said a group was discarded while the file said the group was present, and the file is what a downstream reader parses.

  **Dropped at ingest, not at export, because the graph is what every consumer reads.** The DICOM writer, the WFDB record, the annotation bridge and the PHI report all resolve against `Instance.sequences`; patching the writer would have made one of the four honest and left the other three describing a group whose samples this pipeline does not hold. The objection to the ingest side is that the graph stops recording that another group existed in the source -- but the `DATA_LOSS` entry carries the original group count, which is the compliance record, and a graph that describes samples it cannot produce is not evidence, it is a second answer to "what does this record contain" that disagrees with the first. Nothing else in the pipeline moves: `SidecarWaveformLoader`, `DicomExporter` and `exporters/wfdb.py` all index `items[0]`, so on a two-group ECG the WFDB path emits the same single 8-channel 500 Hz record before and after.

  **The boundary, stated rather than papered over: a store written before this fix still exports the hollow item.** `ingest_worker` is the only place the drop happens, and it does not run again on a session opened from an existing index. A database populated between 0.8.2 and 0.9.0 holds two sequence items and one waveform blob; `persistence.py` hydrates that graph faithfully and the export writes it out exactly as described above. Verified, not assumed -- persisting a two-item graph, reopening the session from that database and exporting still yields `item 1: WaveformData present=False`. Nothing self-heals it: `session.compact()` rewires sidecar offsets and does not re-read source files, so re-ingesting the originals into a fresh index is the remedy today. `DicomExporter.write_tree()` has the same boundary for the same reason -- it is the serializer alone, so a hand-built graph carrying orphaned items is written as it stands; verified, not assumed. An export-side guard would cover it, and it is deliberately not added here -- it would put a second, quieter answer to "which groups does this record have" in the writer, which is the shape of defect this entry exists to remove, and dropping items at export without an entry of its own re-creates the silent truncation #36 closed. Filed as [#168](https://github.com/kvnlng/Isocenter/issues/168).

  The fix is also stable under every #150 outcome, which is why it could land ahead of that decision. If multi-rate support ever arrives (#150's option 3), the block stops firing on its own: the items are dropped *because* the samples are, and then they would not be. (#160)

- **The Data Loss `Scope` column could render wrong and the whole suite stayed green.** Hand mutation-testing the #146 branch left 4 of 21 mutations alive on `main`, all of them in the column a reader uses to trace `REVIEW_REQUIRED` back to the row that caused it.
